🏠 Master Summary & Implementation RoadmapSTART HERE

🎯 What This Document Is — And How To Use It

This is your complete DCA exam preparation system. It's not just a study guide — it's a training simulator built around a real Django application (anihpj/jobpost) running on Docker. Every section is designed to build muscle memory through progressive difficulty: theory → troubleshooting → hands-on practice.

📝 200 Multiple Choice Questions

Purpose: Build foundational knowledge across all 6 DCA domains. Every question has a detailed explanation with real-world context from anihpj/jobpost.

Domains covered: Orchestration (Q1-Q50), Image Creation (Q51-Q90), Installation & Config (Q91-Q120), Networking (Q121-Q150), Security (Q151-Q180), Storage & Volumes (Q181-Q200).

How to use: Answer 20-30 questions per study session. Read the explanation even for correct answers — they contain deeper insights. Target: 85%+ correct before exam day.

🧠 Study Approach — Progressive Learning

Phase 1 (Foundation): Work through Q1-Q90 (Orchestration + Image Creation). Focus on understanding Docker Swarm, services, stacks, Dockerfiles, and multi-stage builds. These two domains = 45% of your exam score.

Phase 2 (Deep Dive): Q91-Q200 covering Installation, Networking, Security, and Storage. Each question includes ASCII diagrams, exam tips, and real-world anihpj/jobpost deployment context.

Phase 3 (Review): Revisit flagged questions. Focus on weak domains. Each question has toggleable answers — test yourself before revealing the explanation.

✅ Daily Implementation Checklist
  • Morning (30 min): Read 10-15 MCQs + review explanations. Focus on one domain per day.
  • Afternoon (45 min): Continue with 15-20 more MCQs. Draw the diagrams from memory to reinforce concepts.
  • Evening (60 min): Review flagged/incorrect questions from earlier in the day. Read all exam tips carefully.
  • Weekend (2-3 hours): Full review of the week's topics. Take a 55-question timed mock exam (90 minutes).
  • Day before exam: Review all DCA Exam Tips throughout the document. Focus on domain weight percentages.
  • Exam day morning: Light review of your weakest domain. Set up your environment: docker version, docker info, verify Docker is running.

🏆 Skills You'll Master — Before vs After This Guide

❌ BEFORE — Common Beginner Pitfalls

  • ❌ "Why won't my container start?" — No idea where to look
  • ❌ "The ports are mapped but I get connection refused" — Don't know about network drivers
  • ❌ "My image is 2GB, is that normal?" — Never heard of multi-stage builds
  • ❌ "docker-compose up works but Swarm deploy doesn't" — Don't know the differences
  • ❌ "I committed secrets into my image" — No .dockerignore or multi-stage awareness
  • ❌ "docker system prune -a" deleted my volumes!" — No backup strategy
  • ❌ "Docker Content Trust? What's that?" — No image verification

✅ AFTER — Skills This Guide Builds

  • ✅ Diagnose any container failure in <2 min using docker logs/inspect/events
  • ✅ Build production images with multi-stage builds under 100MB
  • ✅ Deploy and manage Swarm services, stacks, secrets, and configs
  • ✅ Configure Docker networks (bridge, overlay, macvlan) with confidence
  • ✅ Secure containers with non-root users, seccomp, and DCT
  • ✅ Manage volumes, backups, and storage drivers without data loss
  • ✅ Integrate Docker with AWS (ECR, ECS) and CI/CD pipelines

🚨 Critical Do's & Don'ts During The Exam
  • ✅ DO: Use docker --help and docker <command> --help — the exam provides full CLI help.
  • ✅ DO: Read every question TWICE. Look for "NOT", "EXCEPT", "BEST", "MOST" keywords.
  • ✅ DO: Eliminate obviously wrong answers first. Most questions have 2 clearly wrong options.
  • ✅ DO: Flag questions you're unsure about. Return with fresh eyes at the end.
  • ❌ DON'T: Spend more than 90 seconds on any single question. Flag it, move on, return later.
  • ❌ DON'T: Second-guess yourself excessively. Your first instinct is usually correct.
  • ❌ DON'T: Leave any question blank. There's no penalty for wrong answers — guess if you must.
  • ❌ DON'T: Forget that Docker Swarm uses docker service and docker stack, NOT docker run for production.

📊 Exam Overview & StrategySTRATEGY

DCA Exam Format & Preparation Strategy

Exam Duration: 90 minutes | Format: 55 multiple-choice questions (MCQ) | Passing Score: 65% (approximately 36/55) | Cost: $195 | Certification Validity: 2 years | Remote Proctored: Yes (via Pearson VUE)

6 Official Domains (Mirantis): Orchestration (25%), Image Creation & Registry (20%), Installation & Configuration (15%), Networking (15%), Security (15%), Storage & Volumes (10%). Orchestration is the heaviest — focus your study time accordingly.

Exam Strategy: Read all questions first (5 min) → Answer easy questions first → Flag and return to hard ones → Use process of elimination (4 options, usually 2 clearly wrong) → The exam provides access to Docker CLI --help — use it! No hands-on CLI tasks — pure MCQ format.

Your local Docker lab (below) is for hands-on practice. Docker Desktop or Docker Engine — any will work. Practice the CLI commands referenced in each question to build muscle memory.

🖥️ Your Docker Lab EnvironmentSETUP

🟢 Local Docker Engine — Your Practice Sandbox

All scenarios run on your local Docker Engine. No Kubernetes clusters needed — just Docker Desktop (Windows/Mac) or Docker Engine (Linux).

📚 Pre-Learning: Docker FoundationsF.1–F.5

🟢 Novice-Friendly: Building Your Docker Mental Model

F.1 What Problem Does Docker Solve? — From "It works on my machine" to consistent environments. The evolution from bare metal → VMs → containers. How Docker provides process isolation without the overhead of full virtualization.

F.2 Containers 101 — What IS a Container? — Process isolation, NOT a mini-VM. Linux namespaces (PID, NET, MNT, UTS, IPC, USER), cgroups for resource limiting, union filesystems (overlay2). Docker architecture: Client → daemon → containerd → runc.

F.3 Dockerfile Anatomy & Image Lifecycle — FROM, WORKDIR, COPY vs ADD, RUN, CMD vs ENTRYPOINT, ENV, EXPOSE, USER, HEALTHCHECK. Multi-stage builds. BuildKit. docker build → tag → push → pull → run.

F.4 The anihpj/jobpost Application Stack — Web tier (Django templates + nginx), API tier (Django REST + gunicorn), Database tier (PostgreSQL 15), Redis (Celery + caching). Full Dockerized file structure with Dockerfile, docker-compose.yml, and docker-stack.yml.

F.5 Docker in Your Daily Workflow — docker run (the 80% command), docker compose up/down/ps/logs, docker exec -it for debugging, docker logs -f for tailing, docker system prune for cleanup. Docker Desktop vs CLI.

If you're new to Docker: Read through F.1–F.5 before diving into the MCQs. These foundations will give you the mental models needed to understand the questions. The entire guide uses the anihpj/jobpost Django application as its running example.

📝 Part 1: 200 Multiple Choice Questions

Complete DCA MCQ Bank — Organized by the 6 official Mirantis exam domains. Each question includes a detailed explanation, diagrams, and real-world context using anihpj/jobpost.

200Questions

🎯 Category 1: Orchestration (25%)Q1-Q50

🟢 Novice-Friendly: Understanding Docker Orchestration

This category is about running containers at scale — from a single docker run to managing hundreds of containers across multiple machines with Docker Swarm. Think of it like evolving from cooking one meal at home to running a restaurant kitchen. For anihpj/jobpost, orchestration means deploying the Django web tier, API tier, PostgreSQL database, and Redis cache as Swarm services that self-heal and scale.

Q1-Q50 50 Questions 🟢 Beginner

1.1 Swarm FundamentalsQ1-Q10

Orchestration Q1 of 200
What command initializes a Docker Swarm cluster on the current node, making it the first manager?
A) docker swarm create --manager
B) docker cluster init --role manager
C) docker swarm init
D) docker swarm start --advertise-addr eth0
✅ Correct Answer: C

docker swarm init initializes a new Swarm cluster. The node where you run this command becomes the first manager node in the swarm. This is the foundational command — without it, there are no services, no overlay networks, and no orchestration.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Docker Swarm Architecture — Manager & Worker Nodes
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                     DOCKER SWARM CLUSTER                         │
  │                                                                 │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────┐    ┌─────────────────────────┐    │
  │  │  MANAGER NODE (anih)    │    │  WORKER NODE            │    │
  │  │  ┌───────────────────┐  │    │  ┌───────────────────┐  │    │
  │  │  │ docker swarm init │  │    │  │ docker swarm join │  │    │
  │  │  │ → Raft consensus  │  │    │  │ → Runs tasks only │  │    │
  │  │  │ → Orchestration   │  │    │  │ → No management   │  │    │
  │  │  │ → Service API     │  │    │  └───────────────────┘  │    │
  │  │  └───────────────────┘  │                               │    │
  │  │  Generates join tokens: │    ┌─────────────────────────┐    │
  │  │  $ docker swarm         │    │  WORKER NODE            │    │
  │  │    join-token manager   │    │  (same join mechanism)  │    │
  │  │    join-token worker    │    └─────────────────────────┘    │
  │  └─────────────────────────┘                                   │
  │                                                                 │
  │  After init: docker node ls → shows 1 manager, 0 workers        │
  │  docker info | grep Swarm → Swarm: active, Managers: 1          │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  
  Common flags: --advertise-addr (for multi-NIC), --listen-addr

After docker swarm init, Docker prints the join command that worker nodes use: docker swarm join --token SWMTKN-1-xxx 192.168.1.10:2377. For the anihpj/jobpost stack, you'd deploy the web, API, database, and Redis as Swarm services across your worker nodes for high availability.

💡 DCA Exam Tip: The DCA exam tests your knowledge of docker swarm init, docker swarm join, docker swarm join-token (rotate), and docker swarm leave. Remember: swarm init creates the cluster; swarm join adds nodes.

Orchestration Q2 of 200
After running docker swarm init on the manager, the output shows a command to add workers. What command does a worker node run to join the swarm?
A) docker swarm join --token SWMTKN-1-xxx <manager-ip>:2377
B) docker node connect --swarm <manager-ip>
C) docker swarm add-worker --manager <manager-ip>:2377
D) docker swarm attach --token SWMTKN-1-xxx
✅ Correct Answer: A

docker swarm join --token <token> <manager-ip>:2377 is the exact command printed by docker swarm init. The token is a SWMTKN (Swarm Token) that authenticates the joining node to the cluster. Port 2377 is the Swarm control plane port.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Worker Node Join — Full Process Flow
  MANAGER NODE (192.168.1.10)              WORKER NODE (192.168.1.20)
  ┌─────────────────────────┐              ┌─────────────────────────┐
  │ $ docker swarm init     │              │                         │
  │ Swarm initialized...    │              │                         │
  │                         │              │                         │
  │ To add a worker, run:   │              │ $ docker swarm join \   │
  │ docker swarm join \     │──────────────▶│   --token SWMTKN-1-... │
  │   --token SWMTKN-1-...  │   copy+paste  │   192.168.1.10:2377    │
  │   192.168.1.10:2377     │              │                         │
  └─────────────────────────┘              │ This node joined the    │
                                           │ swarm as a worker.     │
  To add a MANAGER, run:                   └─────────────────────────┘
  docker swarm join \
    --token SWMTKN-1-... (different token!)
    192.168.1.10:2377

  ⚠️ CRITICAL: Manager join token ≠ Worker join token
     $ docker swarm join-token manager  → shows manager token
     $ docker swarm join-token worker   → shows worker token

After joining, the worker node appears in docker node ls on the manager. For the anihpj/jobpost stack, you'd add at least 2-3 worker nodes so your web, API, and database services can be distributed across the swarm for high availability and load balancing.

💡 DCA Exam Tip: Remember that docker swarm join requires BOTH the token AND the manager IP:port. The token proves you're authorized; the IP:port tells the node where to find the manager. If port 2377 is blocked by a firewall, the join will fail with "connection refused".

Orchestration Q3 of 200
What is the fundamental difference between a Docker Swarm manager node and a worker node?
A) Managers run containers; workers only store logs and metrics
B) Managers handle cluster orchestration (scheduling, desired state, API); workers only execute tasks (run containers)
C) Workers manage the swarm; managers run user applications
D) There is no difference — all nodes in a swarm are equal
✅ Correct Answer: B

Managers run the control plane: they maintain the desired state, schedule services onto nodes, serve the Swarm API, and use Raft consensus to agree on cluster state. Workers are "dumb executors" — they receive tasks from managers and run containers. Workers do NOT participate in orchestration decisions.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Manager vs Worker — Responsibilities Split
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                     MANAGER NODE                                 │
  │  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │  ✅ Cluster orchestration           ✅ Scheduling          │  │
  │  │  ✅ Maintain desired state          ✅ Serve Swarm API     │  │
  │  │  ✅ Raft consensus (leader/follower)✅ docker node ls/promote │
  │  │  ✅ Store cluster config in Raft log                       │  │
  │  │  ❌ Can also RUN containers (by default)                   │  │
  │  │     → docker node update --availability drain <manager>    │  │
  │  │       to prevent managers from running workloads           │  │
  │  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                     WORKER NODE                                  │
  │  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │  ✅ Execute tasks (run containers)                         │  │
  │  │  ✅ Report status back to managers                        │  │
  │  │  ✅ Pull images, start/stop containers                    │  │
  │  │  ❌ CANNOT manage the swarm                               │  │
  │  │  ❌ CANNOT schedule services                              │  │
  │  │  ❌ CANNOT run docker node ls, docker service create       │  │
  │  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Promote a worker to manager:  docker node promote <worker>
  Demote a manager to worker:   docker node demote <manager>

For the anihpj/jobpost production deployment, you'd typically run 3 managers (for Raft quorum) and 2-5 workers (for workload capacity). Managers can also run containers by default, but in production you should drain managers so they focus only on orchestration.

💡 DCA Exam Tip: The DCA exam will ask you to docker node promote and docker node demote. Remember: worker → manager = promote; manager → worker = demote. Also know that docker node update --availability drain prevents a node from receiving new tasks.

Orchestration Q4 of 200
A production Docker Swarm should have an odd number of manager nodes. What is the maximum number of manager failures a 5-manager swarm can tolerate and still function?
A) 1 manager failure — the remaining 4 vote and the majority wins
B) 5 manager failures — Swarm uses a round-robin failover, not quorum
C) 3 manager failures — any number less than 5 keeps the swarm alive
D) 2 manager failures — Raft requires (N/2)+1 managers for quorum, so 3 out of 5 must be healthy
✅ Correct Answer: D

Docker Swarm uses the Raft consensus algorithm, which requires a majority (quorum) of managers to be healthy: ⌊N/2⌋ + 1. With 5 managers, quorum is 3 — so you can lose 2 managers and the swarm still functions. If you lose a 3rd, the swarm becomes unavailable (no new services, no scheduling).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Raft Quorum — Why Odd Numbers Matter
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  RAFT QUORUM TABLE                                               │
  ├──────────┬──────────────┬──────────────────┬────────────────────┤
  │ Managers │ Quorum Needed│ Max Failures     │ Recommended?       │
  ├──────────┼──────────────┼──────────────────┼────────────────────┤
  │    1     │      1       │       0          │ ❌ No HA           │
  │    2     │      2       │       0          │ ❌ Worse than 1!   │
  │    3     │      2       │       1          │ ✅ Minimum for HA  │
  │    5     │      3       │       2          │ ✅ Production      │
  │    7     │      4       │       3          │ ✅ Large clusters  │
  └──────────┴──────────────┴──────────────────┴────────────────────┘

  WHY 2 MANAGERS IS WORSE THAN 1:
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  2 managers → quorum = 2 → can't tolerate ANY failure          │
  │  1 manager  → quorum = 1 → at least the swarm is usable        │
  │  → Always use 1, 3, 5, or 7 managers — NEVER 2, 4, or 6       │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  RAFT IN ACTION:
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  [M1 LEADER] ←→ [M2 FOLLOWER] ←→ [M3 FOLLOWER]                 │
  │       │              │                 │                         │
  │  All writes go through the Leader, who replicates to Followers  │
  │  If Leader dies → election → new Leader chosen by majority     │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For the anihpj/jobpost production swarm, deploy 3 managers (tolerates 1 failure) or 5 managers (tolerates 2 failures). Keep managers in different availability zones. If quorum is lost, the swarm continues running existing containers but you cannot deploy new services, scale, or update — it's effectively read-only.

💡 DCA Exam Tip: The formula ⌊N/2⌋ + 1 is tested on the DCA. For 3 managers: ⌊3/2⌋ + 1 = 1 + 1 = 2 managers needed → tolerates 1 failure. For 5 managers: ⌊5/2⌋ + 1 = 2 + 1 = 3 needed → tolerates 2 failures. Never deploy an even number of managers.

Orchestration Q5 of 200
If a Swarm manager's join token is accidentally shared publicly, what command should you run to invalidate the old token and generate a new one?
A) docker swarm token-regenerate --rotate worker
B) docker swarm join-token --rotate worker
C) docker swarm init --new-token
D) docker node update --token-rotate worker
✅ Correct Answer: B

docker swarm join-token --rotate <role> generates a new token for the specified role (manager or worker). The old token is immediately invalidated. This is a critical security operation — if a token leaks, anyone can join nodes to your swarm.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Token Rotation — Secure Your Swarm
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  TOKEN MANAGEMENT LIFECYCLE                                      │
  │                                                                 │
  │  1. VIEW CURRENT TOKENS:                                        │
  │     $ docker swarm join-token manager                           │
  │     SWMTKN-1-5xz0...abc123-6def456 192.168.1.10:2377           │
  │     $ docker swarm join-token worker                            │
  │     SWMTKN-1-5xz0...abc123-8ghi789 192.168.1.10:2377           │
  │                                                                 │
  │  2. TOKEN COMPROMISED? ROTATE IMMEDIATELY:                      │
  │     $ docker swarm join-token --rotate manager                  │
  │     SWMTKN-1-7yz0...xyz789-9jkl012 192.168.1.10:2377  ← NEW!   │
  │                                                                 │
  │  3. EFFECTS OF ROTATION:                                        │
  │     ✅ Old token invalidated immediately                        │
  │     ✅ Existing nodes UNAFFECTED (they're already joined)       │
  │     ✅ New nodes must use the NEW token                         │
  │     ⚠️  Workers joining with old token → "invalid token" error  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  TOKEN STRUCTURE:
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  SWMTKN-1-<cluster-id>-<node-hash>  <manager-ip>:2377        │
  │  ─────── ── ──────────── ───────────                            │
  │  Prefix   │  Cluster ID    Node-specific hash                   │
  │         Version                                               │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For the anihpj/jobpost swarm, always rotate tokens if a team member leaves the organization, if a token is accidentally committed to a Git repository, or as part of regular security hygiene (quarterly). Combined with docker swarm unlock for autolock, this provides defense-in-depth for swarm access control.

💡 DCA Exam Tip: The DCA exam expects you to know that rotating a token does NOT affect already-joined nodes. Only new nodes attempting to join are affected. Also remember: --rotate works for BOTH manager and worker tokens independently.

Orchestration Q6 of 200
You need to permanently remove a worker node from a Docker Swarm. What is the correct sequence of commands?
A) On the manager: docker node rm worker-1 — that's all you need
B) On the worker: docker swarm leave, then on the manager: docker node rm worker-1
C) On the manager: docker swarm remove-node worker-1
D) Stop the Docker daemon on the worker node — it will automatically be removed after 5 minutes
✅ Correct Answer: B

First, the worker must leave the swarm by running docker swarm leave on the worker node. Then, on a manager node, run docker node rm <node> to remove the node's record from the swarm. If a manager node needs to be removed, you must first docker node demote it to worker.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Node Removal — Proper Shutdown Sequence
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  WORKER REMOVAL (Clean)                                         │
  │                                                                 │
  │  ON THE WORKER NODE:                                            │
  │  $ docker swarm leave                                          │
  │  Node left the swarm.                                          │
  │                                                                 │
  │  ON A MANAGER NODE:                                             │
  │  $ docker node ls                                              │
  │  ID        HOSTNAME  STATUS  AVAILABILITY  MANAGER STATUS      │
  │  abc123 *  manager1  Ready   Active        Leader              │
  │  def456    worker-1  Down    Active                             │
  │                                                                 │
  │  $ docker node rm worker-1                                     │
  │  worker-1                                                       │
  │                                                                 │
  │  MANAGER REMOVAL (Extra Step):                                  │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ 1. docker node demote <manager>    ← demote to worker   │   │
  │  │ 2. docker swarm leave               ← on the node itself │   │
  │  │ 3. docker node rm <node>            ← from a manager     │   │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                 │
  │  ⚠️  FORCE LEAVE (if node is unreachable):                      │
  │  $ docker swarm leave --force   ← on the leaving node          │
  │  $ docker node rm --force <node> ← from a manager             │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For the anihpj/jobpost swarm, always follow the proper drain → leave → rm sequence when decommissioning nodes. Leaving a node without removing it from the manager leaves a "Down" entry in docker node ls that clutters the node list and can confuse monitoring tools.

💡 DCA Exam Tip: The DCA exam expects you to know that docker swarm leave is run ON the leaving node, while docker node rm is run ON a manager. If a manager wants to leave, it must first be demoted to worker. A manager cannot leave the swarm while still holding the manager role.

Orchestration Q7 of 200
You run docker node ls on a manager and see a worker node with STATUS: Down and AVAILABILITY: Active. What does this combination indicate?
A) The node has been drained and is waiting for new tasks
B) The node is healthy but temporarily not accepting tasks
C) The node has been removed from the swarm
D) The Docker daemon on that worker is stopped or unreachable, but the node is still registered in the swarm and expected to run tasks
✅ Correct Answer: D

STATUS: Down means the manager hasn't heard from the worker's Docker daemon (heartbeat failed). AVAILABILITY: Active means the node is still expected to run tasks — the scheduler will try to place new services on it, which will fail. This is the worst combination: the swarm thinks the node is available, but it's actually dead.

📝 Detailed Explanation
docker node ls — Decoding STATUS + AVAILABILITY
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  STATUS        │  AVAILABILITY  │  MEANING                      │
  ├────────────────┼────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┤
  │  Ready         │  Active        │  ✅ Normal — healthy worker    │
  │  Ready         │  Drain         │  ⏳ Draining — no new tasks    │
  │  Ready         │  Pause         │  ⏸️  Paused — tasks stay but   │
  │                │                │     no new ones assigned       │
  │  Down          │  Active        │  ❌ DANGER — daemon dead but   │
  │                │                │     swarm expects it to work   │
  │  Down          │  Drain         │  🟡 Drained then died — OK    │
  │  Disconnected  │  Active        │  ⚠️  Network issue, temp       │
  └────────────────┴────────────────┴───────────────────────────────┘

  FIX for Down + Active:
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  1. Check if node can be recovered:                             │
  │     $ ssh worker-1 "systemctl status docker"                   │
  │                                                                 │
  │  2. If recoverable: restart Docker → STATUS returns to Ready   │
  │                                                                 │
  │  3. If NOT recoverable:                                         │
  │     $ docker node update --availability drain worker-1         │
  │     (prevents scheduler from assigning new tasks)               │
  │     Then remove: $ docker node rm --force worker-1              │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For the anihpj/jobpost production swarm, monitor docker node ls output and set up alerts for any node showing Down + Active. This state means your services may have unreachable replicas, and the swarm won't automatically reschedule them until the node is drained or removed.

💡 DCA Exam Tip: The DCA exam tests your ability to interpret docker node ls output. Know the difference between STATUS (Ready/Down/Disconnected — daemon health) and AVAILABILITY (Active/Drain/Pause — scheduling state). They are independent concepts.

Orchestration Q8 of 200
You need to perform maintenance on a Swarm worker node without disrupting running services. Which command gracefully moves all tasks off the node and prevents new tasks from being scheduled on it?
A) docker node update --availability drain worker-1
B) docker node rm worker-1
C) docker service update --constraint-add 'node.hostname!=worker-1'
D) docker swarm leave worker-1
✅ Correct Answer: A

docker node update --availability drain <node> gracefully stops all running tasks on that node and starts equivalent tasks on other available nodes. The drained node won't receive any new tasks. This is the safe way to take a node offline for maintenance — the Swarm equivalent of kubectl drain.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Node Drain — Graceful Maintenance Workflow
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  DRAIN SEQUENCE:                                                │
  │                                                                 │
  │  1. BEFORE DRAIN:                                               │
  │     $ docker node ls                                           │
  │     worker-1  Ready   Active   ← 3 tasks running               │
  │     worker-2  Ready   Active   ← 3 tasks running               │
  │                                                                 │
  │  2. DRAIN THE NODE:                                             │
  │     $ docker node update --availability drain worker-1         │
  │     worker-1                                                    │
  │                                                                 │
  │  3. DURING DRAIN (transient):                                   │
  │     $ docker node ls                                           │
  │     worker-1  Ready   Drain    ← tasks being moved             │
  │     worker-2  Ready   Active   ← 6 tasks (took over from w1)   │
  │                                                                 │
  │  4. DRAIN COMPLETE:                                             │
  │     $ docker node ls                                           │
  │     worker-1  Ready   Drain    ← 0 tasks, no new assignments   │
  │     worker-2  Ready   Active   ← 6 tasks running               │
  │                                                                 │
  │  5. PERFORM MAINTENANCE on worker-1                             │
  │                                                                 │
  │  6. RETURN TO SERVICE:                                          │
  │     $ docker node update --availability active worker-1        │
  │     (Node can receive new tasks again, but old tasks            │
  │      won't automatically move back — only NEW tasks)            │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For the anihpj/jobpost swarm, always drain before maintenance. If you have only 1 replica of anihpj-db (PostgreSQL), draining the node running it will cause a brief outage while Swarm reschedules it elsewhere. For production, always run at least 2 replicas of critical services so drain operations are zero-downtime.

💡 DCA Exam Tip: The DCA exam expects you to know that --availability drain stops containers on the node and reschedules them elsewhere. After maintenance, setting it back to Active does NOT move tasks back — new tasks may be scheduled there, but existing tasks stay where they were rescheduled. Use --availability pause if you want tasks to stay running but no new ones assigned.

Orchestration Q9 of 200
What is the purpose of Docker Swarm's autolock feature, and how do you enable it?
A) Autolock encrypts container filesystems at rest; enable with docker swarm init --encrypt-containers
B) Autolock prevents unauthorized docker commands on worker nodes; enable with docker node lock --enable
C) Autolock protects the Raft logs and TLS keys on disk. When a locked manager restarts, it requires an unlock key before rejoining. Enable with docker swarm init --autolock or docker swarm update --autolock=true
D) Autolock automatically locks the swarm after 15 minutes of inactivity; it is enabled by default on all new swarms
✅ Correct Answer: C

Autolock encrypts the Raft logs and TLS private keys stored on manager nodes' disks. When a locked manager restarts (e.g., after a reboot or crash), it cannot rejoin the swarm until an administrator provides the unlock key. This prevents an attacker with physical access to a decommissioned manager's disk from reading cluster secrets.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Swarm Autolock — Protecting Data at Rest
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ENABLING AUTOLOCK:                                             │
  │                                                                 │
  │  At init time:                                                  │
  │  $ docker swarm init --autolock                                │
  │  Swarm initialized: ...                                        │
  │  *** Unlock key: SWMKEY-1-Xv7q...3kL9w== ***                  │
  │  ⚠️  SAVE THIS KEY! It's needed to unlock managers after restart │
  │                                                                 │
  │  On an existing swarm:                                          │
  │  $ docker swarm update --autolock=true                         │
  │  Swarm updated.                                                │
  │  *** Unlock key: SWMKEY-1-Yp2r...8mF4x== ***                  │
  │                                                                 │
  │  MANAGER RESTART WITH AUTOLOCK:                                 │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ $ systemctl restart docker                              │   │
  │  │ $ docker node ls                                        │   │
  │  │ Error: Swarm is encrypted and needs to be unlocked      │   │
  │  │                                                        │   │
  │  │ $ docker swarm unlock                                   │   │
  │  │ Please enter unlock key: SWMKEY-1-Xv7q...3kL9w==       │   │
  │  │ Swarm unlocked.                                         │   │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                 │
  │  VIEW/CANCEL THE KEY:                                           │
  │  $ docker swarm unlock-key        ← shows current key          │
  │  $ docker swarm unlock-key --rotate ← generates new key        │
  │  $ docker swarm update --autolock=false ← disables autolock    │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For the anihpj/jobpost production swarm, always enable autolock and store the unlock key in a secure secrets manager (AWS Secrets Manager, HashiCorp Vault, or a hardware security module). If you lose the unlock key and all managers restart simultaneously, the swarm is permanently unrecoverable.

💡 DCA Exam Tip: The DCA exam emphasizes that autolock protects data at rest (on disk), not data in transit. It encrypts the Raft log and TLS keys stored on manager nodes. The unlock key is NOT the same as a join token — it's only needed when a locked manager restarts. Existing, running managers don't need it.

Orchestration Q10 of 200
A worker node named "node-3" needs to become a manager to help maintain quorum after a manager failure. What command promotes it?
A) docker node update --role manager node-3
B) docker node promote node-3
C) docker swarm manager-add node-3
D) docker node upgrade --to-manager node-3
✅ Correct Answer: B

docker node promote <node> upgrades a worker to a manager. Conversely, docker node demote <node> downgrades a manager to a worker. This allows dynamic adjustment of the manager set — critical when a manager fails and you need to restore quorum.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Node Promote/Demote — Dynamic Manager Set
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  SCENARIO: 3-manager swarm, 1 manager dies                      │
  │                                                                 │
  │  BEFORE FAILURE:                                                │
  │  $ docker node ls                                              │
  │  ID  HOSTNAME  STATUS  AVAILABILITY  MANAGER STATUS            │
  │  a1  manager1  Ready   Active        Leader                   │
  │  b2  manager2  Ready   Active        Reachable                │
  │  c3  manager3  Down    Active        Unreachable  ← DEAD!     │
  │  d4  worker1   Ready   Active                                 │
  │  e5  worker2   Ready   Active                                 │
  │                                                                 │
  │  QUORUM: 2/3 managers healthy → STILL FUNCTIONAL but at risk   │
  │                                                                 │
  │  FIX — PROMOTE A WORKER:                                        │
  │  $ docker node promote worker1                                 │
  │  worker1 promoted to a manager.                                │
  │                                                                 │
  │  AFTER PROMOTION:                                               │
  │  $ docker node ls                                              │
  │  ID  HOSTNAME  STATUS  AVAILABILITY  MANAGER STATUS            │
  │  a1  manager1  Ready   Active        Leader                   │
  │  b2  manager2  Ready   Active        Reachable                │
  │  d4  worker1   Ready   Active        Reachable  ← NOW MANAGER │
  │  e5  worker2   Ready   Active                                 │
  │                                                                 │
  │  QUORUM: 3/3 managers healthy → FULLY RECOVERED                │
  │                                                                 │
  │  DEMOTE A MANAGER:                                              │
  │  $ docker node demote manager3                                 │
  │  manager3 demoted to a worker.                                  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ⚠️  RULES:
  • Can only promote/demote from a manager node
  • Promoting adds the node to the Raft consensus group
  • Demoting removes it from Raft — use carefully near quorum limits
  • Always keep an ODD number of managers (1, 3, 5, 7)

For the anihpj/jobpost swarm, if a manager node suffers a hardware failure, immediately promote a healthy worker to restore quorum. Monitor with docker node ls and set up alerts for any manager showing "Unreachable" or "Down". A swarm with lost quorum still runs existing containers but cannot schedule new ones.

💡 DCA Exam Tip: The DCA exam will ask you to use docker node promote and docker node demote. Remember: these commands must be run from a manager node (not a worker). A worker cannot promote itself — only existing managers can issue these commands. Also know that docker swarm join-token manager is the alternative way to add a manager from scratch.

1.2 Services Deep DiveQ11-Q20

Orchestration Q11 of 200
What does docker service create --replicas 3 --name web nginx do?
A) Creates 3 separate Docker containers named web-1, web-2, web-3 on the current node
B) Creates a single nginx container and configures it to spawn 2 child processes
C) Creates a Swarm config named "web" with 3 replicas
D) Creates a Swarm service named "web" that maintains 3 nginx container replicas distributed across the swarm
✅ Correct Answer: D

docker service create defines a Swarm service — a declarative definition of a replicated application. The swarm scheduler ensures exactly 3 nginx containers are running, distributed across available nodes. If a container dies, it's automatically replaced.

📝 Detailed Explanation
docker run vs docker service create — The Critical Difference
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  docker run nginx                                               │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  • Runs ONE container on the current node                │   │
  │  │  • If container dies → NOT automatically restarted       │   │
  │  │  │   (unless --restart=always is used)                   │   │
  │  │  • No scaling, no rolling updates, no overlay network    │   │
  │  │  • docker ps shows it as a regular container             │   │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                 │
  │  docker service create --replicas 3 nginx                       │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  • Declares DESIRED STATE: 3 nginx replicas              │   │
  │  │  • Swarm scheduler distributes across ALL worker nodes   │   │
  │  │  • If a replica dies → automatically replaced            │   │
  │  │  • Supports rolling updates, rollbacks, scaling          │   │
  │  │  • docker service ps web → see which node runs what      │   │
  │  │  • Uses overlay network for cross-node communication     │   │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, you'd use docker service create --replicas 3 --name anihpj-api anihpj/jobpost:latest to run 3 API replicas across the swarm. The swarm load balances incoming requests across all 3 replicas via the routing mesh.

💡 DCA Exam Tip: docker service create is NOT the same as docker run. Services are swarm-only objects managed by the orchestrator, not individual containers.

Orchestration Q12 of 200
How do you scale a running Swarm service named "api" from 3 replicas to 5 replicas?
A) docker service update --replicas 5 api
B) docker service scale 5 api
C) docker service scale api=5
D) docker service resize api --count 5
✅ Correct Answer: C

docker service scale api=5 changes the desired replica count. The swarm scheduler then creates 2 new tasks to reach 5 total. You can also use docker service update --replicas 5 api (option A is also technically valid — but option C is the dedicated scale command and more commonly used on the exam).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Scaling a Service — What Happens Under the Hood
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ docker service scale api=5                                   │
  │                                                                 │
  │  BEFORE (3 replicas):         AFTER (5 replicas):               │
  │  ┌─────────┬─────────┐       ┌─────────┬─────────┬─────────┐  │
  │  │ worker1 │ worker2 │       │ worker1 │ worker2 │ worker3 │  │
  │  │  api.1  │  api.2  │       │  api.1  │  api.2  │  api.3  │  │
  │  │         │  api.3  │       │  api.5  │  api.4  │         │  │
  │  └─────────┴─────────┘       └─────────┴─────────┴─────────┘  │
  │                                                                 │
  │  SCALE MULTIPLE SERVICES AT ONCE:                               │
  │  $ docker service scale api=5 web=3 worker=10                   │
  │                                                                 │
  │  EQUIVALENT (using update):                                     │
  │  $ docker service update --replicas 5 api                       │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, you'd scale anihpj-api based on load: docker service scale anihpj-api=10 during peak job application season, then scale back to 3 during off-peak hours.

💡 DCA Exam Tip: Both docker service scale and docker service update --replicas work. The scale command also accepts multiple services at once. Know both for the exam.

Orchestration Q13 of 200
You updated a service with a broken image. The new tasks are failing. What command reverts to the previous working version?
A) docker service rollback api
B) docker service revert api
C) docker service update --revert api
D) docker service undo api --to-previous
✅ Correct Answer: A

docker service rollback api reverts the service to its previous task definition — including the image, environment variables, ports, and all other settings from before the last update.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Service Rollback — Quick Recovery from Bad Deployments
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  SCENARIO:                                                      │
  │  $ docker service update --image anihpj-api:v2.0 api           │
  │  → v2.0 has a bug → all new tasks crash                        │
  │                                                                 │
  │  RECOVERY:                                                      │
  │  $ docker service rollback api                                 │
  │  → Reverts to v1.0 (the previous working version)              │
  │  → Tasks are replaced with the old image                       │
  │                                                                 │
  │  ROLLBACK OPTIONS:                                              │
  │  $ docker service rollback --detach api   ← async rollback     │
  │                                                                 │
  │  CHECK ROLLBACK STATUS:                                         │
  │  $ docker service ps api                                       │
  │  (shows tasks transitioning from v2.0 back to v1.0)            │
  │                                                                 │
  │  ⚠️  Only ONE level of rollback is stored (the previous spec)   │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, if a new API image breaks database connections, docker service rollback anihpj-api instantly restores the working version while you debug v2.0.

💡 DCA Exam Tip: docker service rollback only stores the immediately previous configuration. It doesn't keep a full history like Kubernetes deployments. Use it as your first response to a broken update.

Orchestration Q14 of 200
What does the --update-parallelism flag control during a docker service update?
A) How many CPU cores each task can use during the update
B) How many tasks are updated simultaneously (batch size for rolling updates)
C) How many nodes participate in the update process
D) The total number of containers allowed during the update
✅ Correct Answer: B

--update-parallelism 2 means Docker updates 2 tasks at a time. Combined with --update-delay 10s, it waits 10 seconds between each batch. This gives you a controlled rolling update — never taking down all replicas at once.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Rolling Update — Parallelism & Delay in Action
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ docker service update \                                      │
  │      --update-parallelism 2 \                                   │
  │      --update-delay 15s \                                       │
  │      --image anihpj-api:v2.0 api                                │
  │                                                                 │
  │  TIMELINE (6 replicas):                                         │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ t=0s  ████ ████ ████ ████ ████ ████  (all v1.0)       │   │
  │  │ t=0s  ░░░░ ░░░░ ████ ████ ████ ████  (2 updating)     │   │
  │  │ t=5s  ████ ████ ████ ████ ████ ████  (2 done=v2.0)    │   │
  │  │ t=15s ████ ████ ░░░░ ░░░░ ████ ████  (next 2 update)  │   │
  │  │ t=30s ████ ████ ████ ████ ░░░░ ░░░░  (final 2 update) │   │
  │  │ t=45s ████ ████ ████ ████ ████ ████  (all v2.0)       │   │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │  ████ = running v2.0    ░░░░ = updating                        │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, use --update-parallelism 1 --update-delay 30s for the database service to ensure zero-downtime migrations, and --update-parallelism 50% for stateless API services for faster rollouts.

💡 DCA Exam Tip: Three key update flags: --update-parallelism (batch size), --update-delay (time between batches), --update-failure-action (pause/continue/rollback on failure). Know all three.

Orchestration Q15 of 200
What is the difference between Replicated and Global service modes in Docker Swarm?
A) Replicated services run only on managers; Global services run only on workers
B) Replicated services are stateless; Global services are stateful
C) Replicated services use the overlay network; Global services use the host network
D) Replicated runs exactly N replicas anywhere in the swarm; Global runs exactly 1 task on EVERY node (including new nodes)
✅ Correct Answer: D

Replicated (default): You specify a number, and the swarm maintains exactly that many replicas. Global: One task runs on every node that meets the constraints — perfect for monitoring agents, log collectors, or security scanners.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Replicated vs Global — When to Use Each
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  REPLICATED (--mode replicated) — DEFAULT                       │
  │  $ docker service create --replicas 3 nginx                    │
  │  ┌─────────┬─────────┬─────────┬─────────┐                    │
  │  │ node-1  │ node-2  │ node-3  │ node-4  │                    │
  │  │ nginx.1 │ nginx.2 │ nginx.3 │         │ ← 4 nodes, 3 tasks  │
  │  └─────────┴─────────┴─────────┴─────────┘                    │
  │  Use for: Web servers, APIs, databases (your application)       │
  │                                                                 │
  │  GLOBAL (--mode global)                                         │
  │  $ docker service create --mode global fluentd                 │
  │  ┌─────────┬─────────┬─────────┬─────────┐                    │
  │  │ node-1  │ node-2  │ node-3  │ node-4  │                    │
  │  │ flu.1   │ flu.2   │ flu.3   │ flu.4   │ ← 4 nodes, 4 tasks │
  │  └─────────┴─────────┴─────────┴─────────┘                    │
  │  Use for: Monitoring agents, log shippers, security scanners    │
  │                                                                 │
  │  NEW NODE JOINS:                                                │
  │  • Replicated: replica count stays at 3 (no new task)          │
  │  • Global: automatically adds 1 task on the new node            │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, deploy anihpj-api as replicated (e.g., 5 replicas) and a Fluentd log collector as global (one per node to collect all container logs).

💡 DCA Exam Tip: Global services are the Swarm equivalent of Kubernetes DaemonSets. They're ideal for infrastructure components that need to run on every node.

1.3 Stacks & ComposeQ21-Q28

Orchestration Q21 of 200
What command deploys a full application stack defined in a docker-compose.yml file to a Docker Swarm?
A) docker compose up --swarm
B) docker stack deploy -c docker-compose.yml mystack
C) docker swarm deploy --file docker-compose.yml
D) docker service create --stack docker-compose.yml
✅ Correct Answer: B

docker stack deploy -c <compose-file> <stack-name> takes a Compose file and deploys it as a Swarm stack. Each service in the Compose file becomes a Swarm service. The stack name prefixes all resources: mystack_web, mystack_api, etc.

📝 Detailed Explanation
docker stack deploy — From Compose File to Swarm Services
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  docker-compose.yml:                                            │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ version: "3.8"                                         │   │
  │  │ services:                                              │   │
  │  │   web:                                                 │   │
  │  │     image: nginx                                       │   │
  │  │     ports: ["80:80"]                                   │   │
  │  │     deploy:                                            │   │
  │  │       replicas: 3                                      │   │
  │  │   api:                                                 │   │
  │  │     image: anihpj-api:v1.0                             │   │
  │  │     deploy:                                            │   │
  │  │       replicas: 5                                      │   │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                 │
  │  $ docker stack deploy -c docker-compose.yml anihpj             │
  │                                                                 │
  │  Creates:                                                       │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ • Service: anihpj_web (3 replicas)                     │   │
  │  │ • Service: anihpj_api (5 replicas)                     │   │
  │  │ • Network: anihpj_default (overlay)                    │   │
  │  │ • All prefixed with stack name: "anihpj_"              │   │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For the full anihpj/jobpost production deployment, define all 4 tiers (web, api, db, redis) in a single docker-stack.yml and deploy with docker stack deploy -c docker-stack.yml anihpj. This creates all services, the overlay network, secrets, and configs in one command.

💡 DCA Exam Tip: Stack names must be unique. If you deploy a stack named "anihpj" twice, the second deploy updates the existing stack. Use docker stack ls to list stacks and docker stack services anihpj to see services within a stack.

Orchestration Q22 of 200
What is the key difference between docker compose up and docker stack deploy?
A) docker compose up runs containers locally; docker stack deploy creates Swarm services distributed across the cluster
B) There is no difference — they are aliases for the same command
C) docker compose up is for Linux only; docker stack deploy works on all platforms
D) docker compose up requires a Swarm; docker stack deploy works standalone
✅ Correct Answer: A

docker compose up creates regular containers on the local Docker Engine — no Swarm required. docker stack deploy creates Swarm services distributed across the cluster with orchestration features (rolling updates, self-healing, overlay networking).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Compose vs Stack — Local Dev vs Production Swarm
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  docker compose up (LOCAL DEV)                                  │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ ✅ Single host only         ❌ No rolling updates       │   │
  │  │ ✅ Simple docker-compose.yml❌ No self-healing          │   │
  │  │ ✅ docker compose logs      ❌ No overlay networking    │   │
  │  │ ✅ docker compose down      ❌ No secrets/configs       │   │
  │  │ Use: Local development, testing, CI/CD                  │   │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                 │
  │  docker stack deploy (PRODUCTION SWARM)                         │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ ✅ Multi-node cluster        ✅ Rolling updates          │   │
  │  │ ✅ Self-healing (restart)    ✅ Overlay networking       │   │
  │  │ ✅ Secrets and configs       ✅ docker service logs      │   │
  │  │ ✅ docker stack rm           ✅ docker service scale     │   │
  │  │ Use: Production, staging, multi-host deployments         │   │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost development, use docker compose up on your local machine. For production, convert the Compose file (adding deploy: sections) and use docker stack deploy on the Swarm cluster.

💡 DCA Exam Tip: docker stack deploy ignores build: directives in Compose files — images must already exist in a registry. docker compose up supports building images on the fly.

Orchestration Q16 of 200
What command shows the status and node placement of each task (replica) for a Swarm service?
A) docker service ls --verbose
B) docker service ps <service-name>
C) docker service inspect --tasks <service-name>
D) docker task list --service <service-name>
✅ Correct Answer: B

docker service ps <service-name> shows every task (replica) with its current state, node placement, and error messages. This is your go-to troubleshooting command for "why is my service not working?"

📝 Detailed Explanation
docker service ps — Decoding Task States
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ docker service ps anihpj-api                                 │
  │  ID    NAME           IMAGE            NODE      STATE   ERROR  │
  │  x1a2  anihpj-api.1   anihpj-api:v1    worker1   Running        │
  │  b3c4  anihpj-api.2   anihpj-api:v1    worker2   Running        │
  │  d5e6  anihpj-api.3   anihpj-api:v1    worker3   Running        │
  │  f7g8  anihpj-api.4   anihpj-api:v2    worker1   Failed  "img" │
  │                                                                 │
  │  TASK STATES TO KNOW:                                           │
  │  • Running — Healthy                                             │
  │  │ • Preparing — Pulling image, creating network                │
  │  │ • Pending — Waiting for resources                            │
  │  │ • Failed — Task exited with error                            │
  │  │ • Shutdown — Task was stopped                                │
  │  │ • Rejected — Scheduler couldn't place it                     │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                 │
  │  COMPARE DESIRED STATE column to spot mismatches                 │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For the anihpj/jobpost stack, use docker service ps anihpj-api to verify all API replicas are Running and evenly distributed across workers. If you see "Failed" or "Rejected", check docker service inspect anihpj-api for detailed error messages.

💡 DCA Exam Tip: docker service ps is your #1 diagnostic command for service issues. It shows the DESIRED STATE column too — compare CURRENT STATE vs DESIRED STATE to spot problems. A task "Running" with desired "Shutdown" means it's being replaced.

Orchestration Q17 of 200
How do you view aggregated logs of ALL replicas of a Swarm service?
A) docker logs --all <service-name>
B) docker container logs --service <service-name>
C) docker service logs <service-name>
D) docker swarm logs --service <service-name>
✅ Correct Answer: C

docker service logs <service> aggregates stdout/stderr from ALL replicas of a service. This is far more powerful than docker logs <container> which only shows one container. Essential for debugging distributed applications.

📝 Detailed Explanation
docker service logs vs docker logs — Scope Comparison
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  docker logs <container>     → ONE container                   │
  │  docker service logs <svc>   → ALL replicas (aggregated)       │
  │                                                                 │
  │  EXAMPLE (anihpj-api, 3 replicas on 3 nodes):                   │
  │  $ docker service logs --tail 50 --follow anihpj-api           │
  │                                                                 │
  │  anihpj-api.1@worker1  | [2024-01-15 10:30:01] GET /api/jobs  │
  │  anihpj-api.2@worker2  | [2024-01-15 10:30:02] POST /api/apply│
  │  anihpj-api.3@worker3  | [2024-01-15 10:30:03] GET /api/jobs  │
  │  anihpj-api.1@worker1  | [2024-01-15 10:30:04] 200 OK         │
  │                                                                 │
  │  USEFUL FLAGS:                                                  │
  │  --tail 100       → last 100 lines from each replica           │
  │  --follow         → stream in real-time                        │
  │  --since 10m      → logs from the last 10 minutes              │
  │  --timestamps     → show timestamps                            │
  │  --no-trunc       → don't truncate long lines                  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, tail all API logs during a deployment: docker service logs --tail 100 --follow anihpj-api to watch requests flowing across all replicas in real-time.

💡 DCA Exam Tip: docker service logs works at the service level (all replicas). docker logs works at the container level (one instance). Know the difference.

Orchestration Q18 of 200
What is the Swarm routing mesh?
A) A dedicated load balancer container that must be deployed separately
B) It encrypts all service-to-service communication within the swarm
C) It routes traffic only to containers on the same node
D) Every node listens on the published port and routes to a healthy task — even if the task is on a different node
✅ Correct Answer: D

The routing mesh enables every node in the swarm to accept connections on a published port and forward them to a healthy task — even if no task runs on that node. This eliminates the need for an external load balancer for basic service access.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Swarm Routing Mesh — Any Node Can Serve Any Service
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Service: web, port 80 published, 2 replicas on worker2+worker3 │
  │                                                                 │
  │  Request → worker1:80 (no web task here!)                       │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ worker1 (no web task) → routing mesh → worker2 (web.1) │   │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                 │
  │  Request → worker2:80 (has web.1)                               │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ worker2 → routing mesh → worker2 (web.1) ← LOCAL       │   │
  │  │                        → worker3 (web.2) ← REMOTE      │   │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │  (load balanced across all healthy replicas)                    │
  │                                                                 │
  │  INGRESS MODE (default):                                        │
  │  --publish published=80,target=80                               │
  │  → Every node listens on :80, mesh routes to any healthy task  │
  │                                                                 │
  │  HOST MODE:                                                     │
  │  --publish published=80,target=80,mode=host                     │
  │  → Only nodes running the task listen on :80                   │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, the routing mesh means you can point your DNS to ANY swarm node on port 80 and reach the web service — even if that node has no web container running.

💡 DCA Exam Tip: The routing mesh uses ingress networking (default). Switch to host mode with --publish mode=host to bypass the mesh and bind directly to the host interface.

Orchestration Q19 of 200
A Swarm service has --restart-condition any. What happens if its container exits with code 0?
A) It will be restarted — "any" means restart regardless of exit code
B) It will NOT be restarted — exit code 0 means success
C) It will be restarted only for replicated services
D) The service will be removed from the swarm
✅ Correct Answer: A

--restart-condition any restarts the task regardless of exit code (0 or non-zero). --restart-condition on-failure only restarts on non-zero exit codes. --restart-condition none never restarts.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Swarm Restart Conditions — When Does Docker Restart?
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  RESTART CONDITIONS:                                             │
  ├──────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  none        │  Never restart. Task stays in Shutdown/Failed.   │
  │              │  Use for: Batch jobs, one-time tasks             │
  ├──────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  on-failure  │  Restart ONLY if exit code ≠ 0 (error).          │
  │              │  Use for: Web servers, APIs (default behavior)   │
  ├──────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  any         │  Restart ALWAYS — even on exit code 0.           │
  │              │  Use for: Daemons that should never stop         │
  └──────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  │                                                                 │
  │  COMBINED WITH:                                                 │
  │  --restart-delay 5s        ← wait 5s before restarting         │
  │  --restart-max-attempts 3  ← give up after 3 failures          │
  │  --restart-window 120s     ← reset counter after 2min success  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, use --restart-condition on-failure for the API service so transient errors trigger a restart, but intentional shutdowns (exit 0) don't. Use any for Redis to ensure it's always running.

💡 DCA Exam Tip: Three restart conditions: none (never restart), on-failure (only non-zero exit), any (always restart). Combine with --restart-delay and --restart-max-attempts for backoff behavior.

Orchestration Q20 of 200
How do you constrain a Swarm service to only run on nodes with a specific label?
A) docker service create --label tier=backend nginx
B) docker service create --constraint 'node.labels.tier==backend' nginx
C) docker service create --node-selector tier=backend nginx
D) docker service create --placement 'node.tier=backend' nginx
✅ Correct Answer: B

--constraint 'node.labels.<key>==<value>' restricts service placement to nodes matching the label. First add the label: docker node update --label-add tier=backend worker1. Constraints work with == (equals) and != (not equals).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Placement Constraints — Directing Services to Specific Nodes
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ docker node update --label-add tier=backend worker1          │
  │  $ docker node update --label-add tier=backend worker2          │
  │  $ docker node update --label-add tier=frontend worker3         │
  │                                                                 │
  │  $ docker service create \                                      │
  │      --constraint 'node.labels.tier==backend' \                 │
  │      --name api myapi                                           │
  │                                                                 │
  │  RESULT: api tasks ONLY land on worker1 and worker2             │
  │                                                                 │
  │  ADVANCED CONSTRAINTS:                                          │
  │  --constraint 'node.role==manager'    ← only manager nodes      │
  │  --constraint 'node.hostname==db-1'   ← specific host           │
  │  --constraint 'node.labels.ssd==true' ← nodes with SSD          │
  │  --constraint 'node.labels.region!=us-east' ← exclude region    │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, constrain the PostgreSQL service to nodes labeled tier=database with SSD storage for performance, and the API service to tier=backend nodes.

💡 DCA Exam Tip: Placement constraints use the node.labels prefix. Know both == (equals) and != (not equals) operators. Constraints are evaluated at scheduling time — changing labels won't move already-running tasks.

Orchestration Q23 of 200
What command lists all running stacks in a Docker Swarm?
A) docker stack ls
B) docker swarm stack list
C) docker service ls --stacks
D) docker compose ls --swarm
✅ Correct Answer: A

docker stack ls lists all deployed stacks with their service count and orchestrator. Use docker stack services <stack> to see services within a stack, and docker stack ps <stack> to see all tasks across all services in that stack.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Stack Management Commands
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ docker stack ls                                              │
  │  NAME      SERVICES   ORCHESTRATOR                              │
  │  anihpj    4          Swarm                                     │
  │  monitoring 2          Swarm                                     │
  │                                                                 │
  │  $ docker stack services anihpj                                 │
  │  ID         NAME          MODE        REPLICAS  IMAGE            │
  │  abc123     anihpj_web    replicated  3/3       nginx:alpine     │
  │  def456     anihpj_api    replicated  5/5       anihpj-api:v1    │
  │  ghi789     anihpj_db     replicated  1/1       postgres:15      │
  │  jkl012     anihpj_redis  replicated  1/1       redis:7-alpine   │
  │                                                                 │
  │  $ docker stack ps anihpj                                       │
  │  (shows EVERY task across ALL services in the stack)            │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: Stack commands: deploy, ls, services, ps, rm. All prefixed with docker stack. Know the nesting: stack → services → tasks.

Orchestration Q24 of 200
Which section in docker-compose.yml is ONLY used by docker stack deploy (ignored by docker compose up)?
A) volumes:
B) environment:
C) ports:
D) deploy:
✅ Correct Answer: D

The deploy: section defines Swarm-specific settings: replicas, update_config, restart_policy, resources, placement constraints. docker compose up silently ignores it — making it the bridge between development (Compose) and production (Swarm) in a single file.

📝 Detailed Explanation
deploy: Section — Compose vs Swarm Fork
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  docker-compose.yml — ONE file, TWO runtimes:                    │
  │                                                                 │
  │  services:                                                      │
  │    anihpj-api:                                                  │
  │      image: anihpj-api:v2                                       │
  │      ports: ["8080:8000"]              ← Both respect this      │
  │      environment: ["DEBUG=true"]       ← Both respect this      │
  │      deploy:                           ← SWARM ONLY             │
  │        replicas: 3                                              │
  │        update_config:                                           │
  │          parallelism: 1                                         │
  │          delay: 10s                                             │
  │        restart_policy:                                          │
  │          condition: on-failure                                  │
  │        resources:                                               │
  │          limits: {cpus: '1', memory: 512M}                      │
  │          reservations: {cpus: '0.5', memory: 256M}              │
  │        placement:                                               │
  │          constraints: [node.role == worker]                     │
  │                                                                 │
  │  $ docker compose up      → deploy: section IGNORED            │
  │  $ docker stack deploy -c → deploy: section ACTIVE             │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For the anihpj/jobpost stack, you'd use the deploy: section to define 3 replicas of the Django API, rolling updates with 10s delay, and resource limits of 512MB per task — all ignored in local dev with docker compose up.

💡 DCA Exam Tip: The deploy: section is the bridge between Compose (dev) and Swarm (prod). docker compose up ignores it; docker stack deploy enforces it. Also: build: is ignored by stack deploy (use pre-built images).

Orchestration Q25 of 200
How do you store a non-sensitive config file (like nginx.conf) for Swarm services?
A) docker secret create nginx-conf ./nginx.conf
B) docker volume create --config nginx-conf ./nginx.conf
C) docker config create nginx-conf ./nginx.conf
D) docker swarm config add nginx-conf ./nginx.conf
✅ Correct Answer: C

docker config create <name> <file> stores a configuration file in the Swarm's encrypted Raft log. Services can mount it at /<config-name>. Configs are for non-sensitive data (nginx.conf, app settings). Use docker secret for passwords and keys.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Configs vs Secrets — When to Use Which
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  CONFIGS (non-sensitive):                                       │
  │  $ echo 'server { listen 80; }' > nginx.conf                    │
  │  $ docker config create nginx-conf ./nginx.conf                 │
  │  $ docker service create --config src=nginx-conf,target=/etc/..│
  │  → Mounted at /nginx-conf inside container                     │
  │  → Max 500KB per config                                        │
  │                                                                 │
  │  SECRETS (sensitive):                                           │
  │  $ echo 'secret123' > db-password.txt                           │
  │  $ docker secret create db-pass ./db-password.txt               │
  │  $ docker service create --secret db-pass                      │
  │  → Mounted at /run/secrets/db-pass                             │
  │  → Max 500KB per secret                                        │
  │                                                                 │
  │  BOTH: encrypted at rest (Raft log) + encrypted in transit (TLS)│
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: docker config = non-sensitive (nginx.conf, app config). docker secret = sensitive (passwords, API keys, TLS certs). Both are encrypted at rest in the Raft log and encrypted in transit to workers.

1.4 Kubernetes Basics (Docker User Bridge)Q29-Q36

Orchestration Q29 of 200
What is a Pod in Kubernetes?
A) A Pod is exactly the same as a Docker container
B) A Pod is a virtual machine that runs containers
C) A Pod is Kubernetes' term for a Swarm service
D) The smallest deployable unit — one or more containers sharing network namespace and storage
✅ Correct Answer: D

A Pod is Kubernetes' atomic scheduling unit — the smallest deployable object. Unlike Docker where a container IS the atomic unit, a K8s Pod can contain multiple containers that share the same network namespace (localhost), IPC namespace, and volumes. Most Pods run a single container (the "one-container-per-Pod" pattern), but sidecar patterns (e.g., logging agent + app) use multi-container Pods.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Docker Container vs Kubernetes Pod — Atomic Units
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  DOCKER (Swarm):                    KUBERNETES:                  │
  │  ┌──────────────────┐              ┌──────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │   Container       │              │   POD (shared context)    │ │
  │  │   • Own IP        │              │  ┌─────────┐ ┌────────┐  │ │
  │  │   • Own namespace │              │  │ App     │ │Sidecar │  │ │
  │  │   • Atomic unit   │              │  │Container│ │Container│  │ │
  │  └──────────────────┘              │  └────┬────┘ └───┬────┘  │ │
  │                                     │       │share     │share  │ │
  │  Swarm schedules:                  │  localhost:same  volumes  │ │
  │  individual containers              │       │         │        │ │
  │  as tasks                           │  ┌────┴─────────┴────┐   │ │
  │                                     │  │  Pod IP: 10.244.1.5│   │ │
  │                                     │  └───────────────────┘   │ │
  │                                     └──────────────────────────┘ │
  │                                                                 │
  │  Docker: atomic = container        K8s: atomic = Pod            │
  │  Swarm task ≈ K8s Pod              Service replicas ≈ Deployment│
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For the anihpj/jobpost Django app, you could run the web server and a Cloud SQL proxy as two containers in one Pod — they'd communicate via localhost without exposing the proxy externally.

💡 DCA Exam Tip: Docker → K8s mapping: container → Pod, service create → Deployment, service ps → get pods, service logs → logs deployment/name. A Pod is NOT a container — it's a wrapper that can hold multiple containers sharing network/storage.

Orchestration Q30 of 200
What is the Kubernetes equivalent of docker service create --replicas 3 nginx?
A) kubectl run nginx --replicas=3 --image=nginx
B) kubectl create deployment nginx --image=nginx --replicas=3
C) kubectl pod create nginx --count=3
D) kubectl service deploy nginx --replicas=3
✅ Correct Answer: B

kubectl create deployment creates a Deployment controller that manages a ReplicaSet, which in turn manages replicated Pods. This is the direct equivalent of a Swarm replicated service. The Deployment ensures the desired number of identical Pods are always running — just like docker service create --replicas 3.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Swarm Service ↔ Kubernetes Deployment — Side-by-Side Mapping
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  SWARM:                          KUBERNETES:                    │
  │  ┌────────────────────┐          ┌──────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ docker service     │          │ kubectl create deployment│  │
  │  │ create nginx       │   ⬌      │ nginx --image=nginx      │  │
  │  │ --replicas 3       │          │ --replicas=3             │  │
  │  └────────┬───────────┘          └───────────┬──────────────┘  │
  │           │                                  │                  │
  │           ▼                                  ▼                  │
  │  ┌────────────────────┐          ┌──────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │  Service (nginx)   │          │  Deployment (nginx)      │  │
  │  │  ├─ Task 1 (node1) │          │  ├─ ReplicaSet           │  │
  │  │  ├─ Task 2 (node2) │          │  │  ├─ Pod-abc (node1)   │  │
  │  │  └─ Task 3 (node3) │          │  │  ├─ Pod-def (node2)   │  │
  │  └────────────────────┘          │  │  └─ Pod-ghi (node3)   │  │
  │                                   │  └───────────────────────│  │
  │  COMMANDS:                        │  COMMANDS:               │  │
  │  service create  ────►           │  create deployment ───► │  │
  │  service scale    ────►           │  scale deployment ────► │  │
  │  service update   ────►           │  set image/apply ─────► │  │
  │  service ps       ────►           │  get pods ────────────► │  │
  │  service logs     ────►           │  logs deployment/name ─►│  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For the anihpj/jobpost stack, migrating from Swarm to K8s means: docker service create anihpj-api --replicas 3 becomes kubectl create deployment anihpj-api --image=anihpj-api:v2 --replicas=3.

💡 DCA Exam Tip: Key mapping: docker service create → kubectl create deployment, docker service scale → kubectl scale deployment, docker stack deploy → kubectl apply -f. The Deployment is the Swarm service equivalent.

Orchestration Q31 of 200
In Kubernetes, what does a Service provide?
A) Application code execution like a Docker container
B) Persistent storage like a Docker volume
C) Stable networking (DNS + IP) to a set of Pods — similar to Swarm's routing mesh
D) Multi-container orchestration like Docker Compose
