✅ Correct Answer: C

A Kubernetes Service provides stable networking to a dynamic set of Pods. Pods are ephemeral (IPs change on restart), but a Service gives them a fixed ClusterIP and DNS name. It acts as a layer-4 load balancer, selecting Pods by labels — exactly like Swarm's routing mesh + VIP combined.

📝 Detailed Explanation
K8s Service ≡ Swarm Routing Mesh + VIP
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  SWARM:                          KUBERNETES:                    │
  │  ┌────────────────────┐          ┌──────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ Service VIP +      │          │ Service (ClusterIP)      │  │
  │  │ Routing Mesh        │   ⬌      │ • Stable IP: 10.96.0.1   │  │
  │  │                    │          │ • DNS: api.default.svc  │  │
  │  │ Client → VIP        │          │ • Selector: app=api      │  │
  │  │   → any node        │          │   → Pods with app=api    │  │
  │  │   → task on any node│          │ • Types:                 │  │
  │  └────────────────────┘          │   ClusterIP  (internal)  │  │
  │                                   │   NodePort   (external)  │  │
  │  Both provide:                   │   LoadBalancer(cloud)    │  │
  │  • Stable endpoint for dynamic   └──────────────────────────┘  │
  │    backends (tasks/Pods)                                        │
  │  • Load balancing across replicas                              │
  │  • DNS-based service discovery                                 │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For the anihpj/jobpost app, the Django API Service exposes port 8000 internally via ClusterIP, while the web frontend uses a LoadBalancer Service to expose port 80 externally — just like Swarm published ports.

💡 DCA Exam Tip: K8s Service ≈ Swarm routing mesh + service VIP. Both provide stable endpoints for dynamic backends. Know the three Service types: ClusterIP (internal only), NodePort (node IP:port), LoadBalancer (cloud LB).

Orchestration Q32 of 200
What does kubectl apply -f deployment.yaml do?
A) Creates or updates resources declaratively — equivalent to docker stack deploy
B) Only creates new resources — fails if the resource exists
C) Validates YAML syntax without applying changes
D) Converts YAML to a Docker Compose file and deploys it
✅ Correct Answer: A

kubectl apply -f is declarative: it creates resources if they don't exist or patches them to match the desired state in the YAML. This is the Kubernetes equivalent of docker stack deploy -c — both are idempotent convergence commands that reconcile actual state toward desired state.

📝 Detailed Explanation
kubectl apply vs kubectl create — Declarative vs Imperative
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  IMPERATIVE (kubectl create):        DECLARATIVE (kubectl apply):│
  │  ┌────────────────────────────┐     ┌──────────────────────────┐│
  │  │ $ kubectl create -f dep.yml│     │ $ kubectl apply -f dep.yml││
  │  │ → Creates resource         │     │ → Creates IF new          ││
  │  │ → FAILS if already exists  │     │ → UPDATES IF exists       ││
  │  │ → No merging               │     │ → 3-way merge patch       ││
  │  └────────────────────────────┘     └──────────────────────────┘│
  │                                                                 │
  │  SWARM EQUIVALENT:                  K8s APPLY (CI/CD friendly): │
  │  $ docker stack deploy -c ...       $ kubectl apply -f ./k8s/   │
  │  Both are idempotent!               Run it every deploy!        │
  │                                                                 │
  │  kubectl apply tracks last-applied-configuration annotation     │
  │  to detect what changed between the file and the live object    │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For the anihpj/jobpost stack, you'd store all K8s YAML in a ./k8s/ directory (deployment.yaml, service.yaml, configmap.yaml) and run kubectl apply -f ./k8s/ on every CI/CD deploy — just like docker stack deploy -c stack.yml anihpj.

💡 DCA Exam Tip: kubectl apply (declarative, idempotent) vs kubectl create (imperative, fails if exists). apply is the CI/CD-friendly choice. Equivalent to docker stack deploy.

1.5 Swarm Advanced OperationsQ37-Q44

Orchestration Q37 of 200
What command updates a Swarm node's availability to prevent it from receiving new tasks while keeping existing tasks running?
A) docker node update --availability drain worker-1
B) docker node pause worker-1
C) docker node update --availability pause worker-1
D) docker node update --state paused worker-1
✅ Correct Answer: C

--availability pause keeps existing tasks running but blocks the scheduler from assigning new tasks to the node. This is ideal for maintenance preparation — you drain traffic gradually. --availability drain moves ALL tasks off the node (more disruptive). --availability active is the normal operating state.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Node Availability States — Active, Pause, Drain
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ACTIVE (default):             PAUSE (maintenance prep):         │
  │  ┌──────────────────┐         ┌──────────────────────────┐     │
  │  │ Node: worker-1   │         │ $ docker node update \. │     │
  │  │ [Task1] [Task2]  │         │   --availability pause   │     │
  │  │ [Task3] ← NEW    │         │   worker-1               │     │
  │  │ Allowed: ✅ run  │         │                          │     │
  │  │          ✅ new  │         │ [Task1] [Task2]          │     │
  │  └──────────────────┘         │ [Task3] ❌ NOT placed    │     │
  │                                │ Existing tasks: ✅ stay  │     │
  │  DRAIN (decommission):         │ New tasks:      ❌ blocked│     │
  │  ┌──────────────────┐         └──────────────────────────┘     │
  │  │ $ docker node ...│                                           │
  │  │   --availability │         USE CASES:                        │
  │  │   drain worker-1 │         • Pause: pre-maintenance, gradual │
  │  │                  │           traffic shift                   │
  │  │ [Task1] → node2  │         • Drain: decommission, replace    │
  │  │ [Task2] → node3  │         • Active: normal operation        │
  │  │ All tasks MOVED  │                                           │
  │  └──────────────────┘                                           │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For the anihpj/jobpost stack, if worker-1 is running your Django API tasks and you need to apply security patches, you'd first pause it (no new tasks), monitor, then drain it to move all tasks elsewhere before rebooting.

💡 DCA Exam Tip: Active = normal, Pause = no new tasks (existing stay), Drain = all tasks moved elsewhere. Three availability states to know. Pause is for gradual maintenance; Drain is for decommissioning.

Orchestration Q38 of 200
Where does Docker Swarm store its cluster state (Raft logs)?
A) On manager nodes, at /var/lib/docker/swarm/
B) In an external etcd cluster
C) In the Docker Hub cloud service
D) In a shared NFS volume mounted by all nodes
✅ Correct Answer: A

Docker Swarm stores its Raft logs, TLS keys, and cluster state in /var/lib/docker/swarm/ on each manager node. Backing up this directory is your disaster recovery strategy.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Swarm State — Where It Lives and How to Backup
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  SWARM STATE LOCATION: /var/lib/docker/swarm/                   │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ /var/lib/docker/swarm/                                  │   │
  │  │ ├── raft/          ← Raft logs (cluster state)          │   │
  │  │ ├── certificates/  ← TLS certs for node communication   │   │
  │  │ ├── worker/        ← Worker-specific state              │   │
  │  │ └── docker-state.json ← Local daemon state              │   │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                 │
  │  BACKUP PROCEDURE:                                              │
  │  1. docker swarm update --autolock=true (enable encryption)     │
  │  2. systemctl stop docker (on ONE manager only)                 │
  │  3. tar czf swarm-backup.tar.gz /var/lib/docker/swarm/          │
  │  4. systemctl start docker                                      │
  │                                                                 │
  │  ⚠️  Unlike K8s (which has etcdctl snapshot), Swarm has NO       │
  │     built-in backup command — it's a manual file copy.          │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: Unlike Kubernetes (which uses external etcd), Swarm's Raft store is built into the Docker daemon. No separate etcd cluster needed. Backup is manual file copy.

Orchestration Q39 of 200
What does docker service update --force api do?
A) Deletes and recreates the service
B) Scales the service up by 1 replica temporarily
C) Forces the service to restart only on the manager node
D) Forces a rolling restart of all tasks without changing the service definition
✅ Correct Answer: D

--force triggers a rolling restart of all replicas without changing image, ports, or any other settings. Useful when you need containers to pick up new ConfigMap/Secret contents or restart after a node issue.

📝 Detailed Explanation
docker service update --force — Rolling Restart
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ docker service update --force api                            │
  │                                                                 │
  │  BEFORE:  api.1(v1)  api.2(v1)  api.3(v1)  api.4(v1)  api.5(v1)│
  │                                                                 │
  │  DURING (rolling, 1 at a time):                                 │
  │  t=0s   ░░░░  api.2(v1)  api.3(v1)  api.4(v1)  api.5(v1)      │
  │  t=5s   api.1(v1)  ░░░░  api.3(v1)  api.4(v1)  api.5(v1)      │
  │  t=10s  api.1(v1)  api.2(v1)  ░░░░  api.4(v1)  api.5(v1)      │
  │  t=25s  api.1(v1)  api.2(v1)  api.3(v1)  api.4(v1)  api.5(v1)  │
  │  ░░░░ = restarting (same image, same config)                    │
  │                                                                 │
  │  WHEN TO USE:                                                   │
  │  • After updating a docker config or secret content            │
  │  • After a node issue (to reschedule)                          │
  │  • To clear memory leaks (force fresh process start)           │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: --force is your quick "restart everything" button. Unlike Kubernetes, Swarm services don't automatically restart when configs/secrets change — use --force to trigger it.

Orchestration Q40 of 200
What does the --update-failure-action flag control during a service update?
A) Whether to send an email alert on failure
B) What happens when a task fails to update — pause, continue, or rollback
C) How many times to retry pulling a broken image
D) Whether to log the failure to syslog or journald
✅ Correct Answer: B

Values are pause (stop the update, keep running tasks), continue (keep updating remaining tasks despite failures), and rollback (automatically revert to previous version).

📝 Detailed Explanation
update-failure-action — Three Strategies
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  --update-failure-action pause (DEFAULT)                        │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ Task 3 of 5 fails → update STOPS                        │   │
  │  │ ████ ████ ❌ ████ ████  (2 updated, 2 old, 1 broken)    │   │
  │  │ Operator investigates, then resumes or rolls back       │   │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                 │
  │  --update-failure-action continue                               │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ Task 3 fails → update CONTINUES anyway                  │   │
  │  │ ████ ████ ████ ████ ████  (all updated, some broken)    │   │
  │  │ Use for: canary testing, non-critical workloads         │   │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                 │
  │  --update-failure-action rollback (RECOMMENDED FOR PROD)        │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ Task 3 fails → AUTOMATIC ROLLBACK to previous version   │   │
  │  │ v1.0 ████ ████ ████ ████ ████  (all restored!)          │   │
  │  │ Zero human intervention needed. Safe for production.    │   │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: Production best practice: set --update-failure-action rollback so bad deployments automatically revert without human intervention.

1.6 Orchestration in AWSQ45-Q50

Orchestration Q45 of 200
What AWS service is the managed container orchestration platform that supports both EC2 and Fargate launch types?
A) AWS Lambda
B) AWS Batch
C) Amazon ECS (Elastic Container Service)
D) AWS CodeDeploy
✅ Correct Answer: C

Amazon ECS (Elastic Container Service) is AWS's native container orchestration service. It supports two launch types: EC2 (you manage the instances, more control) and Fargate (serverless, no instance management). ECS is the AWS equivalent of Docker Swarm for production workloads, with deep integration into the AWS ecosystem (ALB, IAM, CloudWatch, VPC).

📝 Detailed Explanation
AWS Container Services — ECS, EKS, Fargate Comparison
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  AWS CONTAINER ECOSYSTEM (DCA Exam Coverage):                    │
  │                                                                 │
  │  ┌──────────────┬────────────────────┬──────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ SERVICE      │ WHAT IT DOES        │ DOCKER ANALOGY       │  │
  │  ├──────────────┼────────────────────┼──────────────────────┤  │
  │  │ ECS          │ Managed orchestration│ Docker Swarm (AWS)  │  │
  │  │ EKS          │ Managed Kubernetes  │ Kubernetes (AWS)     │  │
  │  │ ECR          │ Managed registry    │ Docker Hub (private) │  │
  │  │ Fargate      │ Serverless compute  │ Swarm + no EC2 mgmt  │  │
  │  │ App Runner   │ Container→web app   │ docker run as a svc  │  │
  │  └──────────────┴────────────────────┴──────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                 │
  │  ECS LAUNCH TYPES:                                              │
  │  ┌──────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ EC2 Launch Type      │ Fargate Launch Type               │   │
  │  ├──────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤   │
  │  │ • You manage EC2s    │ • No instance management         │   │
  │  │ • Pay per instance   │ • Pay per task (vCPU+memory)     │   │
  │  │ • More control       │ • Simpler operations             │   │
  │  │ • GPU, EBS, ENI      │ • Limited customization          │   │
  │  └──────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────┘   │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For the anihpj/jobpost stack, you could deploy your Django API on ECS Fargate (serverless, no instance patching), Postgres on RDS, and Redis on ElastiCache — fully managed AWS-native architecture.

💡 DCA Exam Tip: The DCA covers Docker on AWS. Know ECS (managed orchestration), ECR (managed registry), EKS (managed Kubernetes), and Fargate (serverless compute). ECS = Swarm equivalent; EKS = K8s equivalent; Fargate = no instance management.

Orchestration Q46 of 200
What is Amazon ECR?
A) A fully managed Docker container registry integrated with AWS IAM
B) A compute service for running containers without managing servers
C) A managed Kubernetes control plane
D) A CI/CD service for building Docker images
✅ Correct Answer: A

Amazon ECR (Elastic Container Registry) is a fully managed, private Docker container registry deeply integrated with AWS IAM for authentication and authorization. It supports image scanning for vulnerabilities (via Clair), immutable tags, lifecycle policies, and cross-region/cross-account replication. Login uses temporary tokens obtained via the AWS CLI.

📝 Detailed Explanation
ECR Deep Dive — Registry Features & Authentication
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  AMAZON ECR — Fully Managed Private Registry                     │
  │                                                                 │
  │  KEY FEATURES:                                                  │
  │  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ • IAM-based auth (no separate credentials needed)          │ │
  │  │ • Image vulnerability scanning (Clair engine)             │ │
  │  │ • Immutable tags (prevent overwrites)                     │ │
  │  │ • Lifecycle policies (auto-delete old images)             │ │
  │  │ • Cross-region & cross-account replication                │ │
  │  │ • VPC endpoints (private access, no internet)             │ │
  │  │ • SBOM export (Software Bill of Materials)                │ │
  │  │ • Repository per image (naming: anihpj/api, anihpj/web)   │ │
  │  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │                                                                 │
  │  ECR vs DOCKER HUB:                                             │
  │  ┌─────────────────┬──────────────────┬─────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ FEATURE         │ ECR              │ DOCKER HUB          │  │
  │  ├─────────────────┼──────────────────┼─────────────────────┤  │
  │  │ Auth            │ AWS IAM (temp)   │ Username/Password   │  │
  │  │ Scanning        │ Built-in (Clair) │ Hub Scout           │  │
  │  │ Private repos   │ Unlimited*        │ 1 free private       │  │
  │  │ Replication     │ Cross-region     │ Hub registry        │  │
  │  │ VPC private     │ Yes (endpoints)  │ No                  │  │
  │  └─────────────────┴──────────────────┴─────────────────────┘  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For the anihpj/jobpost CI/CD pipeline, you'd push built images to ECR: docker tag anihpj-api:v2 123456789.dkr.ecr.us-east-1.amazonaws.com/anihpj/api:v2, then ECS pulls from ECR automatically.

💡 DCA Exam Tip: ECR integrates with IAM for access control, supports image vulnerability scanning, and uses temporary login tokens (12-hour validity). ECR = private Docker registry in AWS; ECS/EKS pull from it automatically.

Orchestration Q26 of 200
How do you remove a stack and all its services from the swarm?
A) docker compose down --swarm mystack
B) docker stack rm mystack
C) docker swarm stack-delete mystack
D) docker service rm --stack mystack
✅ Correct Answer: B

docker stack rm <stack> removes all services, networks, and secrets associated with the stack. Note: volumes, configs, and secrets are NOT removed — you must clean those up separately to avoid orphaned resources.

📝 Detailed Explanation
docker stack rm — What Gets Removed vs What Survives
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ docker stack rm anihpj                                       │
  │                                                                 │
  │  ✅ REMOVED (automatically):        ❌ SURVIVES (manual cleanup):│
  │  ┌──────────────────────────┐      ┌──────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ • All services           │      │ • Named volumes           │ │
  │  │ • All overlay networks   │      │ • Configs (docker config) │ │
  │  │ • Stack-internal secrets │      │ • Externally ref'd secrets│ │
  │  │ • DNS entries            │      │ • Published ports released│ │
  │  └──────────────────────────┘      └──────────────────────────┘ │
  │                                                                 │
  │  FULL CLEANUP SEQUENCE:                                         │
  │  $ docker stack rm anihpj                                       │
  │  $ docker volume rm anihpj_postgres_data                        │
  │  $ docker config rm anihpj_nginx-conf                           │
  │  $ docker secret rm anihpj_db-pass                              │
  │  $ docker network prune  (removes orphaned overlay networks)    │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For the anihpj/jobpost stack, after docker stack rm anihpj, your Postgres data volume still exists — running docker stack deploy -c stack.yml anihpj again will re-attach to it, preserving your database.

💡 DCA Exam Tip: docker stack rm removes services and networks but NOT volumes, configs, or secrets. Use docker volume prune and docker config rm separately. This is intentional — data volumes survive stack teardowns.

Orchestration Q27 of 200
In a docker-compose.yml used with docker stack deploy, how do you reference a Swarm secret?
A) secrets: section at top level + secrets: reference under each service
B) environment: with DOCKER_SECRET_ prefix
C) volumes: with secret driver
D) deploy: secrets: inline
✅ Correct Answer: A

Secrets must be declared at the top-level secrets: key in the Compose file, then referenced in each service. The secret must already exist: docker secret create db-pass ./password.txt before deploying.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Secrets in Stack Files — External References
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  docker-stack.yml:                                              │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ version: "3.8"                                         │   │
  │  │ services:                                              │   │
  │  │   db:                                                  │   │
  │  │     image: postgres:15                                 │   │
  │  │     secrets:                                           │   │
  │  │       - db-pass          ← references top-level        │   │
  │  │     environment:                                       │   │
  │  │       POSTGRES_PASSWORD_FILE: /run/secrets/db-pass     │   │
  │  │                                                       │   │
  │  │ secrets:                   ← TOP-LEVEL declaration     │   │
  │  │   db-pass:                                             │   │
  │  │     external: true         ← must already exist!       │   │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                 │
  │  BEFORE deploying the stack:                                    │
  │  $ docker secret create db-pass ./password.txt                  │
  │  $ docker stack deploy -c docker-stack.yml anihpj               │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: Stack deploy doesn't create secrets from files — they must be pre-created with docker secret create. Use external secrets in compose: secrets: db-pass: external: true.

Orchestration Q28 of 200
What happens when you run docker stack deploy again with an updated Compose file?
A) It fails — stack names must be unique
B) It creates a second stack with a numeric suffix
C) It deletes the old stack and creates a new one
D) It performs a rolling update — services are updated in-place without downtime
✅ Correct Answer: D

Re-deploying a stack with the same name performs a rolling update. Services that changed are updated; new services are added; removed services are deleted. This idempotent behavior is how you safely update production stacks without downtime.

📝 Detailed Explanation
docker stack deploy — Idempotent Convergence
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  INITIAL DEPLOY:                                                │
  │  $ docker stack deploy -c stack-v1.yml anihpj                   │
  │  → Creates: web (2 reps), api (3 reps), db (1 rep)              │
  │                                                                 │
  │  UPDATE (edit stack-v2.yml: add redis, change api image):       │
  │  $ docker stack deploy -c stack-v2.yml anihpj                   │
  │                                                                 │
  │  Swarm converges to desired state:                              │
  │  ┌──────────────────┬──────────────────┬──────────────────┐    │
  │  │ SERVICE  │ STATUS │ ACTION                              │    │
  │  ├──────────┼────────┼─────────────────────────────────────┤    │
  │  │ web      │ unchanged│ No action (same spec)              │    │
  │  │ api      │ CHANGED │ Rolling update (image v1→v2)       │    │
  │  │ db       │ unchanged│ No action (same spec)              │    │
  │  │ redis    │ NEW     │ Created with defined replicas      │    │
  │  │ (removed)│ DELETED │ Any service not in new file removed│    │
  │  └──────────────────┴──────────────────┴──────────────────┘    │
  │                                                                 │
  │  KEY: Same stack name = convergent update. No downtime.         │
  │  Different stack name = separate stack (no conflict).           │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For the anihpj/jobpost stack in production, you'd version your Compose file (stack-v1.yml → stack-v2.yml) and re-deploy with the same stack name. Changed services (e.g., new Django image) get rolling updates; unchanged services (Postgres) stay untouched.

💡 DCA Exam Tip: docker stack deploy is idempotent — run it as many times as you want with the same stack name. It converges to the desired state. Changed services get rolling updates; unchanged are untouched; removed are deleted.

Orchestration Q33 of 200
What Kubernetes resource type is equivalent to a Docker Compose file for deploying an entire application stack?
A) Pod
B) ConfigMap
C) A collection of YAML manifests (Deployments + Services + ConfigMaps) applied together
D) Dockerfile
✅ Correct Answer: C

Kubernetes doesn't have a single "stack" concept like Docker. Instead, you define multiple resources (Deployment, Service, ConfigMap, Secret, Ingress) in one or more YAML files and apply them together: kubectl apply -f ./k8s/. Helm charts further package these into reusable, templated bundles.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Docker Compose vs Kubernetes Manifests — Stack Equivalents
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  DOCKER (one file):                 KUBERNETES (multiple files): │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────┐       ┌──────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ docker-compose.yml      │       │ k8s/                     │ │
  │  │                         │       │ ├── deployment.yaml      │ │
  │  │ services:               │       │ ├── service.yaml         │ │
  │  │   web:  ...             │ ═══►  │ ├── configmap.yaml      │ │
  │  │   api:  ...             │       │ ├── secret.yaml          │ │
  │  │   db:   ...             │       │ ├── ingress.yaml         │ │
  │  │   redis: ...            │       │ └── pvc.yaml             │ │
  │  └─────────────────────────┘       └──────────────────────────┘ │
  │                                                                 │
  │  $ docker stack deploy -c \.     $ kubectl apply -f ./k8s/     │
  │                                                                 │
  │  HELM (K8s package manager):       One chart ≈ one docker-compose│
  │  $ helm install anihpj ./chart/    (templated, versioned, shared)│
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For the anihpj/jobpost stack, you'd create separate YAML files for the Django deployment, Postgres statefulset, Redis deployment, and their respective services/configs — then apply them as a group with one command.

💡 DCA Exam Tip: Helm is the Kubernetes equivalent of Docker stacks — it packages multiple resources into a deployable "chart". Know that Helm exists for the DCA exam and that kubectl apply -f on a directory applies all YAML files.

Orchestration Q34 of 200
How do you view logs from a specific container in a multi-container Kubernetes Pod?
A) kubectl logs <pod> -c <container-name>
B) kubectl container logs <container-name>
C) kubectl exec <pod> -- docker logs
D) kubectl describe pod <pod> --logs
✅ Correct Answer: A

kubectl logs <pod> -c <container-name> specifies which container in a multi-container Pod to fetch logs from. If the Pod has only one container, the -c flag is optional — exactly like docker logs <container> for single-container scenarios.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Logs: Docker vs Kubernetes — Container Targeting
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  DOCKER:                          KUBERNETES:                   │
  │  ┌────────────────────────┐       ┌──────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ docker logs web-01     │       │ kubectl logs web-pod-abc │ │
  │  │ (one container = easy) │       │ (single container Pod)   │ │
  │  └────────────────────────┘       └──────────────────────────┘ │
  │                                                                 │
  │  MULTI-CONTAINER POD (sidecar):                                 │
  │  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ POD: web-pod-abc                                          │ │
  │  │ ┌─────────────┐  ┌─────────────┐                          │ │
  │  │ │ Container:   │  │ Container:  │                          │ │
  │  │ │ nginx (web)  │  │ filebeat    │                          │ │
  │  │ │              │  │ (log shipper│                          │ │
  │  │ └─────────────┘  └─────────────┘                          │ │
  │  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │                                                                 │
  │  $ kubectl logs web-pod-abc              → ERROR (ambiguous)   │
  │  $ kubectl logs web-pod-abc -c nginx     → nginx logs          │
  │  $ kubectl logs web-pod-abc -c filebeat  → filebeat logs       │
  │                                                                 │
  │  COMMON FLAGS (same in Docker & K8s):                           │
  │  --tail=100  --follow (-f)  --since=1h  --timestamps           │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For the anihpj/jobpost Django app, if you run an nginx sidecar alongside the Django container in the same Pod, you'd use kubectl logs django-pod-xyz -c django for app logs and -c nginx for access logs.

💡 DCA Exam Tip: Docker: docker logs <container>. K8s: kubectl logs <pod> -c <container>. Both share --tail, --follow, --since flags. The -c flag is mandatory for multi-container Pods.

Orchestration Q35 of 200
What is the primary advantage of Kubernetes over Docker Swarm for large enterprise deployments?
A) Swarm is faster and simpler — Kubernetes has no advantage
B) Kubernetes has a built-in container registry
C) Kubernetes doesn't require any networking configuration
D) Kubernetes has a richer ecosystem: autoscaling (HPA/VPA), operators, CRDs, service meshes, and broader cloud provider support
✅ Correct Answer: D

Kubernetes' extensibility (CRDs, operators, admission webhooks) and ecosystem (Helm, Istio, Prometheus, ArgoCD) make it the standard for large-scale deployments. Swarm is simpler but less extensible.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Docker Swarm vs Kubernetes — When to Use Which
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  CHOOSE SWARM WHEN:                          CHOOSE K8s WHEN:   │
  │  • Small-medium teams (<50 services)          • Large orgs (50+) │
  │  │ • Simple deployments needed               • Complex workflows │
  │  │ • Tight Docker integration                • Service mesh needed│
  │  │ • Learning curve matters                  • Autoscaling needed│
  │  │ • Single cloud/on-prem                    • Multi-cloud needed│
  │  │ • Quick to set up (1 command)             • GitOps (Flux/Argo)│
  │                                                                 │
  │  SWARM: Built into Docker daemon, no extra install               │
  │  K8s:   Separate control plane, richer but more complex          │
  │                                                                 │
  │  DCA EXAM: You need to know BOTH — exam covers Swarm primarily   │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: The DCA exam expects you to understand when to use Docker Swarm (simplicity, tight Docker integration) vs Kubernetes (extensibility, ecosystem). Know the strengths of each.

Orchestration Q36 of 200
What does kubectl get all show?
A) Every resource in the entire cluster including cluster-level objects
B) Common workload resources: pods, services, deployments, replicasets — but NOT configmaps, secrets, or cluster-level resources
C) Only pods running in the current namespace
D) All Docker containers running on all nodes
✅ Correct Answer: B

kubectl get all shows pods, services, deployments, and replicasets — the "core" workload resources. Despite its misleading name, it does NOT show configmaps, secrets, ingresses, daemonsets, statefulsets, jobs, cronjobs, or cluster-level objects (nodes, namespaces, PVs).

📝 Detailed Explanation
kubectl get all — What It Actually Shows
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ kubectl get all                                              │
  │                                                                 │
  │  ✅ SHOWN (workload basics):        ❌ NOT SHOWN (hidden from all):│
  │  ┌──────────────────────────┐      ┌──────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ • pods                   │      │ • configmaps             │ │
  │  │ • services               │      │ • secrets                │ │
  │  │ • deployments            │      │ • ingresses              │ │
  │  │ • replicasets            │      │ • daemonsets             │ │
  │  └──────────────────────────┘      │ • statefulsets           │ │
  │                                     │ • jobs / cronjobs        │ │
  │  SWARM EQUIVALENT:                  │ • nodes                  │ │
  │  $ docker stack services anihpj     │ • namespaces             │ │
  │  $ docker service ls                │ • persistentvolumes      │ │
  │  $ docker stack ps anihpj           │ • serviceaccounts        │ │
  │                                     └──────────────────────────┘ │
  │                                                                 │
  │  For COMPLETE listing, be explicit:                             │
  │  $ kubectl get pods,svc,deploy,cm,secret,ingress -n anihpj     │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For the anihpj/jobpost stack on K8s, kubectl get all -n anihpj shows your Django pods and Postgres service, but NOT your database password (Secret) or nginx config (ConfigMap) — you'd need explicit kubectl get secret,configmap for those.

💡 DCA Exam Tip: Despite its name, kubectl get all does NOT show ALL resources — only pods, services, deployments, and replicasets. For a complete view, query each resource type explicitly. The DCA tests this misleading command.

Orchestration Q41 of 200
What command backs up the Docker Swarm configuration?
A) docker swarm export --backup > swarm-backup.tar
B) Copy the /var/lib/docker/swarm/ directory from a manager node (while the swarm is locked or stopped)
C) docker config export --all > backup.json
D) Docker Swarm is automatically backed up to Docker Hub
✅ Correct Answer: B

Swarm state is stored in /var/lib/docker/swarm/ on manager nodes. To backup: stop Docker on a manager, copy the entire directory. There's no built-in docker swarm backup command — unlike Kubernetes which has etcdctl snapshot save. This is a critical operational difference.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Swarm Backup vs Kubernetes Backup — Manual vs Built-in
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  SWARM BACKUP (manual):              K8s BACKUP (built-in):      │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────┐     ┌──────────────────────────┐│
  │  │ 1. Lock the swarm:         │     │ $ etcdctl snapshot save  ││
  │  │ $ docker swarm unlock-key  │     │   /backup/etcd.db        ││
  │  │                            │     │                          ││
  │  │ 2. Stop docker on manager: │     │ Single command!          ││
  │  │ $ systemctl stop docker    │     │ Snapshot = consistent    ││
  │  │                            │     │ point-in-time backup     ││
  │  │ 3. Copy Raft state:        │     └──────────────────────────┘│
  │  │ $ cp -r /var/lib/docker/   │                                 │
  │  │     swarm/ /backup/swarm/  │      WHAT'S IN /var/lib/docker/ │
  │  │                            │      swarm/?                    │
  │  │ 4. Restart docker:         │      • Raft log (all cluster   │
  │  │ $ systemctl start docker   │        state, service defs)    │
  │  └────────────────────────────┘      • Node certificates        │
  │                                       • Join tokens              │
  │  ⚠️ Backup from a MANAGER only!       • Encryption keys          │
  │  ⚠️ Stop docker first (data corruption│ • Network configs         │
  │    risk if copied while running)       ─────────────────────────  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For the anihpj/jobpost production Swarm, schedule a cron job that stops Docker on a follower manager (never the leader), copies /var/lib/docker/swarm/ to an off-site backup, and restarts Docker — all during a maintenance window.

💡 DCA Exam Tip: Swarm backup is manual (copy files from /var/lib/docker/swarm/ on a manager). Kubernetes backup uses etcdctl snapshot save. Know both approaches for the DCA exam. Never copy the swarm directory while Docker is running — risk of corrupt Raft logs.

Orchestration Q42 of 200
What does docker service update --limit-cpu 0.5 --limit-memory 512M api do?
A) Reserves CPU/memory on the host but doesn't enforce limits
B) Only applies to new replicas, not existing ones
C) Enforces resource limits on all service replicas — CPU capped at 0.5 cores, memory at 512MB
D) Limits the total resources the entire service can use across all replicas
✅ Correct Answer: C

--limit-cpu and --limit-memory set hard limits per task. Use --reserve-cpu and --reserve-memory for soft reservations that help the scheduler place tasks.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Resource Limits vs Reservations
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  LIMITS (Hard Cap):                                             │
  │  --limit-cpu 0.5      → Container can use MAX 0.5 CPU cores    │
  │  --limit-memory 512M  → Container OOM-killed if exceeded       │
  │                                                                 │
  │  RESERVATIONS (Soft Hint):                                      │
  │  --reserve-cpu 0.25    → Scheduler reserves 0.25 CPU            │
  │  --reserve-memory 256M → Scheduler ensures 256MB available      │
  │                                                                 │
  │  EXAMPLE (anihpj-api):                                          │
  │  $ docker service update \                                      │
  │      --limit-cpu 1 \                                            │
  │      --limit-memory 512M \                                      │
  │      --reserve-cpu 0.5 \                                        │
  │      --reserve-memory 256M \                                    │
  │      anihpj-api                                                 │
  │  → Each replica: 0.5 CPU guaranteed, burstable to 1.0 CPU       │
  │  → 256MB RAM guaranteed, OOM at 512MB                          │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: Limits = hard cap (OOM if exceeded). Reservations = scheduling hint. Know both. Resource management is a core DCA topic.

Orchestration Q43 of 200
How do you inspect detailed JSON configuration of a Swarm service?
A) docker service inspect <service-name>
B) docker service describe <service-name>
C) docker service config <service-name>
D) docker service show --json <service-name>
✅ Correct Answer: A

docker service inspect <service-name> returns the full JSON specification of the service — every setting from image, ports, environment variables, to update config, rollback config, resource limits, and placement constraints. Use --pretty for human-readable output or Go templates with --format to extract specific fields.

📝 Detailed Explanation
docker service inspect — Universal Inspection Command
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ docker service inspect anihpj-api --pretty                   │
  │                                                                 │
  │  OUTPUT SECTIONS:                                               │
  │  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ ID:        wz48q9k3l2m1                                   │ │
  │  │ Name:      anihpj-api                                     │ │
  │  │ Labels:    env=production                                  │ │
  │  │ Service Mode: Replicated (Replicas: 3)                    │ │
  │  │ ─────────────────────────────────────                      │ │
  │  │ ContainerSpec:                                            │ │
  │  │   Image:    anihpj-api:v2@sha256:abc...                   │ │
  │  │   Env:      DATABASE_URL=postgres://...                   │ │
  │  │   Mounts:   config:nginx-conf → /etc/nginx                │ │
  │  │ ─────────────────────────────────────                      │ │
  │  │ Resources:                                                │ │
  │  │   Limits:       CPU=1.0, Memory=512MiB                    │ │
  │  │   Reservations: CPU=0.5, Memory=256MiB                    │ │
  │  │ ─────────────────────────────────────                      │ │
  │  │ UpdateConfig:  Parallelism=1, Delay=10s                   │ │
  │  │ RollbackConfig: Parallelism=1, Delay=0s                   │ │
  │  │ ─────────────────────────────────────                      │ │
  │  │ Endpoint Mode: VIP                                        │ │
  │  │ Ports: 8080:8000/tcp                                      │ │
  │  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │                                                                 │
  │  GO TEMPLATE EXTRACTION:                                        │
  │  $ docker service inspect --format='{{.Spec.TaskTemplate...}}'  │
  │  → Extract just the image, env, or any specific field           │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For the anihpj/jobpost stack, use docker service inspect anihpj-api --pretty to verify the deployed image version, resource limits, and update config match your intended production settings — especially after a docker stack deploy.

💡 DCA Exam Tip: docker inspect is the universal inspection command — works on containers, images, networks, volumes, nodes, services, secrets, and configs. --pretty for humans, --format with Go templates for scripting.

Orchestration Q44 of 200
What is the default network driver used for Swarm services communicating across different nodes?
A) bridge
B) host
C) macvlan
D) overlay
✅ Correct Answer: D

The overlay network driver creates a distributed network using VXLAN tunnels spanning all Swarm nodes, allowing containers on different hosts to communicate as if on the same subnet. docker stack deploy automatically creates an overlay network for each stack.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Docker Network Drivers — Quick Reference
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  DRIVER    │  SCOPE       │  USE CASE                            │
  ├────────────┼──────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┤
  │  bridge    │  Single host │  Default. Containers on same host.  │
  │  overlay   │  Multi-host  │  Swarm services across nodes.       │
  │  host      │  Single host │  Direct host stack. No isolation.   │
  │  macvlan   │  Single host │  Own MAC/IP. Legacy app migration.  │
  │  none      │  Container   │  Complete network isolation.         │
  └────────────┴──────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────┘
  │                                                                 │
  │  OVERLAY (default for Swarm services):                          │
  │  node-1:10.0.0.2 ←──VXLAN tunnel──→ node-2:10.0.0.3            │
  │  Containers get IPs from 10.0.0.0/24 subnet                     │
  │  Cross-node communication is transparent to containers          │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: bridge = single host, overlay = multi-host (Swarm), host = direct host stack, macvlan = own MAC/IP. Overlay is the default for Swarm services.

Orchestration Q47 of 200
How do you authenticate Docker to push/pull images from Amazon ECR?
A) docker login --username AWS --password <IAM-access-key>
B) Use a permanent username/password created in the ECR console
C) docker ecr login --region us-east-1
D) aws ecr get-login-password | docker login --username AWS --password-stdin <account>.dkr.ecr.<region>.amazonaws.com
✅ Correct Answer: D

ECR uses temporary tokens (12-hour validity) obtained via the AWS CLI. The command pipes the token to docker login --password-stdin. This is more secure than storing permanent credentials.

📝 Detailed Explanation
ECR Authentication Flow
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  AWS ECR LOGIN (token valid for 12 hours):                      │
  │                                                                 │
  │  $ aws ecr get-login-password --region us-east-1 | \            │
  │    docker login --username AWS \                                │
  │    --password-stdin 123456789.dkr.ecr.us-east-1.amazonaws.com  │
  │  Login Succeeded                                                │
  │                                                                 │
  │  THEN push/pull as normal:                                      │
  │  $ docker tag anihpj-api:v1 123456789.dkr.ecr.us-east-1...     │
  │  $ docker push 123456789.dkr.ecr.us-east-1.amazonaws.com/...   │
  │                                                                 │
  │  ECR URL FORMAT:                                                │
  │  <account-id>.dkr.ecr.<region>.amazonaws.com/<repo>:<tag>     │
  │                                                                 │
  │  CI/CD PIPELINE: Always run get-login-password before push/pull │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: The ECR login token expires after 12 hours. In CI/CD pipelines, always run aws ecr get-login-password before any push/pull operations.

Orchestration Q48 of 200
What is AWS Fargate?
A) A managed Kubernetes control plane
B) A serverless compute engine for containers — you don't manage the underlying EC2 instances
C) A Docker image scanning service
D) A container registry integrated with Docker Hub
✅ Correct Answer: B

AWS Fargate is a serverless compute engine for containers. You define CPU and memory per task, and AWS provisions the underlying infrastructure automatically. No EC2 instances to patch, scale, or manage — you only pay for the vCPU and memory your containerized application consumes while running.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Fargate vs EC2 Launch Type — Serverless vs Self-Managed
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  EC2 LAUNCH TYPE:                    FARGATE LAUNCH TYPE:        │
  │  ┌────────────────────────┐         ┌──────────────────────────┐│
  │  │ YOU manage:            │         │ AWS manages EVERYTHING:  ││
  │  │ ┌────────────────────┐ │         │ ┌──────────────────────┐ ││
  │  │ │ • EC2 instances    │ │         │ │ • No instances       │ ││
  │  │ │ • AMI patching     │ │         │ │ • No SSH access      │ ││
  │  │ │ • Docker daemon    │ │         │ │ • No capacity planning│ ││
  │  │ │ • Cluster scaling  │ │         │ │                      │ ││
  │  │ │ • Instance type    │ │         │ │ Each task gets its own│ ││
  │  │ │ • OS security      │ │         │ │ isolated compute env. │ ││
  │  │ └────────────────────┘ │         │ └──────────────────────┘ ││
  │  │                        │         │                          ││
  │  │ Pay per EC2 instance   │         │ Pay per task (vCPU+memory)││
  │  │ (even if underutilized)│         │ (only while running)      ││
  │  └────────────────────────┘         └──────────────────────────┘│
  │                                                                 │
  │  FARGATE = Serverless containers | EC2 = Self-managed containers│
  │  Both work with ECS AND EKS                                     │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For the anihpj/jobpost stack, Fargate is ideal for the stateless Django API and web frontend — no servers to manage, automatic scaling, pay-per-request pricing. For the database, you'd still use RDS (not containerized).

💡 DCA Exam Tip: Fargate = serverless containers (pay per task, no instance management). EC2 launch type = you manage instances (pay per instance, more control). Both work with ECS and EKS. Fargate is simpler but less customizable.

Orchestration Q50 of 200
When deploying anihpj/jobpost on AWS, which combination of services provides a fully managed container deployment?
A) EC2 + Docker Engine + manual Swarm setup
B) Lambda + API Gateway
C) ECR (image storage) + ECS/Fargate (orchestration) + RDS (PostgreSQL) + ElastiCache (Redis)
D) S3 + CloudFront for static hosting
✅ Correct Answer: C

For a fully managed anihpj/jobpost deployment on AWS: ECR stores the Docker images, ECS/Fargate runs the containers, RDS hosts PostgreSQL, and ElastiCache provides Redis. No servers to manage.

📝 Detailed Explanation
anihpj/jobpost on AWS — Fully Managed Architecture
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Internet → ALB (port 80/443)                                   │
  │      │                                                          │
  │      ├──→ ECS/Fargate: anihpj-web (nginx)                       │
  │      │       │                                                  │
  │      │       └──→ ECS/Fargate: anihpj-api (gunicorn+Django)     │
  │      │               │                                          │
  │      │               ├──→ RDS: PostgreSQL 15 (managed DB)       │
  │      │               └──→ ElastiCache: Redis 7 (managed cache)  │
  │      │                                                          │
  │  IMAGE STORAGE: Amazon ECR                                      │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ anihpj-web:latest  anihpj-api:latest  (scanned for CVEs) │   │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                 │
  │  NO SERVERS TO PATCH, SCALE, OR MAINTAIN (all managed services) │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: The DCA covers Docker on AWS. Understand the managed services: ECR (registry), ECS (orchestration), Fargate (serverless compute), EKS (managed K8s). Know how they fit together for a production deployment.

🐳 Category 2: Image Creation, Management & Registry20% — Q51-Q90

📦 Category 2: Image Creation, Management & Registry — 40 Questions

This category is about creating efficient, secure Docker images and managing them across registries. Think of it like packaging your application into a shipping container — the better you pack it, the safer and faster it travels. Master Dockerfile instructions, multi-stage builds, image lifecycle, and enterprise registries (Docker Hub, ECR, MSR). 20% of the DCA exam.

2.1 Dockerfile MasteryQ51-Q62

Image Creation Q51 of 200
What is the purpose of the FROM instruction in a Dockerfile?
A) It specifies which port the container listens on
B) It sets the base image for subsequent instructions — every Dockerfile must start with FROM
C) It imports code from a Git repository into the image
D) It defines the working directory for the build context
✅ Correct Answer: B

FROM is the first instruction in every Dockerfile (except ARG declarations before FROM). It pulls a base image from a registry and sets the foundation layer upon which all subsequent instructions build. Base image selection — alpine vs slim vs distroless — directly impacts image size, security, and compatibility.

📝 Detailed Explanation
FROM — Base Image Selection Strategy
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  FROM <image>[:<tag>] [AS <stage-name>]                         │
  │                                                                 │
  │  BASE IMAGE COMPARISON (for anihpj-api Python app):             │
  │  ┌──────────────┬──────────┬──────────┬──────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ IMAGE        │ SIZE     │ CVEs     │ USE CASE              │ │
  │  ├──────────────┼──────────┼──────────┼──────────────────────┤ │
  │  │ python:3.12  │ ~1GB     │ 100+     │ Full OS, dev only     │ │
  │  │ python:3.12- │ ~150MB   │ 30-50    │ Production, some tools │ │
  │  │   slim       │          │          │                       │ │
  │  │ python:3.12- │ ~50MB    │ 0-5      │ Minimal, musl libc    │ │
  │  │   alpine     │          │          │ (may break C ext)     │ │
  │  │ distroless/  │ ~50MB    │ 0-2      │ Hardened, NO shell    │ │
  │  │   python3    │          │          │ (debugging harder)    │ │
  │  │ scratch      │ ~0MB     │ 0        │ Static binaries only  │ │
  │  └──────────────┴──────────┴──────────┴──────────────────────┘ │
  │                                                                 │
  │  BEST PRACTICE: Use specific tags (NOT :latest) and pin with    │
  │  digests: FROM python:3.12-slim@sha256:abc123...               │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For the anihpj/jobpost Django API, we use FROM python:3.12-slim — it provides glibc compatibility for C extensions (like psycopg2) while keeping the image reasonably small at ~150MB.

💡 DCA Exam Tip: Every Dockerfile must start with FROM (or a parser directive). Know the base image tradeoffs: full OS (big, many CVEs), slim (balanced), alpine (tiny, musl libc issues), distroless (hardened, no shell), scratch (static binaries only).

Image Creation Q52 of 200
What happens if you omit WORKDIR from your Dockerfile?
A) The build fails with a "WORKDIR required" error
B) Docker automatically uses /app as the working directory
C) All subsequent RUN, CMD, ENTRYPOINT, COPY, and ADD instructions execute from / (root directory)
D) The container starts in a temporary directory that is deleted after the process exits
✅ Correct Answer: C

Without WORKDIR, the default working directory is / (root). This means COPY . . copies files to /, RUN commands execute in /, and relative paths are resolved from root — potentially cluttering the filesystem and creating security risks.

📝 Detailed Explanation
WORKDIR — Before vs After
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  WITHOUT WORKDIR:                    WITH WORKDIR:               │
  │  ┌────────────────────────┐         ┌────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ FROM python:3.12-slim  │         │ FROM python:3.12-slim  │  │
  │  │ COPY . .  → copies to /│         │ WORKDIR /app           │  │
  │  │ RUN pip install ...    │         │ COPY . .  → /app/      │  │
  │  │   → runs from /        │         │ RUN pip install ...    │  │
  │  │ CMD ["python", "app.py"]│        │   → runs from /app     │  │
  │  │   → runs from /        │         │ CMD ["python","app.py"]│  │
  │  │                        │         │   → runs from /app     │  │
  │  │ Files scattered in /   │         │                        │  │
  │  │ Risky: overwrite /etc? │         │ All files in /app/     │  │
  │  └────────────────────────┘         │ Clean, organized       │  │
  │                                      └────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                 │
  │  WORKDIR creates the directory if it doesn't exist.             │
  │  Best practice: WORKDIR should be set RIGHT AFTER FROM.         │
  │  Multiple WORKDIR instructions = each changes the CWD.          │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For the anihpj/jobpost Dockerfile, WORKDIR /app ensures all application code, requirements, and commands are scoped to one directory — preventing accidental overwrites of system files in /.

💡 DCA Exam Tip: WORKDIR sets the working directory for all subsequent instructions AND creates the directory if missing. Without it, everything runs from /. Always set WORKDIR early in your Dockerfile — right after FROM.

Image Creation Q53 of 200
What is the key difference between COPY and ADD in a Dockerfile?
A) COPY only copies local files; ADD can also fetch remote URLs and auto-extract tar archives
B) COPY preserves file permissions; ADD strips them
C) COPY can fetch from URLs; ADD is for local files only
D) There is no difference — they are aliases
✅ Correct Answer: A

COPY is simple and explicit — it copies files/directories from the build context into the image. ADD has two extra features: it can fetch remote URLs and auto-extract local .tar archives. Docker's best practice is to prefer COPY unless you specifically need tar extraction.

📝 Detailed Explanation
COPY vs ADD — Decision Flowchart
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Are you copying local files?                                    │
  │  ┌──────────┐                                                   │
  │  │   YES    │──────▶ Use COPY                                    │
  │  └──────────┘       $ COPY requirements.txt /app/               │
  │       │             $ COPY . /app/                              │
  │       │                                                         │
  │  Are you extracting a local .tar archive?                        │
  │  ┌──────────┐                                                   │
  │  │   YES    │──────▶ Use ADD (auto-extracts)                    │
  │  └──────────┘       $ ADD archive.tar.gz /app/                  │
  │       │             → extracts to /app/                         │
  │       │                                                         │
  │  Are you fetching from a remote URL?                             │
  │  ┌──────────┐                                                   │
  │  │   YES    │──────▶ Use RUN curl/wget instead!                 │
  │  └──────────┘       ADD <url> creates unpredictable layers     │
  │                     $ RUN curl -o /app/file.tar https://...    │
  │                     $ RUN tar -xzf /app/file.tar -C /app       │
  │                                                                 │
  │  ⚠️ DOCKER OFFICIAL: "For most use cases, COPY is preferred."   │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For the anihpj/jobpost Dockerfile, we use COPY requirements.txt . and COPY . . — simple, predictable, no surprises. We never use ADD because we don't need tar extraction in our build pipeline.

💡 DCA Exam Tip: Prefer COPY over ADD. COPY = local files only (simple). ADD = local files + remote URLs + tar extraction (magical, less predictable). Docker's official best practice: "Use COPY unless you specifically need ADD's tar auto-extraction."

Image Creation Q54 of 200
Why is it recommended to chain multiple RUN commands with && instead of using separate RUN instructions?
A) Chained RUNs execute faster because Docker parallelizes them
B) Separate RUNs are deprecated in Docker 24+
C) Chained RUNs produce better caching because Docker caches the entire chain
D) Each RUN creates a new image layer; chaining with && reduces layer count and overall image size
✅ Correct Answer: D

Every RUN instruction creates a new image layer. Multiple separate RUNs = multiple layers = larger image. Chaining commands with && combines them into ONE layer. Additionally, cleaning up in the SAME layer (e.g., apt-get update && apt-get install -y pkg && rm -rf /var/lib/apt/lists/*) prevents deleted files from persisting in earlier layers.

📝 Detailed Explanation
RUN — Layer Optimization with && Chaining
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  BAD (4 layers, larger image):        GOOD (1 layer, smaller):   │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────┐       ┌──────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ RUN apt-get update       ← L1│      │ RUN apt-get update &&\│  │
  │  │ RUN apt-get install -y \  │       │     apt-get install -y \│  │
  │  │     gcc python3-dev     ← L2│      │     gcc python3-dev &&\│  │
  │  │ RUN pip install -r       ← L3│      │     pip install -r    │  │
  │  │     requirements.txt      │       │     requirements.txt &&\│  │
  │  │ RUN rm -rf /var/lib/apt  ← L4│      │     rm -rf /var/lib/  │  │
  │  │                            │       │     apt/lists/*      ← 1│  │
  │  │ L4 hides files from L2-L3 │       │                        │  │
  │  │ but they're STILL in L2,L3│       │ Cleanup IN the layer!  │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────┘       └──────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                 │
  │  LAYER CACHING TRADE-OFF:                                       │
  │  • Separate RUNs = better cache granularity (one change ≠       │
  │    rebuild everything)                                           │
  │  • Chained RUNs = smaller image, but one change = full rebuild  │
  │  • BALANCE: Group related ops, but keep frequently-changing     │
  │    commands (pip install) separate from rarely-changing ones    │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For the anihpj/jobpost Dockerfile, we chain apt operations with cleanup in one layer, but keep pip install -r requirements.txt separate — since requirements change more often than system packages.

💡 DCA Exam Tip: Each RUN = a new layer. Chain with && to reduce layers and image size. Clean up (rm -rf /var/lib/apt/lists/*) in the SAME RUN layer. Balance: chaining reduces size but may hurt cache efficiency for frequently-changing commands.

Image Creation Q55 of 200
What is the difference between CMD and ENTRYPOINT in a Dockerfile?
A) CMD runs at build time; ENTRYPOINT runs at container startup
B) ENTRYPOINT defines the executable; CMD provides default arguments that can be overridden at runtime
C) CMD is for shell commands; ENTRYPOINT is for Python scripts only
D) They are interchangeable — no functional difference
✅ Correct Answer: B

ENTRYPOINT defines the main executable that always runs. CMD provides default arguments to ENTRYPOINT — and these arguments can be overridden by docker run <image> <new-args>. If only CMD is used, it defines both the executable AND default arguments (fully overridable). Think: ENTRYPOINT = "what to run", CMD = "default flags".

📝 Detailed Explanation
CMD vs ENTRYPOINT — Shell Form vs Exec Form
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  EXEC FORM (preferred):              SHELL FORM (avoid):         │
  │  ENTRYPOINT ["gunicorn"]             ENTRYPOINT gunicorn         │
  │  CMD ["--bind", "0.0.0.0:8000"]     CMD --bind 0.0.0.0:8000    │
  │                                     → Runs via /bin/sh -c       │
  │  THREE COMBINATIONS:                → No signal forwarding!     │
  │  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ 1. CMD only:            CMD ["python", "app.py"]          │ │
  │  │    $ docker run img echo hi  → "echo hi" replaces CMD!   │ │
  │  │                                                          │ │
  │  │ 2. ENTRYPOINT only:     ENTRYPOINT ["python", "app.py"]   │ │
  │  │    $ docker run img --verbose  → python app.py --verbose │ │
  │  │    (args APPENDED to ENTRYPOINT)                         │ │
  │  │                                                          │ │
  │  │ 3. BOTH (recommended):  ENTRYPOINT ["gunicorn"]          │ │
  │  │                         CMD ["--bind", "0.0.0.0:8000"]   │ │
  │  │    $ docker run img  → gunicorn --bind 0.0.0.0:8000       │ │
  │  │    $ docker run img --workers 4 → gunicorn --workers 4    │ │
  │  │    (CMD overridden, ENTRYPOINT stays)                    │ │
  │  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For the anihpj/jobpost API, we use ENTRYPOINT ["gunicorn"] with CMD ["anihpj.wsgi:application", "--bind", "0.0.0.0:8000"] — so users can override the bind address without changing the gunicorn executable.

💡 DCA Exam Tip: ENTRYPOINT = the "what" (executable). CMD = the "with what" (default args). Exec form (JSON array) is preferred over shell form. CMD is fully overridable by docker run args; ENTRYPOINT requires --entrypoint to override.

Image Creation Q56 of 200
What is the difference between ENV and ARG in a Dockerfile?
A) ENV is for secrets; ARG is for public variables
B) ARG persists in the running container; ENV is only available at build time
C) ENV sets environment variables available at BOTH build and runtime; ARG is only available during build and can be passed via --build-arg
D) There is no difference; both set environment variables
✅ Correct Answer: C

ENV variables persist in both the build stage AND the final running container — they're baked into the image. ARG variables exist ONLY during the build and are NOT available in the running container unless explicitly saved to ENV. ARGs can be passed via --build-arg; ENVs are set in the Dockerfile.

📝 Detailed Explanation
ENV vs ARG — Build-Time vs Run-Time Scope
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ARG (build-time only):              ENV (build + runtime):      │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────┐        ┌──────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ ARG PYTHON_VERSION=3.12  │        │ ENV DJANGO_ENV=prod   │   │
  │  │ $ docker build \         │        │ ENV DATABASE_URL=...  │   │
  │  │   --build-arg PYTHON_..  │        │                       │   │
  │  │   =3.11 .                │        │ $ docker run -it img   │   │
  │  │                          │        │ $ env | grep DJANGO    │   │
  │  │ $ docker run img env     │        │ DJANGO_ENV=prod       │   │
  │  │ PYTHON_VERSION: (absent) │        │ ✅ Persists at runtime │   │
  │  │ ❌ NOT in running container│      └──────────────────────┘   │
  │  └──────────────────────────┘                                   │
  │                                                                 │
  │  PASS ARG to ENV (bridge):                                      │
  │  ARG APP_VERSION=v1.2.3                                         │
  │  ENV APP_VERSION=$APP_VERSION  ← ARG value baked into ENV       │
  │                                                                 │
  │  ⚠️ SECURITY: Never put secrets in ENV or ARG — they appear     │
  │     in docker history and docker inspect. Use Swarm secrets.    │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For the anihpj/jobpost Dockerfile, we use ARG DJANGO_ENV=production to set the default environment at build time and ENV DJANGO_ENV=$DJANGO_ENV to persist it into the running container for Django's settings module.

💡 DCA Exam Tip: ENV = available at build AND runtime (persists in image). ARG = build-time only, passed via --build-arg. Never store secrets in either — use Docker secrets or a vault. ARG values are visible in docker history!

Image Creation Q57 of 200
What does the EXPOSE instruction actually do?
A) It opens the specified port on the host firewall
B) It publishes the port to the host, making the container accessible externally
C) It binds the container port to a random host port automatically
D) It is purely documentation — it tells users which ports the container listens on but does NOT publish them
✅ Correct Answer: D

EXPOSE is documentation only — it doesn't publish ports or open firewalls. It tells image consumers which ports the application expects to listen on. To actually publish a port, you need -p 8080:80 at runtime or ports: in Compose.

📝 Detailed Explanation
EXPOSE vs -p — Documentation vs Actual Publishing
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Dockerfile:                           Container Runtime:        │
  │  ┌────────────────────────┐            ┌──────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ EXPOSE 8000            │            │ $ docker run -d \    │ │
  │  │ → "This app uses 8000" │            │   -p 8080:8000 img   │ │
  │  │ → Metadata ONLY        │            │ → 8080 on HOST       │ │
  │  │ → Does NOT open port   │            │ → Forwards to 8000   │ │
  │  └────────────────────────┘            └──────────────────────┘ │
  │                                                                 │
  │  LIST EXPOSED PORTS:                ACTUAL PORT PUBLISHING:     │
  │  $ docker image inspect img         -p HOST:CONTAINER           │
  │    "ExposedPorts": {                -p 8080:80  (map specific) │
  │      "8000/tcp": {}                 -p 80       (random host)  │
  │    }                                -P          (publish all   │
  │                                                 EXPOSEd ports) │
  │                                                                 │
  │  ONLY -P (capital P) uses EXPOSE metadata to auto-publish!     │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For the anihpj/jobpost Dockerfile, EXPOSE 8000 documents that gunicorn listens on port 8000 — but the actual publishing happens via docker run -p 8080:8000 or the Compose file's ports: section.

💡 DCA Exam Tip: EXPOSE is documentation/metadata ONLY — it does NOT publish ports. Use -p (lowercase) for specific port mapping, -P (uppercase) to publish all EXPOSEd ports to random host ports. The DCA exam loves this trick question.

Image Creation Q58 of 200
What does the VOLUME instruction do in a Dockerfile?
A) It mounts a host directory into the container at build time
B) It creates a mount point and marks it as holding externally mounted data — the actual volume is created at container runtime
C) It copies data from the host into the container's filesystem
D) It creates a persistent volume that survives image deletion
✅ Correct Answer: B

VOLUME declares a mount point in the image and marks it for external storage. At container runtime, Docker creates an anonymous volume (or uses a named one if specified at run). This ensures data written to that path doesn't increase the container's writable layer and persists beyond the container's lifecycle.

📝 Detailed Explanation
VOLUME — Declaring Mount Points
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Dockerfile:                        Runtime behavior:            │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────┐       ┌───────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ VOLUME /var/lib/postgres │       │ $ docker run -d \     │  │
  │  │ → Declares mount point   │       │   -v pgdata:/var/lib/ │  │
  │  │ → Data at /var/lib/      │       │     postgres postgres │  │
  │  │   postgres during build  │       │ → Named volume: pgdata│  │
  │  │   is COPIED to volume    │       │                       │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────┘       │ $ docker run -d \     │  │
  │                                      │   postgres            │  │
  │  ANONYMOUS vs NAMED:                │ → Anonymous volume    │  │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────┐    │   (random hash name)  │  │
  │  │ Anonymous: VOLUME /data     │    └───────────────────────┘  │
  │  │ → docker creates random vol │                                │
  │  │                             │    ⚠️ VOLUME in Dockerfile:    │
  │  │ Named (at runtime):         │    • Cannot specify host path  │
  │  │ → docker run -v name:/data  │    • Subsequent RUN instructions│
  │  │   Overrides the anonymous   │      CANNOT write to volume    │
  │  └─────────────────────────────┘    • Use docker run -v instead │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For the anihpj/jobpost Postgres image, VOLUME /var/lib/postgresql/data ensures database files are stored outside the container's writable layer — critical for data persistence across container restarts and updates.

💡 DCA Exam Tip: VOLUME creates a mount point at build time; the actual volume is created at runtime. VOLUME cannot specify a host path — use docker run -v for that. After VOLUME, subsequent RUN instructions can't write to that directory (Docker creates a temporary volume).

Image Creation Q59 of 200
Why should you use the USER instruction to switch from root to a non-root user?
A) Running containers as root is a security risk — if compromised, the attacker gains root access to the host. USER limits the blast radius.
B) Docker Hub requires all images to use non-root users for publishing
C) Non-root users have better performance due to reduced kernel context switches
D) Root user cannot access mounted volumes
✅ Correct Answer: A

Containers share the host kernel. If a container process running as root escapes the container (via a kernel exploit or misconfiguration), it has root access to the host. Switching to a non-root user via USER 1000 or USER appuser follows the principle of least privilege — even if compromised, the attacker is limited to that user's permissions.

📝 Detailed Explanation
USER — Root vs Non-Root Security Model
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ROOT CONTAINER (dangerous):          NON-ROOT CONTAINER (safe): │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────┐        ┌──────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ Container runs as UID 0  │        │ Container runs as     │  │
  │  │ (root)                   │        │ UID 1000 (non-root)   │  │
  │  │                          │        │                       │  │
  │  │ If attacker escapes:     │        │ If attacker escapes:  │  │
  │  │ → Root on HOST! 😱       │        │ → Only UID 1000 perms │  │
  │  │ → Read /etc/shadow       │        │ → Cannot modify system │  │
  │  │ → Install backdoors      │        │ → Limited blast radius │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────┘        └──────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                 │
  │  DOCKERFILE BEST PRACTICE:                                      │
  │  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ RUN addgroup -S appgroup && adduser -S appuser -G appgroup│ │
  │  │ USER appuser                                              │ │
  │  │ → All subsequent RUN, CMD, ENTRYPOINT run as appuser      │ │
  │  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │                                                                 │
  │  Also: --userns-remap (user namespace remapping) at daemon level│
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For the anihpj/jobpost Dockerfile, we use FROM gcr.io/distroless/python3-debian12:nonroot which already runs as a non-root user, or explicitly USER 1000:1000 after creating the user in a slim-based image.

💡 DCA Exam Tip: Always switch to a non-root USER for production images. Root in container ≈ root on host if containment breaks. Use USER after installing packages (which need root). Distroless images often default to non-root.

Image Creation Q60 of 200
What is the purpose of the HEALTHCHECK instruction?
A) It runs a diagnostic scan on the host system before the container starts
B) It checks if the base image has known vulnerabilities
C) It tells Docker how to test if the container is still working — the status appears in docker ps as "healthy" or "unhealthy"
D) It automatically restarts the container if the application crashes
✅ Correct Answer: C

HEALTHCHECK defines a command that Docker runs periodically inside the container to verify the application is healthy. Results appear in docker ps STATUS column as (healthy), (unhealthy), or (starting). Swarm services can use health checks for rolling update decisions.

📝 Detailed Explanation
HEALTHCHECK — Container Health Monitoring
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  HEALTHCHECK [OPTIONS] CMD <command>                             │
  │                                                                 │
  │  OPTIONS:                                                        │
  │  --interval=30s    (how often to check, default 30s)             │
  │  --timeout=3s      (how long to wait for response)              │
  │  --start-period=5s (grace period before checks begin)            │
  │  --retries=3       (consecutive failures before "unhealthy")     │
  │                                                                 │
  │  EXAMPLE (anihpj-api):                                           │
  │  HEALTHCHECK --interval=30s --timeout=3s --retries=3 \           │
  │    CMD curl -f http://localhost:8000/health/ || exit 1           │
  │                                                                 │
  │  $ docker ps                                                     │
  │  CONTAINER ID   STATUS                    NAMES                  │
  │  abc123...      Up 2 minutes (healthy)    anihpj-api             │
  │  def456...      Up 30 seconds (starting)  anihpj-api-new         │
  │                                                                 │
  │  SWARM: docker service update --update-order start-first        │
  │  → New task must be "healthy" before old task stops              │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For the anihpj/jobpost Django API, we add a /health/ endpoint and use HEALTHCHECK CMD curl -f http://localhost:8000/health/ || exit 1 — Swarm then waits for new replicas to be healthy before removing old ones during rolling updates.

💡 DCA Exam Tip: HEALTHCHECK makes docker ps show (healthy)/(unhealthy)/(starting). Swarm uses it for rolling updates (--update-order start-first). Exit code 0 = healthy; 1 = unhealthy; 2 = reserved (don't use). Only ONE HEALTHCHECK per Dockerfile.

Image Creation Q61 of 200
What is the purpose of a .dockerignore file?
A) It lists images that should not be pulled from the registry
B) It excludes files/directories from the build context — speeding up builds and preventing secrets from leaking into images
C) It specifies which containers should not be started with docker compose up
D) It defines which Dockerfile instructions to skip during build
✅ Correct Answer: B

.dockerignore works like .gitignore — it tells Docker CLI which files NOT to send to the build context. This speeds up builds (smaller context to send to daemon), prevents sensitive files (.env, .git, credentials) from leaking into images, and avoids cache invalidation from irrelevant file changes.

📝 Detailed Explanation
.dockerignore — Build Context Filtering
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  WITHOUT .dockerignore:               WITH .dockerignore:        │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────┐         ┌──────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ $ docker build .         │         │ $ docker build .     │  │
  │  │ Sending build context:   │         │ Sending build context:│  │
  │  │ 500MB to daemon          │         │ 50MB to daemon       │  │
  │  │ (includes .git, node_    │         │ (filtered!)          │  │
  │  │  modules, .env, __pyc__) │         │                      │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────┘         └──────────────────────┘  │
