  │                                                                 │
  │  anihpj/.dockerignore:                                          │
  │  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ # Version control                                         │ │
  │  │ .git/                                                     │ │
  │  │ .gitignore                                                │ │
  │  │                                                           │ │
  │  │ # Python                                                   │ │
  │  │ __pycache__/                                              │ │
  │  │ *.pyc                                                     │ │
  │  │ .venv/                                                    │ │
  │  │                                                           │ │
  │  │ # Secrets & environment                                    │ │
  │  │ .env                                                      │ │
  │  │ *.pem                                                     │ │
  │  │ id_rsa                                                    │ │
  │  │                                                           │ │
  │  │ # Local dev                                               │ │
  │  │ db.sqlite3                                                │ │
  │  │ docker-compose.override.yml                               │ │
  │  │                                                           │ │
  │  │ # OS junk                                                 │ │
  │  │ .DS_Store                                                 │ │
  │  │ Thumbs.db                                                 │ │
  │  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For the anihpj/jobpost project, the .dockerignore excludes .git/ (not needed in the image), db.sqlite3 (local dev DB, not for containers), .env (contains secrets), and __pycache__/ — reducing the build context from ~200MB to ~20MB.

💡 DCA Exam Tip: .dockerignore speeds up builds and prevents secret leaks. Place it in the build context root. Pattern syntax similar to .gitignore. Always exclude: .git, .env, node_modules, __pycache__, *.log, and IDE files.

Image Creation Q62 of 200
Review this Dockerfile for anihpj-api. Which instruction is placed incorrectly?
A) FROM python:3.12-slim should be FROM python:3.12-alpine for production
B) WORKDIR should be declared before FROM
C) EXPOSE 8000 should be at the top of the Dockerfile
D) COPY . . is placed BEFORE COPY requirements.txt — this breaks layer caching because any code change invalidates the pip install layer
✅ Correct Answer: D

The correct order is: COPY requirements.txt . THEN RUN pip install THEN COPY . .. Copying all code first means EVERY code change invalidates the pip install cache layer — making builds much slower. Copying requirements.txt first means pip install is only re-run when dependencies change.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Layer Caching Strategy — Correct COPY Order
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ❌ WRONG ORDER (slow builds):        ✅ CORRECT ORDER (fast):   │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────┐       ┌──────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ FROM python:3.12-slim      │       │ FROM python:3.12-slim│  │
  │  │ WORKDIR /app               │       │ WORKDIR /app         │  │
  │  │ COPY . .          ← Layer 3│       │ COPY requirements.txt│  │
  │  │ RUN pip install -r│        │       │   .          ← Layer 3│  │
  │  │   requirements.txt ← L4    │       │ RUN pip install -r   │  │
  │  │                            │       │   requirements.txt   │  │
  │  │ Every code change → L3     │       │               ← L4   │  │
  │  │ invalidated → L4 rebuilds  │       │ COPY . .     ← Layer 5│  │
  │  │ pip install EVERY TIME!    │       │                       │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────┘       │ Code change → only L5 │  │
  │                                        │ rebuilds (pip CACHED)│  │
  │  BUILD TIMES:                          └──────────────────────┘  │
  │  • Code change: ~5 min (pip reinstall)                          │
  │  • Requirements change: ~5 min         • Code change: ~2 sec    │
  │                                        • Req change: ~5 min     │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For the anihpj/jobpost Dockerfile, the correct order is: COPY requirements.txt . → RUN pip install --no-cache-dir -r requirements.txt → COPY . .. This way, changing Django views doesn't trigger a full pip install — only dependency changes do.

💡 DCA Exam Tip: Always COPY requirements.txt (or package.json) BEFORE COPY . . — this maximizes layer caching. The dependency install layer is only rebuilt when dependencies actually change, not on every code edit. This is one of the most tested DCA Dockerfile patterns.

2.2 Multi-Stage Builds & OptimizationQ63-Q70

Image Creation Q63 of 200
What is the primary purpose of multi-stage builds in Docker?
A) To build multiple images simultaneously from one Dockerfile
B) To run multiple containers from a single image
C) To separate build-time dependencies from the runtime image — producing a smaller, more secure final image
D) To deploy the same image to multiple environments
✅ Correct Answer: C

Multi-stage builds use multiple FROM statements in one Dockerfile. The first stage(s) compile/build the application with all necessary tools (compilers, SDKs, dev headers). The final stage copies ONLY the compiled artifacts — leaving behind compilers, package managers, and build tools that would bloat the image and increase the attack surface.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Multi-Stage Build — Separate Build from Runtime
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  SINGLE STAGE (bad):                 MULTI-STAGE (good):         │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────┐       ┌──────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ FROM python:3.12         │       │ # Stage 1: BUILD     │   │
  │  │ RUN apt-get install gcc  │       │ FROM python:3.12 AS  │   │
  │  │ COPY . .                 │       │   builder            │   │
  │  │ RUN pip install -r reqs  │       │ RUN apt-get install  │   │
  │  │ CMD ["gunicorn", "..."]  │       │   gcc python3-dev    │   │
  │  │                          │       │ COPY requirements.txt│   │
  │  │ Image: 1.2 GB 😱         │       │ RUN pip install ...  │   │
  │  │ Includes: gcc, git,      │       │ COPY . .             │   │
  │  │   dev headers, build deps│       │                       │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────┘       │ # Stage 2: RUNTIME   │   │
  │                                      │ FROM python:3.12-slim│   │
  │                                      │ COPY --from=builder  │   │
  │                                      │   /usr/local/lib/... │   │
  │                                      │ COPY --from=builder  │   │
  │                                      │   /app /app          │   │
  │                                      │ CMD ["gunicorn",...] │   │
  │                                      │                       │   │
  │                                      │ Image: 150 MB ✅      │   │
  │                                      │ NO gcc, git, headers  │   │
  │                                      └──────────────────────┘   │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For the anihpj/jobpost API, multi-stage builds reduce the image from ~1.2GB (full Python + build tools) to ~150MB (slim runtime only) — a 8x size reduction and dramatically smaller attack surface.

💡 DCA Exam Tip: Multi-stage builds are the #1 technique for reducing image size. Builder stage = compilers + SDKs; runtime stage = minimal base + copied artifacts. Use COPY --from=<stage> to copy between stages. This is heavily tested on the DCA.

Image Creation Q64 of 200
What does AS builder do in FROM python:3.12-slim AS builder?
A) It marks the image as a build-only image that cannot be run
B) It names the build stage so later stages can reference it with COPY --from=builder
C) It tells Docker to use the BuildKit builder backend
D) It automatically removes the stage after the build completes
✅ Correct Answer: B

AS <name> gives a build stage a name that can be referenced later. Without names, stages are referenced by zero-based index (0, 1, 2...). Named stages make the Dockerfile self-documenting and resilient to reordering: COPY --from=builder /app /app is clearer than COPY --from=0 /app /app.

📝 Detailed Explanation
AS — Naming Build Stages
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  WITHOUT NAMES (fragile):              WITH NAMES (robust):      │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────┐     ┌─────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ FROM python:3.12-slim        │     │ FROM python:3.12-slim│  │
  │  │ ...                          │     │   AS builder         │  │
  │  │                              │     │ ...                  │  │
  │  │ FROM python:3.12-slim        │     │                      │  │
  │  │ COPY --from=0 /app/deps /app │     │ FROM python:3.12-slim│  │
  │  │                              │     │   AS tester          │  │
  │  │ If you add a stage before    │     │ COPY --from=builder  │  │
  │  │ stage 0 → ALL indexes shift! │     │   /app/deps /app    │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────────┘     │ COPY --from=tester   │  │
  │                                        │   /app /app          │  │
  │  STAGE TYPES:                          └─────────────────────┘  │
  │  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ • builder   — compile/build artifacts                     │ │
  │  │ • tester    — run unit/lint tests before final image      │ │
  │  │ • runtime   — minimal production image (last FROM)        │ │
  │  │ • dependencies — separate pip/npm layer for better cache  │ │
  │  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For the anihpj/jobpost Dockerfile, we use FROM python:3.12-slim AS builder, FROM python:3.12-slim AS tester, and FROM python:3.12-slim AS runtime — three named stages with clear purposes.

💡 DCA Exam Tip: Always name your stages with AS — it makes the Dockerfile self-documenting and prevents breakage if stages are reordered. COPY --from=builder is preferred over COPY --from=0. You can have as many stages as you want; only the last FROM produces the final image.

Image Creation Q65 of 200
How do you copy a Python virtual environment from a builder stage to the final runtime stage?
A) COPY --from=builder /app/.venv /app/.venv
B) COPY builder:/app/.venv /app/.venv
C) RUN docker cp builder:/app/.venv /app/.venv
D) ADD --stage=builder /app/.venv /app/.venv
✅ Correct Answer: A

COPY --from=<stage-name> <src> <dest> copies files/directories from a previous build stage into the current stage. This is the mechanism that makes multi-stage builds work — you build in one stage, copy only the artifacts to the final stage. You can also copy from named images: COPY --from=nginx:alpine /etc/nginx/nginx.conf /etc/nginx/.

📝 Detailed Explanation
COPY --from — Artifact Transfer Between Stages
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  STAGE 1: builder                     STAGE 2: runtime           │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────┐         ┌──────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ FROM python:3.12 AS      │         │ FROM python:3.12-slim│  │
  │  │   builder                │         │                      │  │
  │  │ WORKDIR /app             │         │ COPY --from=builder  │  │
  │  │ RUN pip install --user   │         │   /root/.local \     │  │
  │  │   -r requirements.txt    │         │   /home/nonroot/.local│  │
  │  │                          │         │                      │  │
  │  │ Build artifacts:         │         │ COPY --from=builder  │  │
  │  │ /root/.local/  (deps)    │────────▶│   /app /app          │  │
  │  │ /app/          (code)    │────────▶│                      │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────┘         │ Only 2 COPY lines,   │  │
  │                                        │ but all deps + code  │  │
  │  ALSO WORKS: COPY --from=nginx:alpine  │ transferred!         │  │
  │  /etc/nginx/nginx.conf /etc/nginx/     └──────────────────────┘  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For the anihpj/jobpost Dockerfile: COPY --from=builder /root/.local /home/nonroot/.local transfers only the installed Python packages, leaving the gcc compiler and dev headers behind in the builder stage.

💡 DCA Exam Tip: COPY --from=<stage> is the ONLY way to copy between stages. You can also reference external images: COPY --from=nginx:alpine /etc/nginx/nginx.conf /. Cannot use RUN or ADD for cross-stage copies.

Image Creation Q66 of 200
What is the optimal Dockerfile instruction order to maximize layer caching for a Python application?
A) COPY . . → RUN pip install → FROM (any order works the same)
B) FROM → RUN pip install → COPY . . (install before copying code)
C) FROM → COPY . . → RUN pip install (all-in-one layer)
D) FROM → WORKDIR → COPY requirements.txt → RUN pip install → COPY . . (dependencies first, code last)
✅ Correct Answer: D

The golden rule of Docker layer caching: put things that change LEAST often FIRST, things that change MOST often LAST. requirements.txt changes rarely → copied early → pip install layer stays cached. Application code changes frequently → copied last → only the top layer rebuilds.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Layer Caching Optimization — The Dependency-First Pattern
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  OPTIMAL ORDER (fastest builds):                                 │
  │                                                                 │
  │  FROM python:3.12-slim        ← Cached (never changes)          │
  │  WORKDIR /app                 ← Cached (never changes)          │
  │  COPY requirements.txt .      ← Rebuilt ONLY if deps change     │
  │  RUN pip install --no-cache-dir│ ← Rebuilt ONLY if deps change   │
  │      -r requirements.txt      │                                 │
  │  COPY . .                     ← Rebuilt on ANY code change      │
  │  CMD ["gunicorn", "..."]      ← Cached (never changes)          │
  │                                                                 │
  │  BUILD SCENARIOS (anihpj-api):                                  │
  │  ┌──────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ Changed views.py     │ Only last COPY . . rebuilds (~1s)│   │
  │  │ Changed requirements │ pip install rebuilds (~2 min)    │   │
  │  │ Changed Python ver   │ Everything rebuilds (~3 min)      │   │
  │  └──────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────┘   │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For the anihpj/jobpost API, following this order means that changing a Django view (daily occurrence) only rebuilds the last layer (~2 seconds), while changing a Python dependency (weekly occurrence) correctly rebuilds pip install (~2 minutes).

💡 DCA Exam Tip: The dependency-file-first pattern (COPY requirements.txt → RUN pip install → COPY . .) is one of the most tested Dockerfile patterns. Same applies for Node.js (COPY package.json → RUN npm install → COPY . .). Maximize cache hits = faster CI/CD.

Image Creation Q67 of 200
Which technique reduces a Python Docker image from ~1GB to ~50MB?
A) Using docker image compress after build
B) Deleting files from the container after it starts
C) Multi-stage build + distroless or alpine base + pip --no-cache-dir + removing build deps
D) Running the image through gzip before pushing to registry
✅ Correct Answer: C

Shrinking from 1GB to 50MB requires a combination: multi-stage builds (leave compilers behind), minimal base images (alpine or distroless instead of full Debian), --no-cache-dir for pip (don't cache downloaded packages), and explicit cleanup (rm -rf /var/lib/apt/lists/*). Each technique contributes; together they make a dramatic difference.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Image Size Reduction — 1GB → 50MB Journey
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  START: python:3.12 (full Debian)             ~1,000 MB         │
  │    │  ↓ Switch to slim variant                   -850 MB        │
  │    │  python:3.12-slim                          ~150 MB          │
  │    │  ↓ Multi-stage (leave gcc behind)            -50 MB         │
  │    │  Only copy .local/ + /app                   ~100 MB         │
  │    │  ↓ pip --no-cache-dir                        -20 MB         │
  │    │  Don't cache downloaded wheels               ~80 MB         │
  │    │  ↓ Alpine base (musl libc)                   -30 MB         │
  │    │  python:3.12-alpine                          ~50 MB         │
  │    │  ↓ OR: Distroless (glibc, no shell)          ~50 MB         │
  │    │  gcr.io/distroless/python3-debian12                          │
  │                                                                 │
  │  FINAL: anihpj-api → ~50 MB (20x smaller than original!)        │
  │  Bonus: --squash (experimental) merges all layers into one       │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For the anihpj/jobpost API, we went from python:3.12 at 1GB to python:3.12-slim multi-stage at ~80MB — a 12.5x reduction. The production image carries only Django, gunicorn, and psycopg2 — no compilers, no Git, no dev headers.

💡 DCA Exam Tip: The DCA expects you to know multiple image-shrinking techniques: multi-stage builds, minimal base images (alpine/distroless), --no-cache-dir for pip, chain + cleanup in RUN, .dockerignore for smaller build context. Know the tradeoffs of each.

Image Creation Q68 of 200
What is a key characteristic of distroless images?
A) They include a full package manager for installing runtime dependencies
B) They contain only the application and its runtime dependencies — no shell, no package manager, no utilities, minimal attack surface
C) They are distributed without any operating system libraries
D) They automatically update themselves when vulnerabilities are patched
✅ Correct Answer: B

Distroless images (by Google) contain ONLY the application runtime and its required libraries — no shell (no /bin/sh), no package manager (no apt/yum/apk), no Unix utilities (no curl, ls, cat). This dramatically reduces the attack surface: attackers can't spawn shells, install tools, or navigate the filesystem if they compromise the application.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Distroless vs Standard Images — Surface Area Comparison
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  STANDARD (python:3.12):                DISTROLESS:              │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────┐        ┌──────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ ✅ Shell (/bin/bash, /bin/sh)│       │ ❌ NO shell at all    │ │
  │  │ ✅ Package manager (apt)    │        │ ❌ NO package manager │ │
  │  │ ✅ curl, wget, git         │        │ ❌ NO curl, wget, git │ │
  │  │ ✅ ls, cat, cp, mv, find   │        │ ❌ NO Unix utilities  │ │
  │  │ ✅ Python runtime          │        │ ✅ Python runtime     │ │
  │  │ ✅ libc, ca-certificates   │        │ ✅ libc, ca-certs     │ │
  │  │                            │        │ ✅ timezone data      │ │
  │  │ CVEs: ~100+ vulnerabilities│        │ CVEs: 0-2 (minimal)   │ │
  │  │                            │        │                        │ │
  │  │ $ docker exec -it img sh   │        │ $ docker exec -it img  │ │
  │  │ # (attacker has full shell) │        │ Error: No such exe    │ │
  │  └────────────────────────────┘        └──────────────────────┘ │
  │                                                                 │
  │  TRADE-OFF: Great for security, harder to debug.                │
  │  Use for production; use slim for development.                  │
  │  Debug: docker run --entrypoint /bin/sh (on slim image)        │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For the anihpj/jobpost production deployment, we use gcr.io/distroless/python3-debian12:nonroot — it has Python and glibc but no shell. If an attacker compromises the Django app, they can't spawn a shell or install backdoors.

💡 DCA Exam Tip: Distroless = no shell, no package manager, minimal attack surface. You can't docker exec into them (no sh). Use for production; use slim/alpine for dev. Distroless images are maintained by Google and have near-zero CVEs.

Image Creation Q69 of 200
What does BuildKit (DOCKER_BUILDKIT=1) enable that the classic builder does not?
A) Building Windows containers on Linux hosts
B) Running containers without Docker daemon
C) Automatic image signing with Docker Content Trust
D) Parallel stage building, --mount=type=cache for persistent build caches, --mount=type=secret for build-time secrets, and better layer caching
✅ Correct Answer: D

BuildKit is the modern Docker build engine (enabled by default in Docker 23+). It builds independent stages in PARALLEL, skips unused stages, provides --mount=type=cache (persistent package caches across builds), --mount=type=secret (mount secrets during build without them ending up in image layers), and has smarter layer caching.

📝 Detailed Explanation
BuildKit — Modern Build Engine Features
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  BUILDKIT KEY FEATURES:                                         │
  │                                                                 │
  │  1. PARALLEL STAGE BUILDING:                                    │
  │     Stage "builder" ──┐                                          │
  │     Stage "tester"  ──┼── All run simultaneously                │
  │     Stage "linter"  ──┘   (classic: sequential only)            │
  │                                                                 │
  │  2. BUILD-TIME SECRETS (--mount=type=secret):                   │
  │     RUN --mount=type=secret,id=aws_creds \                      │
  │         aws s3 cp s3://bucket/app.tar.gz /app/                  │
  │     → Secret available DURING build, NOT in final image!        │
  │                                                                 │
  │  3. PERSISTENT CACHE (--mount=type=cache):                      │
  │     RUN --mount=type=cache,target=/root/.cache/pip \            │
  │         pip install -r requirements.txt                         │
  │     → pip cache persists across builds (no re-download)         │
  │                                                                 │
  │  4. SMARTER CACHING: Skip unused stages, better invalidation    │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For the anihpj/jobpost CI/CD pipeline, BuildKit's --mount=type=secret lets us use private PyPI credentials during build without them appearing in the image layers — and --mount=type=cache keeps pip downloads cached between CI runs.

💡 DCA Exam Tip: BuildKit is the default builder since Docker 23. Know its key features: parallel stages, --mount=type=secret (build-time secrets that don't leak), --mount=type=cache (persistent package caches). DOCKER_BUILDKIT=1 for older Docker versions.

Image Creation Q70 of 200
Which is the correct multi-stage Dockerfile structure for the anihpj/jobpost Django application?
A) Stage 1 (builder): pip install --user deps → Stage 2 (runtime): COPY --from=builder deps + code, non-root user, gunicorn CMD
B) Single stage with all build tools and runtime tools combined
C) Stage 1: compile Python from source → Stage 2: copy Python binary only
D) Stage 1: download base image → Stage 2: add application code with ADD
✅ Correct Answer: A

The correct production Dockerfile for anihpj-api uses two stages: a builder stage with the full Python image to compile/install dependencies (using pip install --user to isolate them), and a runtime stage with a minimal base (slim or distroless) that copies only the installed deps and application code, then runs as non-root.

📝 Detailed Explanation
anihpj/jobpost — Full Multi-Stage Dockerfile
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  # Stage 1: Builder — install Python dependencies               │
  │  FROM python:3.12-slim@sha256:abc... AS builder                  │
  │  WORKDIR /app                                                   │
  │  COPY requirements.txt .                                        │
  │  RUN pip install --no-cache-dir --user -r requirements.txt     │
  │                                                                 │
  │  # Stage 2: Runtime — minimal production image                  │
  │  FROM gcr.io/distroless/python3-debian12:nonroot                │
  │  COPY --from=builder /root/.local /home/nonroot/.local          │
  │  COPY . /app                                                    │
  │  WORKDIR /app                                                   │
  │  USER 1000:1000                                                 │
  │  ENV PATH="/home/nonroot/.local/bin:$PATH"                      │
  │  ENV PYTHONUNBUFFERED=1                                         │
  │  CMD ["gunicorn", "anihpj.wsgi:application", \                  │
  │       "--bind", "0.0.0.0:8000", "--workers", "4"]               │
  │                                                                 │
  │  RESULT: ~80MB image, NO shell, NO compilers, NO dev headers    │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: The DCA expects you to recognize the correct multi-stage pattern: builder installs deps → runtime copies artifacts → non-root user → minimal base. This pattern applies to Python, Node.js, Go, Java — all languages.

2.3 Image Lifecycle ManagementQ71-Q78

Image Creation Q71 of 200
What does the docker build context refer to?
A) The Dockerfile instructions compiled into the image
B) The directory (or URL) sent to the Docker daemon containing all files available to COPY/ADD instructions
C) The registry where the built image will be pushed
D) The build cache stored on the Docker host
✅ Correct Answer: B

The build context is the set of files at the specified PATH (usually .) that are sent to the Docker daemon before the build starts. Only files within the context can be COPY/ADD'd. Large contexts slow builds — use .dockerignore to exclude unnecessary files.

📝 Detailed Explanation
docker build — Context, Tags, and Caching
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ docker build [OPTIONS] PATH | URL | -                         │
  │                                                                 │
  │  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ 1. Docker CLI reads Dockerfile at PATH                    │ │
  │  │ 2. CLI creates a TAR of the build context (PATH contents) │ │
  │  │ 3. CLI sends tar to Docker daemon                         │ │
  │  │ 4. Daemon builds image layer by layer                     │ │
  │  │ 5. Each instruction checks cache before executing         │ │
  │  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │                                                                 │
  │  KEY FLAGS:                                                     │
  │  -t, --tag anihpj-api:v1      Tag the image                     │
  │  -f, --file Dockerfile.prod   Use a different Dockerfile        │
  │  --no-cache                    Ignore build cache               │
  │  --build-arg VAR=value        Pass build arguments              │
  │  --target stage-name          Stop at a specific stage          │
  │  --pull                        Always pull newer base image    │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For the anihpj/jobpost project, docker build -t anihpj-api:v2 . sends the entire project directory as context. With .dockerignore excluding .git/ and __pycache__/, the context shrinks from ~200MB to ~20MB, speeding up the build.

💡 DCA Exam Tip: The build context is sent to the daemon BEFORE building starts. Only files in the context can be COPY'd. Use .dockerignore to keep contexts small. The context PATH is the last argument to docker build (usually a dot).

Image Creation Q72 of 200
What is the recommended image tagging strategy for production?
A) Always use :latest and rely on pull policies
B) Use only numeric versions like :1, :2, :3
C) Use semantic versioning (:v1.2.3) AND content-addressed digests (@sha256:...) for immutable references
D) Use the Git commit hash as the only tag
✅ Correct Answer: C

Semantic version tags (v1.2.3) provide human-readable versioning. But tags are mutable — someone can push a different image with the same tag. Digests (@sha256:abc123...) are immutable content addresses — they ALWAYS reference the exact same image. Best practice: tag with semver AND reference by digest in production.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Image Tagging Strategy — Tags vs Digests
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  TAG (mutable):                       DIGEST (immutable):        │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────┐    ┌──────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ anihpj-api:v1.2.3            │    │ anihpj-api@sha256:   │  │
  │  │                               │    │ abc123def456...      │  │
  │  │ $ docker push api:v1.2.3     │    │                       │  │
  │  │ $ docker push api:v1.2.3     │    │ Content-addressable!  │  │
  │  │   → Tag now points to DIFF   │    │ Always returns same   │  │
  │  │     image! (tag overwritten) │    │ exact image.          │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────────┘    └──────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                 │
  │  RECOMMENDED STRATEGY (anihpj/jobpost):                         │
  │  $ docker build -t anihpj-api:v2.1.0 .                          │
  │  $ docker push anihpj-api:v2.1.0                                │
  │  $ docker push → ALSO captures digest:                          │
  │    sha256:abc123... ← USE THIS in production YAML               │
  │                                                                 │
  │  In docker-stack.yml:                                           │
  │    image: anihpj-api:v2.1.0@sha256:abc123...                    │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For the anihpj/jobpost production stack, we use both: anihpj-api:v2.1.0 for human readability and @sha256:abc123... for immutability — ensuring that rolling updates pull the exact tested image, never a retagged one.

💡 DCA Exam Tip: Tags are mutable (can be overwritten); digests (@sha256:...) are immutable. For production, use semver tags for humans AND digests for deployment. Never rely on :latest in production — it's a moving target.

Image Creation Q73 of 200
What happens when you docker push an image to a registry?
A) The entire image tarball is uploaded as a single file
B) Only the Dockerfile is uploaded; the registry builds the image
C) The image is encrypted and pushed to Docker Hub by default
D) Each layer is uploaded separately — only layers not already in the registry are transferred
✅ Correct Answer: D

Docker images are composed of layers. When you push, Docker checks which layers already exist in the registry (by content hash) and only uploads NEW layers. This is why pushing an updated image is fast if only the top application layer changed — the base image layers are already cached in the registry.

📝 Detailed Explanation
docker push/pull — Layer-Level Transfer
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  PUSH (only new layers):             PULL (only missing layers): │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────┐        ┌──────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ Image: anihpj-api:v2     │        │ $ docker pull img    │  │
  │  │                          │        │                      │  │
  │  │ Layer 5: app code (NEW)  │──▶     │ Layer 1: EXISTS ✓    │  │
  │  │ Layer 4: pip deps (SAME) │ skip   │ Layer 2: EXISTS ✓    │  │
  │  │ Layer 3: apt pkgs (SAME) │ skip   │ Layer 3: EXISTS ✓    │  │
  │  │ Layer 2: slim base (SAME)│ skip   │ Layer 4: EXISTS ✓    │  │
  │  │ Layer 1: scratch (SAME)  │ skip   │ Layer 5: DOWNLOAD ←  │  │
  │  │                          │        │                      │  │
  │  │ Only Layer 5 uploaded!   │        │ Only Layer 5 pulled! │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────┘        └──────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                 │
  │  This is why small, frequent code changes push/pull FAST.      │
  │  Base image changes = all layers above rebuild = slow push.    │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For the anihpj/jobpost CI/CD pipeline, only the application layer (~5MB) is pushed on each deploy — the Python slim base and pip dependencies layers are already in ECR, making pushes near-instant.

💡 DCA Exam Tip: docker push/pull transfer only NEW layers — existing layers (by content hash) are skipped. This is why layer optimization matters. Push/pull speed depends on how many layers changed, not total image size.

Image Creation Q74 of 200
What command lists all Docker images stored locally?
A) docker image ls (or docker images)
B) docker ls --images
C) docker ps -a --images
D) docker list containers --all
✅ Correct Answer: A

docker image ls (or the older docker images) lists all locally stored images with repository, tag, image ID, creation date, and size. Use docker image ls --filter "dangling=true" to find untagged images (build cache leftovers).

📝 Detailed Explanation
docker image ls — Local Image Inventory
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ docker image ls                                              │
  │  REPOSITORY       TAG       IMAGE ID       CREATED       SIZE  │
  │  anihpj-api       v2.1.0    abc123def456   2 hours ago   85MB  │
  │  anihpj-api       v2.0.0    def789abc123   1 week ago    85MB  │
  │  anihpj-web       latest    456abc789def   3 days ago    25MB  │
  │  python           3.12-slim 789def123abc   2 weeks ago   150MB │
  │  <none>           <none>    xyz111222333   5 days ago    85MB  │
  │                                   ↑ dangling image (untagged)   │
  │                                                                 │
  │  USEFUL FILTERS:                                                │
  │  $ docker image ls --filter "before=anihpj-api:v2.0.0"          │
  │  $ docker image ls --filter "reference=anihpj-*"                │
  │  $ docker image ls --format "{{.Repository}}:{{.Tag}}"          │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: docker image ls = list local images. docker images is the old alias (still works). Dangling images (:) are untagged layers — clean them with docker image prune.

Image Creation Q75 of 200
What does docker image inspect show?
A) Only the image's environment variables
B) A list of containers running from this image
C) Full JSON metadata: layers, exposed ports, environment, entrypoint, command, architecture, creation date, and checksums
D) The Dockerfile used to build the image
✅ Correct Answer: C

docker image inspect returns exhaustive JSON metadata about an image: every layer's digest, all ENV vars, EXPOSEd ports, ENTRYPOINT+CMD, VOLUMEs, WORKDIR, USER, architecture, OS, creation timestamp, repo digests, and more. Use --format with Go templates to extract specific fields.

📝 Detailed Explanation
docker image inspect — JSON Metadata Deep Dive
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ docker image inspect anihpj-api:v2                            │
  │  [                                                               │
  │    {                                                             │
  │      "Id": "sha256:abc123...",                                   │
  │      "RepoTags": ["anihpj-api:v2", "anihpj-api:latest"],         │
  │      "Architecture": "amd64",                                    │
  │      "Os": "linux",                                              │
  │      "Created": "2026-06-14T10:30:00Z",                          │
  │      "ContainerConfig": {                                        │
  │        "Env": ["PATH=/usr/local/bin:...", "DJANGO_ENV=prod"],    │
  │        "ExposedPorts": {"8000/tcp": {}},                         │
  │        "Entrypoint": ["gunicorn"],                               │
  │        "Cmd": ["anihpj.wsgi:application","--bind","0.0.0.0:..."]│
  │        "WorkingDir": "/app",                                     │
  │        "User": "1000"                                            │
  │      },                                                          │
  │      "RootFS": {                                                 │
  │        "Layers": ["sha256:layer1...", "sha256:layer2..."]        │
  │      }                                                           │
  │    }                                                             │
  │  ]                                                               │
  │                                                                 │
  │  GO TEMPLATE (extract specific field):                           │
  │  $ docker image inspect --format='{{.ContainerConfig.User}}' img │
  │  1000                                                           │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: docker inspect works on EVERY Docker object (images, containers, networks, volumes, nodes, services, secrets, configs). docker image inspect = same as docker inspect. Use --format with Go templates for scripting.

Image Creation Q76 of 200
What does docker image history show?
A) The commit history of the Git repository used to build the image
B) Each layer in the image — its size, creation date, and the instruction that created it
C) All containers that have been run from this image
D) The push/pull history between registries
✅ Correct Answer: B

docker image history displays the layer lineage of an image — each layer's ID, when it was created, its size, and the Dockerfile instruction that created it. This is invaluable for understanding why an image is large: find the layer contributing the most bytes and optimize it.

📝 Detailed Explanation
docker image history — Layer-by-Layer Analysis
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ docker image history anihpj-api:v2                            │
  │  IMAGE          CREATED         SIZE      COMMENT               │
  │  abc123def456   2 hours ago     5MB      CMD ["gunicorn"...]    │
  │  def789abc123   2 hours ago     15MB     COPY . .  # app code   │
  │  789abc456def   1 week ago      45MB     RUN pip install -r ... │
  │  456def123abc   2 weeks ago     2MB      COPY requirements.txt  │
  │  123abc789def   2 weeks ago     150MB    FROM python:3.12-slim  │
  │                                                                 │
  │  ANALYSIS:                                                       │
  │  • Base image: 150MB (fixed, can't optimize without changing)   │
  │  • pip install: 45MB (largest writable layer — check for        │
  │    unnecessary deps, use --no-cache-dir)                        │
  │  • App code: 15MB (reasonable)                                  │
  │                                                                 │
  │  $ docker image history --no-trunc → full command text           │
  │  $ docker image history --human   → human-readable sizes       │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: docker image history shows every layer and the instruction that created it. Use it to identify bloated layers. The SIZE column shows the UNIQUE contribution of each layer. --no-trunc shows full commands (useful for debugging).

Image Creation Q77 of 200
What does docker image prune do?
A) Removes ALL images from the local system
B) Compresses all images to save disk space
C) Removes only images that are older than 24 hours
D) Removes dangling images (untagged, <none>:<none>) — use -a to remove ALL unused images
✅ Correct Answer: D

docker image prune removes dangling images — untagged layers with <none>:<none> that accumulate from repeated builds. Adding -a removes ALL unused images (not referenced by any container). This is essential for reclaiming disk space on CI/CD build agents.

📝 Detailed Explanation
docker image prune — Disk Space Cleanup
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ docker image prune                    (dangling only)        │
  │  $ docker image prune -a                 (all unused)            │
  │  $ docker image prune -a --filter "until=24h"  (unused, >24h)  │
  │                                                                 │
  │  DANGLING IMAGES:                     UNUSED IMAGES:             │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────┐        ┌──────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ <none>    <none>   abc123│        │ anihpj-api  v1.0.0    │  │
  │  │ <none>    <none>   def456│        │ (no container uses    │  │
  │  │                            │        │  this version)        │  │
  │  │ Created when:              │        │                       │  │
  │  │ • Rebuilding same tag      │        │ ⚠️ -a removes ALL     │  │
  │  │ • Multi-stage intermediates│        │   unused images!      │  │
  │  │ • docker pull ...          │        └──────────────────────┘  │
  │  └──────────────────────────┘                                   │
  │                                                                 │
  │  SYSTEM-WIDE CLEANUP:                                           │
  │  $ docker system prune -a           → Images + containers +     │
  │                                        networks + cache         │
  │  $ docker system df                 → Show disk usage first!    │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For the anihpj/jobpost CI/CD pipeline, add docker image prune -a -f --filter "until=72h" as a cron job on build agents to prevent disk exhaustion from accumulated build layers.

💡 DCA Exam Tip: docker image prune = dangling images only (:). docker image prune -a = ALL unused images. Always run docker image ls first to see what you'll remove. docker system df shows disk usage by Docker objects.

Image Creation Q78 of 200
When would you use docker save and docker load?
A) To export an image as a .tar file for air-gapped environments or offline transfer, then import it on another machine
B) To backup and restore Docker volumes
C) To compress images before pushing to a registry
D) To migrate containers between Docker and Kubernetes
✅ Correct Answer: A

docker save exports an image (with all layers and tags) to a tar archive. docker load imports from a tar archive back into the local image store. This is used for air-gapped environments (no registry access), offline transfers via USB drive, or archiving specific image versions.

📝 Detailed Explanation
docker save/load vs docker export/import
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  SAVE/LOAD (images):               EXPORT/IMPORT (containers):   │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────┐      ┌──────────────────────┐    │
  │  │ $ docker save -o img.tar │      │ $ docker export -o    │    │
  │  │   anihpj-api:v2          │      │   container.tar c1    │    │
  │  │                          │      │                       │    │
  │  │ $ docker load -i img.tar │      │ $ docker import       │    │
  │  │ → Preserves: ALL layers, │      │   container.tar       │    │
  │  │   tags, metadata, history│      │ → FLATTENS all layers │    │
  │  │                          │      │   into ONE layer!     │    │
  │  └──────────────────────────┘      │   Loses: history,     │    │
  │                                      │   metadata, tags     │    │
  │  USE SAVE/LOAD for:                └──────────────────────┘    │
  │  • Air-gapped deployments                                        │
  │  • Image archival                                              │
  │  • Transfer via USB/physical media                              │
  │  • CI/CD artifact storage                                      │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For the anihpj/jobpost, if deploying to an on-premise server with no internet access, you'd docker save anihpj-api:v2 -o anihpj-api-v2.tar on your build machine, transfer via USB, then docker load -i anihpj-api-v2.tar on the production server.

💡 DCA Exam Tip: save/load preserves layers, tags, and history (for IMAGES). export/import flattens to one layer and loses history (for CONTAINERS). Know when to use each. save → load is the air-gapped registry alternative.

2.4 Enterprise RegistriesQ79-Q86

Image Creation Q79 of 200
What is Docker Hub?
A) A desktop application for managing Docker containers
B) A Kubernetes distribution by Docker Inc.
C) Docker's public cloud registry — the default registry where docker pull/push goes, hosting millions of container images
D) A monitoring dashboard for Docker Swarm clusters
✅ Correct Answer: C

Docker Hub is the world's largest public container registry — the default destination for docker pull and docker push. It hosts official images (maintained by Docker or vendors), verified publisher images, and community images. It's the "npm registry" or "PyPI" of the container world.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Docker Hub — The Default Public Registry
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ docker pull python:3.12-slim                                 │
  │  → Actually pulls from: docker.io/library/python:3.12-slim      │
  │  → docker.io = Docker Hub's registry hostname                   │
  │                                                                 │
  │  IMAGE TYPES ON DOCKER HUB:                                     │
  │  ┌──────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ Official Images  │ Docker-maintained (python, nginx,     │  │
  │  │                  │ postgres, redis, node)                 │  │
  │  ├──────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────┤  │
  │  │ Verified Publisher│ Vendor-maintained (bitnami, grafana, │  │
  │  │                  │ elastic, hashicorp)                    │  │
  │  ├──────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────┤  │
  │  │ Community        │ Anyone can publish (quality varies)   │  │
  │  └──────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                 │
  │  RATE LIMITS (unauthenticated): 100 pulls/6 hours               │
  │  RATE LIMITS (authenticated):    200 pulls/6 hours (free)       │
  │  SOLUTION: docker login + registry mirrors for CI/CD            │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: Docker Hub is the default registry (docker.io). docker pull python:3.12 = docker pull docker.io/library/python:3.12. Know about rate limits (100/6h unauthenticated) and how to avoid them (login, mirror).

Image Creation Q80 of 200
How do you authenticate to a private Docker registry?
A) Add credentials to the Dockerfile with ENV
B) Pass --username and --password flags to docker push
C) Store credentials in docker-compose.yml
D) docker login <registry-url> — credentials are stored in ~/.docker/config.json (base64-encoded)
✅ Correct Answer: D

docker login authenticates to a registry and stores credentials in ~/.docker/config.json — base64-encoded (NOT encrypted). For CI/CD, use --password-stdin to avoid credentials appearing in shell history or process lists: echo "$TOKEN" | docker login -u user --password-stdin.

📝 Detailed Explanation
docker login — Registry Authentication
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  DOCKER HUB:                          PRIVATE REGISTRY:          │
  │  $ docker login                       $ docker login myreg.com  │
  │  Username: anihsdev                   Username: admin            │
  │  Password: ********                   Password: ********        │
  │  Login Succeeded                      Login Succeeded           │
  │                                                                 │
  │  CREDENTIAL STORAGE (~/.docker/config.json):                    │
  │  {                                                               │
  │    "auths": {                                                    │
  │      "https://index.docker.io/v1/": {                           │
  │        "auth": "YW5paHM6cGFzc3dvcmQ="  ← base64(user:pass)     │
  │      }                                                           │
  │    }                                                             │
  │  }                                                               │
  │                                                                 │
  │  ⚠️ SECURITY: base64 ≠ encryption! Anyone with file access     │
  │     can decode credentials. Use credential helpers for safety.  │
  │                                                                 │
  │  CI/CD BEST PRACTICE:                                           │
  │  $ echo "$DOCKER_TOKEN" | docker login -u $USER --password-stdin│
  │  → Password never appears in logs or process list               │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For the anihpj/jobpost CI/CD pipeline, we store ECR/Docker Hub credentials in GitHub Secrets and pipe them via --password-stdin, never exposing them in build logs or shell history.

💡 DCA Exam Tip: docker login stores creds in ~/.docker/config.json (base64, not encrypted). Always use --password-stdin in CI/CD. docker logout removes credentials. Credential helpers (docker-credential-ecr-login) are available for cloud registries.

Image Creation Q81 of 200
What is a Docker registry mirror (pull-through cache)?
A) A backup copy of Docker Hub maintained by Docker Inc.
B) A local registry that caches images from Docker Hub — first pull downloads to cache, subsequent pulls come from cache at LAN speed
C) A feature that automatically pushes images to multiple registries simultaneously
D) A Docker Desktop feature that mirrors containers between Windows and Linux
✅ Correct Answer: B

A registry mirror is a local Docker registry configured as a pull-through cache. When Docker pulls an image, it first checks the mirror. If the image is cached (already pulled once), it's served at LAN speed (gigabit) instead of internet speed. This also reduces Docker Hub rate limit consumption.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Registry Mirror — Pull-Through Cache Architecture
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  WITHOUT MIRROR:                     WITH MIRROR:                │
  │  ┌────────────────────┐             ┌────────────────────┐     │
  │  │ CI Agent           │             │ CI Agent           │     │
  │  │ $ docker pull ...  │             │ $ docker pull ...  │     │
  │  │       │            │             │       │            │     │
  │  │       ▼            │             │       ▼            │     │
  │  │  Docker Hub        │             │  Mirror (local)    │     │
  │  │  (internet, slow)  │             │  ┌──────────────┐  │     │
  │  │  500ms latency     │             │  │ Cache HIT?   │  │     │
  │  │  Rate limited      │             │  │ YES→LAN speed│  │     │
  │  └────────────────────┘             │  │ NO→fetch from│  │     │
  │                                      │  │ Docker Hub   │  │     │
  │  CONFIG (daemon.json):               │  └──────────────┘  │     │
  │  {                                   └────────────────────┘     │
  │    "registry-mirrors": [                                        │
  │      "https://mirror.local:5000"                               │
  │    ]                                                             │
  │  }                                                               │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For the anihpj/jobpost team, setting up a registry mirror reduces image pull times from 2 minutes (internet) to 5 seconds (LAN) and eliminates Docker Hub rate limit issues during busy CI/CD days.

💡 DCA Exam Tip: Registry mirrors are configured in daemon.json under "registry-mirrors". They act as pull-through caches — first pull = slow fetch from Hub; subsequent pulls = fast LAN speed. Essential for CI/CD environments with rate limits.

Image Creation Q82 of 200
What is Mirantis Secure Registry (MSR, formerly Docker Trusted Registry)?
A) An enterprise-grade private registry with RBAC, image signing, vulnerability scanning, immutable tags, and geo-replication
B) A free, open-source alternative to Docker Hub
C) A Kubernetes operator for managing container images
D) Docker's built-in local image cache
✅ Correct Answer: A

MSR (Mirantis Secure Registry) is the enterprise registry component of the Mirantis suite (formerly Docker Enterprise's DTR). It provides: role-based access control (RBAC) on repositories, image signing with Docker Content Trust, automated vulnerability scanning, immutable tags, garbage collection, and geo-replication across data centers.

📝 Detailed Explanation
MSR — Enterprise Registry Features
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  MIRANTIS SECURE REGISTRY (MSR) — Enterprise Image Management:  │
  │                                                                 │
  │  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ • RBAC: Teams, users, roles per repository                │ │
  │  │ • Image Signing: Docker Content Trust (Notary)            │ │
  │  │ • Vulnerability Scanning: Per-image, per-layer CVE scan   │ │
  │  │ • Immutable Tags: Prevent production tag overwrites       │ │
  │  │ • Garbage Collection: Auto-remove untagged manifests      │ │
  │  │ • Geo-Replication: Replicate images across datacenters    │ │
  │  │ • LDAP/AD/SAML: Enterprise identity integration           │ │
  │  │ • Prometheus Metrics: Monitoring and alerting             │ │
  │  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │                                                                 │
  │  MSR vs HARBOR (open-source alternative):                       │
  │  MSR = commercial, full Mirantis suite integration              │
  │  Harbor = CNCF open-source, similar features, K8s-native       │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: MSR (Mirantis Secure Registry) = enterprise private registry. Formerly Docker DTR. Features: RBAC, image signing, vulnerability scanning, geo-replication. Know that it replaced Docker Trusted Registry after the Mirantis acquisition.

Image Creation Q83 of 200
What is Docker Content Trust (DCT)?
A) A feature that encrypts all container network traffic
B) A license verification system for Docker Enterprise
C) A system for signing and verifying image integrity — ensures images haven't been tampered with between publisher and consumer
D) A blockchain-based image registry
✅ Correct Answer: C

Docker Content Trust (DCT) uses Notary and The Update Framework (TUF) to sign images with publisher keys. When enabled (DOCKER_CONTENT_TRUST=1), Docker verifies signatures before pulling — rejecting unsigned or tampered images. This prevents supply chain attacks where a registry is compromised and images are replaced.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Docker Content Trust — Image Signing Flow
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  PUBLISHER SIDE:                      CONSUMER SIDE:             │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────┐        ┌──────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ $ export DOCKER_CONTENT_ │        │ $ export DOCKER_      │  │
  │  │   TRUST=1                │        │   CONTENT_TRUST=1     │  │
  │  │ $ docker push anihpj:v2  │        │ $ docker pull anihpj  │  │
  │  │                          │        │   :v2                 │  │
  │  │ → Signs image with       │        │                        │  │
  │  │   publisher's private key│        │ → Verifies signature   │  │
  │  │ → Pushes signature to    │        │   against publisher's  │  │
  │  │   Notary server          │        │   public key           │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────┘        │                        │  │
  │                                       │ ✅ Signature valid?    │  │
  │  KEY MANAGEMENT:                     │   → Pull succeeds     │  │
  │  • Root key (offline, air-gapped)    │ ❌ No signature?      │  │
  │  • Repository key (per-repo)         │   → Pull REJECTED!   │  │
  │  • Timestamp key (short-lived)       └──────────────────────┘  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For the anihpj/jobpost production pipeline, enabling DCT ensures that only images signed by the CI/CD pipeline are deployed — preventing supply chain attacks where a compromised registry could serve malicious images.

💡 DCA Exam Tip: DOCKER_CONTENT_TRUST=1 enables image signing and verification. When enabled, docker pull rejects unsigned images. Uses Notary + TUF under the hood. DCT is a critical supply chain security feature — know it for the DCA.

Image Creation Q84 of 200
What does image vulnerability scanning do?
A) Checks if the image runs without errors
B) Verifies that the image was built from a valid Dockerfile
C) Ensures the image is smaller than a maximum size threshold
D) Analyzes each layer against CVE databases to identify known vulnerabilities in installed packages — shifting security left into the build pipeline
✅ Correct Answer: D

Image vulnerability scanners (Docker Scout, Trivy, Snyk, Clair) compare installed packages in each image layer against public CVE databases (NVD, OSV). They identify known vulnerabilities BEFORE deployment — enabling "shift-left" security where issues are caught in CI/CD, not in production.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Image Vulnerability Scanning — Shift-Left Security
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  SCANNING TOOLS:                                                 │
  │  ┌──────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ Docker   │ Built-in (docker scout quickview)             │   │
  │  │ Scout    │ Policy evaluation, SBOM generation            │   │
  │  ├──────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────┤   │
  │  │ Trivy    │ Open-source (Aqua), fast, CI-friendly         │   │
  │  │ Snyk     │ Commercial, broad language support            │   │
  │  │ Clair    │ Used by ECR, Quay; static analysis            │   │
  │  └──────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                 │
  │  CI/CD INTEGRATION (anihpj/jobpost):                            │
  │  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ 1. docker build -t anihpj-api:$VERSION .                   │ │
  │  │ 2. docker scout quickview anihpj-api:$VERSION             │ │
  │  │ 3. If CRITICAL CVEs → FAIL build, notify team             │ │
  │  │ 4. If clean → docker push anihpj-api:$VERSION             │ │
  │  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │                                                                 │
  │  CVE SEVERITY: Critical > High > Medium > Low > Negligible      │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For the anihpj/jobpost CI/CD pipeline, we run docker scout quickview anihpj-api:$VERSION after every build — if any CRITICAL or HIGH CVEs are found, the pipeline fails and the image is never pushed to production.

💡 DCA Exam Tip: Docker Scout is Docker's built-in vulnerability scanner. Other tools: Trivy (open-source, CI-friendly), Snyk (commercial), Clair (ECR backend). Know that scanning should happen BEFORE pushing to registry and be integrated into CI/CD.

Image Creation Q85 of 200
Why should you use immutable tags in production registries?
A) Immutable tags improve image download speed
B) They prevent a tag from being overwritten — ensuring :v2.0.0 always points to the same image, eliminating "works on my machine" inconsistencies
C) They automatically delete old images when new ones are pushed
D) They encrypt the image layers for secure transfer
✅ Correct Answer: B

Without immutable tags, someone can push a different image with the same tag — and different nodes in a Swarm cluster might have different versions of "the same tag" cached. Immutable tags guarantee that once pushed, anihpj-api:v2.0.0 will ALWAYS refer to exactly the same image. Combined with digest references, this provides deterministic deployments.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Immutable Tags — Deterministic Deployments
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  MUTABLE TAG (dangerous):              IMMUTABLE TAG (safe):     │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────┐         ┌──────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ $ docker push api:v2.0   │         │ $ docker push api:v2 │ │
  │  │ → Tag points to img#abc  │         │ → Tag = img#abc      │ │
  │  │                          │         │                       │ │
  │  │ $ docker push api:v2.0   │         │ $ docker push api:v2 │ │
  │  │ → Tag NOW points to      │         │ → ERROR: Tag already │ │
  │  │   img#def (different!)   │         │   exists (immutable) │ │
  │  │                          │         │                       │ │
  │  │ Node1: cached img#abc    │         │ All nodes: same image│ │
  │  │ Node2: cached img#def    │         │ Deterministic!       │ │
  │  │ → INCONSISTENT! 😱       │         └──────────────────────┘ │
  │  └──────────────────────────┘                                   │
  │                                                                 │
  │  BEST PRACTICE: Use unique version tags (v2.0.1, v2.0.2)       │
  │  with immutable tags enabled + deploy by digest for safety.     │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

At anihpj/jobpost, we enable immutable tags on ECR and use unique semver tags (v2.1.0, v2.1.1) — never reusing a tag. For deployments, we also pin to digests for absolute determinism.

💡 DCA Exam Tip: Immutable tags prevent tag overwrites — critical for production consistency. Combine with digest references (@sha256:...) for absolute determinism. Available on ECR, ACR, MSR, and Harbor. Not available on Docker Hub free tier.

Image Creation Q86 of 200
What is the purpose of docker scout commands?
A) To search for images on Docker Hub
B) To monitor container resource usage in real-time
C) To analyze images for vulnerabilities, generate SBOMs, and evaluate policies — Docker's built-in supply chain security tool
D) To deploy images to cloud platforms automatically
✅ Correct Answer: C

docker scout is Docker's built-in supply chain security tool. It analyzes images for known vulnerabilities (CVEs), generates Software Bill of Materials (SBOM), evaluates images against compliance policies, and provides fix recommendations — all integrated into the Docker CLI.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Docker Scout — Supply Chain Security Commands
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ docker scout quickview anihpj-api:v2   (fast CVE summary)    │
  │  $ docker scout cves anihpj-api:v2        (detailed CVE list)   │
  │  $ docker scout recommendations anihpj-api:v2  (fix suggestions) │
  │  $ docker scout compare anihpj-api:v1..v2  (diff two images)    │
  │  $ docker scout policy anihpj-api:v2      (compliance check)    │
  │                                                                 │
  │  SCOUT OUTPUT (quickview):                                      │
  │  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ Image: anihpj-api:v2                                      │ │
  │  │ Base image: python:3.12-slim                              │ │
  │  │                                                            │ │
  │  │ CVEs: 0 Critical, 2 High, 5 Medium, 12 Low                │ │
  │  │ Packages: 156                                              │ │
  │  │ Last push: 2 hours ago                                     │ │
  │  │                                                            │ │
  │  │ Recommendations:                                           │ │
  │  │ • Update python:3.12-slim → 3.12.4-slim (fixes 2 High)    │ │
  │  │ • Remove unused libexpat1 (not needed at runtime)         │ │
  │  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For the anihpj/jobpost CI/CD pipeline, docker scout policy gates production deployments — if the image violates compliance policies (no critical CVEs, base image must be approved), the pipeline fails.

💡 DCA Exam Tip: docker scout is Docker's supply chain security tool. Know the key commands: quickview (summary), cves (detail), recommendations (fixes), policy (compliance gates). It's free for Docker Personal/Pro and integrated into Docker Desktop.

2.5 Image Building Best PracticesQ87-Q90

Image Creation Q87 of 200
What does "shifting security left" mean in the context of Docker images?
A) Moving security scanning from Linux to Windows
B) Integrating vulnerability scanning, policy checks, and signing into the CI/CD pipeline — catching issues BEFORE they reach production
C) Using only images from the left side of Docker Hub search results
D) Running security tools inside the container instead of on the host
✅ Correct Answer: B

"Shift left" means moving security checks earlier in the development lifecycle — from post-deployment scanning (right) to CI/CD build time (left). Instead of discovering vulnerabilities after they're in production, you fail the build if an image has critical CVEs, isn't signed, or violates compliance policies.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Shift-Left Security — Catch Issues in CI/CD, Not Production
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  TRADITIONAL (RIGHT):                  SHIFT-LEFT (MODERN):       │
  │  Code → Build → Deploy → SCAN → 😱    Code → SCAN → Build →     │
  │  (find CVEs in production!)              SCAN → Sign → Deploy ✅ │
  │                                                                 │
  │  CI/CD PIPELINE WITH SHIFT-LEFT SECURITY:                       │
  │  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ 1. Git push → trigger pipeline                            │ │
  │  │ 2. docker build -t anihpj-api:$SHA .                      │ │
  │  │ 3. docker scout cves anihpj-api:$SHA  ← SHIFT-LEFT scan   │ │
  │  │    ├─ CRITICAL CVEs → FAIL pipeline 🛑                    │ │
  │  │    └─ Clean → continue                                    │ │
  │  │ 4. docker scout policy anihpj-api:$SHA  ← policy check   │ │
  │  │ 5. DOCKER_CONTENT_TRUST=1 docker push ...  ← sign image   │ │
  │  │ 6. docker stack deploy -c stack.yml anihpj                │ │
  │  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For the anihpj/jobpost pipeline, shifting left means: scan for CVEs → fail if critical → run policy check → sign with DCT → only then deploy. This catches security issues minutes after code is pushed, not days later in production.

💡 DCA Exam Tip: "Shift left" = security earlier in the lifecycle. Docker Scout, Trivy, and DCT are shift-left tools. The DCA expects you to understand that security scanning should happen in CI/CD pipelines, not just in production monitoring.

Image Creation Q88 of 200
Which technique does NOT reduce an image's attack surface?
A) Using a minimal base image (alpine or distroless)
B) Removing unnecessary packages and build tools in the same RUN layer
C) Running as a non-root USER
D) Adding more layers with COPY to separate application components
✅ Correct Answer: D

Adding more layers does NOT reduce attack surface — it has no effect on what's IN the image, just how it's structured. Reducing attack surface means: fewer packages (less code = fewer vulnerabilities), smaller base images (alpine with 5 packages vs Debian with 200+), running as non-root (limits blast radius), and removing build tools after compilation.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Attack Surface Reduction — What Works (and What Doesn't)
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ✅ REDUCES attack surface:          ❌ Does NOT reduce:         │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────┐   ┌──────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ • Minimal base image (alpine,│   │ • More COPY layers    │   │
  │  │   distroless, scratch)       │   │   (same total content)│   │
  │  │ • Remove build tools (gcc,   │   │                        │   │
  │  │   git, make) after compile   │   │ • Splitting RUN into  │   │
  │  │ • Non-root USER              │   │   multiple layers     │   │
  │  │ • --no-install-recommends    │   │   (same installed pkgs)│   │
  │  │   (apt-get)                  │   │                        │   │
  │  │ • .dockerignore (no .git,    │   │ • Adding comments to   │   │
  │  │   .env in image)             │   │   Dockerfile          │   │
  │  │ • Pinned versions by digest  │   └──────────────────────┘   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────┘                               │
  │                                                                 │
  │  ATTACK SURFACE = Total packages + running as root + open ports │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For the anihpj/jobpost API image, we reduce attack surface by using distroless (no shell), running as UID 1000, and keeping only Python + gunicorn + psycopg2 — no compilers, no curl, no package manager.

💡 DCA Exam Tip: Attack surface = what an attacker can exploit. Reduce it by: minimal base images, non-root users, removing build tools, pinning by digest. Layer count doesn't affect attack surface — only what's IN the layers matters.

Image Creation Q89 of 200
Why should you pin base images by digest (@sha256:...) instead of just tags?
A) Tags are mutable — :latest or :3.12 can point to different images over time; digests are immutable content addresses that guarantee you get the EXACT same image every time
B) Digests download faster than tags from registries
C) Tags require authentication; digests work anonymously
D) Digests enable automatic vulnerability patching
✅ Correct Answer: A

Tags like python:3.12-slim are mutable — the maintainer can push a new image with the same tag at any time. Digests (@sha256:abc123...) are cryptographic hashes of the image's content — they ALWAYS reference exactly the same bits. Pinning by digest provides reproducible builds and prevents supply chain surprises.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Digest Pinning — Reproducible, Immutable References
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  TAG (mutable, risky):                 DIGEST (immutable, safe): │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────┐     ┌─────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ FROM python:3.12-slim        │     │ FROM python:3.12-    │  │
  │  │                              │     │   slim@sha256:abc... │  │
  │  │ Build today: → python 3.12.0 │     │                      │  │
  │  │ Build tomorrow: → python     │     │ Build today: 3.12.0  │  │
  │  │   3.12.4 (DIFFERENT image!) │     │ Build in 1 year:     │  │
  │  │                              │     │   STILL 3.12.0!     │  │
  │  │ ❌ Not reproducible!         │     │ ✅ Fully reproducible│  │
  │  └──────────────────────────────┘     └─────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                 │
  │  HOW TO GET THE DIGEST:                                         │
  │  $ docker pull python:3.12-slim                                 │
  │  $ docker image inspect python:3.12-slim \                      │
  │      --format='{{.RepoDigests}}'                                │
  │  [python:3.12-slim@sha256:abc123def456...]                      │
  │                                                                 │
  │  RENOVATE/DEPENDABOT: Auto-update digests in Dockerfiles        │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For the anihpj/jobpost Dockerfile, we use FROM python:3.12-slim@sha256:abc123... — ensuring ALL builds (dev, CI, production) use the exact same base image until we deliberately update the digest.

💡 DCA Exam Tip: Digests (@sha256:...) = immutable, deterministic. Tags = mutable, can change. Always pin by digest in production Dockerfiles. Use tools like Renovate or Dependabot to automate digest updates. This is a key DCA best practice.

Image Creation Q90 of 200
What is an SBOM (Software Bill of Materials) and why is it important for Docker images?
A) A license file listing all open-source components and their costs
B) A configuration file that defines how to build the Docker image
C) A machine-readable inventory of all packages, libraries, and dependencies in an image — critical for vulnerability management and supply chain transparency
D) A Docker Compose file that documents all services in a stack
✅ Correct Answer: C

An SBOM is like a nutrition label for your container image — it lists every software component, version, and dependency inside. When a CVE is announced (e.g., "OpenSSL 3.0.x has a vulnerability"), you can query your SBOM to instantly determine which images are affected, rather than manually checking hundreds of images.

📝 Detailed Explanation
SBOM — Software Bill of Materials for Containers
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ docker sbom anihpj-api:v2                                     │
  │                                                                 │
  │  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ NAME             VERSION      TYPE         LICENSE         │ │
  │  ├───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤ │
  │  │ Python           3.12.4       interpreter  PSF             │ │
  │  │ Django           5.0.7        python       BSD-3           │ │
  │  │ gunicorn         22.0.0       python       MIT             │ │
  │  │ psycopg2         2.9.9        python       LGPL            │ │
  │  │ redis            5.0.7        python       MIT             │ │
  │  │ libssl3          3.0.13       deb          Apache-2.0      │ │
  │  │ libc6            2.36         deb          LGPL-2.1        │ │
  │  │ ...                                                         │ │
  │  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │                                                                 │
  │  SBOM FORMATS: SPDX, CycloneDX, Syft JSON                       │
  │  REGULATORY: US Executive Order 14028 requires SBOMs for        │
  │  federal software — becoming mandatory across industries.       │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For the anihpj/jobpost project, we generate an SBOM with docker sbom anihpj-api:v2 --format spdx-json > sbom.json and store it alongside the image — enabling instant CVE impact analysis when new vulnerabilities are disclosed.

💡 DCA Exam Tip: docker sbom generates a Software Bill of Materials — a list of all packages in an image. SBOMs are becoming mandatory for compliance (US EO 14028). Formats: SPDX, CycloneDX. Tools: docker sbom, Syft, Trivy. Know what an SBOM is and why it matters.

⚙️ Category 3: Installation & Configuration15% — Q91-Q120

🔧 Category 3: Installation & Configuration — 30 Questions

This category is about setting up Docker itself — installing the engine, configuring the daemon, setting up logging, and managing the Docker environment. Think of it like setting up your workshop before you build. Master Docker Engine installation, daemon.json configuration, logging drivers, and enterprise tooling. 15% of the DCA exam.

3.1 Docker Engine InstallationQ91-Q98

Installation Q91 of 200
What is the recommended method to install Docker Engine on Ubuntu?
A) sudo apt-get install docker (from Ubuntu's default repositories)
B) Download the .deb package from docker.com and install with dpkg
C) Use snap install docker
D) Set up Docker's apt repository, then apt-get install docker-ce (Docker Community Edition)
✅ Correct Answer: D

Docker's official apt repository provides the latest, stable Docker Engine (docker-ce). Ubuntu's default repositories contain an old, unmaintained docker.io package. The snap version lacks features. Always add Docker's GPG key and apt repository for production installations.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Docker Engine Installation — Ubuntu Step-by-Step
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  # 1. Remove old versions                                       │
  │  $ sudo apt-get remove docker docker.io containerd runc         │
  │                                                                 │
  │  # 2. Add Docker's official GPG key                             │
  │  $ sudo apt-get update                                          │
  │  $ sudo apt-get install ca-certificates curl                    │
  │  $ sudo install -m 0755 -d /etc/apt/keyrings                    │
  │  $ curl -fsSL https://download.docker.com/linux/ubuntu/gpg | \  │
  │    sudo gpg --dearmor -o /etc/apt/keyrings/docker.gpg           │
  │                                                                 │
  │  # 3. Add the repository                                        │
  │  $ echo "deb [signed-by=/etc/apt/keyrings/docker.gpg] \         │
  │    https://download.docker.com/linux/ubuntu \                   │
  │    $(lsb_release -cs) stable" | \                               │
  │    sudo tee /etc/apt/sources.list.d/docker.list                 │
  │                                                                 │
  │  # 4. Install Docker Engine                                     │
  │  $ sudo apt-get update                                          │
  │  $ sudo apt-get install docker-ce docker-ce-cli \               │
  │      containerd.io docker-buildx-plugin docker-compose-plugin   │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: ALWAYS install from Docker's official repository, NOT the OS default (apt-get install docker). The OS package is outdated. Know the package names: docker-ce (engine), docker-ce-cli (CLI), containerd.io (runtime). docker-buildx-plugin and docker-compose-plugin are separate.

Installation Q92 of 200
How do you install Docker on CentOS/RHEL?
A) sudo yum install docker (from default repos)
B) Add Docker's yum repository (yum-config-manager --add-repo), then yum install docker-ce
C) Use the Docker Universal Install Script: curl -fsSL https://get.docker.com | sh
D) Docker only supports Debian/Ubuntu; use Podman on RHEL
✅ Correct Answer: B

For RHEL/CentOS, add Docker's yum repository using yum-config-manager --add-repo https://download.docker.com/linux/rhel/docker-ce.repo, then install with yum install docker-ce docker-ce-cli containerd.io. The convenience script (get.docker.com) works but is not recommended for production.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Docker on RHEL/CentOS vs Ubuntu — Installation Comparison
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  UBUNTU/DEBIAN:                      RHEL/CENTOS/ALMA:           │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────┐      ┌──────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ Package mgr: apt-get       │      │ Package mgr: yum/dnf │   │
  │  │ Repo: download.docker.com  │      │ Repo: download.docker│   │
  │  │   /linux/ubuntu            │      │   .com/linux/rhel    │   │
  │  │                            │      │                       │   │
  │  │ $ sudo apt-get install \   │      │ $ sudo yum install \  │   │
