  │  │   docker-ce \              │      │   docker-ce \         │   │
  │  │   docker-ce-cli \          │      │   docker-ce-cli \     │   │
  │  │   containerd.io            │      │   containerd.io       │   │
  │  └────────────────────────────┘      └──────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                 │
  │  ⚠️ CONVENIENCE SCRIPT (NOT for production):                    │
  │  $ curl -fsSL https://get.docker.com | sh                       │
  │  → Good for dev/test, but no GPG verification, no version pinning│
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: The convenience script (get.docker.com) is for development only — NOT for production. Production requires the official repository with GPG verification. Know both yum and apt installation methods. The package names are the same across distros.

Installation Q93 of 200
What does Docker Desktop for Windows use to run Linux containers?
A) Hyper-V only — requires a full Linux VM
B) Native Windows kernel container support
C) WSL2 (Windows Subsystem for Linux 2) — a lightweight VM with a real Linux kernel managed by Windows
D) VirtualBox with a pre-configured Linux image
✅ Correct Answer: C

Docker Desktop on Windows uses WSL2 as its backend — a lightweight virtual machine running a real Linux kernel tightly integrated with Windows. This provides near-native performance, filesystem sharing, and full Docker compatibility. WSL2 replaced the older Hyper-V backend.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Docker Desktop Architecture — Windows + WSL2
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  WINDOWS HOST                                                    │
  │  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │                    DOCKER DESKTOP                          │ │
  │  │  ┌─────────────────────┐    ┌─────────────────────────┐  │ │
  │  │  │ Docker CLI (Win)    │    │ WSL2 Backend             │  │ │
  │  │  │ docker.exe          │◀──▶│ ┌─────────────────────┐ │  │ │
  │  │  └─────────────────────┘    │ │ Docker Daemon       │ │  │ │
  │  │                              │ │ (dockerd)           │ │  │ │
  │  │  /mnt/c/ → Windows files    │ │ containerd → runc  │ │  │ │
  │  │  \\wsl$\ → Linux files      │ │                     │ │  │ │
  │  │                              │ │ Linux Kernel (real!)│ │  │ │
  │  │                              │ └─────────────────────┘ │  │ │
  │  │                              └─────────────────────────┘  │ │
  │  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │                                                                 │
  │  WSL2 vs HYPER-V (legacy):                                      │
  │  • WSL2: faster I/O, automatic file sharing, lower memory       │
  │  • Hyper-V: isolated VM, slower file sharing, deprecated        │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: Docker Desktop on Windows uses WSL2 (not Hyper-V since 2020+). On macOS, it uses a lightweight Linux VM via Apple's Hypervisor.framework. Know that Docker Desktop includes Docker Engine, CLI, Compose, Kubernetes, and GUI — all in one package.

Installation Q94 of 200
After installing Docker on Linux, why can't a regular user run docker ps without sudo?
A) The docker group doesn't exist or the user isn't in it — /var/run/docker.sock is owned by root:docker by default
B) Docker requires a paid license for non-root users
C) The Docker daemon only accepts connections from root
D) Docker commands must always be prefixed with sudo on Linux
✅ Correct Answer: A

The Docker daemon listens on /var/run/docker.sock, which is owned by root:docker. Users must be in the docker group to access the socket. Post-installation: sudo usermod -aG docker $USER then log out and back in. Note: being in the docker group effectively gives root access — use with caution.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Post-Installation — Docker Group & Socket Access
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ ls -la /var/run/docker.sock                                  │
  │  srw-rw---- 1 root docker 0 Jun 14 10:30 /var/run/docker.sock │
  │                 │     │                                          │
  │                 │     └── Group: docker (rw access)              │
  │                 └── Owner: root (rw access)                      │
  │                                                                 │
  │  POST-INSTALL STEPS:                                            │
  │  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ 1. Create docker group (usually auto-created):            │ │
  │  │    $ sudo groupadd docker                                 │ │
  │  │                                                           │ │
  │  │ 2. Add your user to docker group:                         │ │
  │  │    $ sudo usermod -aG docker $USER                        │ │
  │  │                                                           │ │
  │  │ 3. Log out and back in (or newgrp docker)                 │ │
  │  │                                                           │ │
  │  │ 4. Test:                                                  │ │
  │  │    $ docker run hello-world                               │ │
  │  │    Hello from Docker!                                     │ │
  │  │                                                           │ │
  │  │ ⚠️ SECURITY: docker group ≈ root access.                  │ │
  │  │    Only add trusted users.                                │ │
  │  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: The docker group is created at installation. Users must be added to it to run docker without sudo. docker.sock is owned by root:docker. WARNING: docker group membership effectively grants root access — be selective.

Installation Q95 of 200
What is the difference between docker version and docker info?
A) docker version shows image versions; docker info shows container details
B) docker version is for Docker Hub; docker info is for the local daemon
C) They show the same information — docker info is just more verbose
D) docker version shows Client + Server versions; docker info shows daemon configuration — storage driver, logging driver, registries, Swarm status, and more
✅ Correct Answer: D

docker version displays the client and server (daemon) version numbers, OS/arch, and Go version. docker info displays the daemon's full configuration: storage driver, logging driver, Cgroup version, registries, runtime (containerd/runc), Swarm status, number of containers/images, and kernel version.

📝 Detailed Explanation
docker version vs docker info — What Each Shows
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  docker version:                      docker info:               │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────┐      ┌──────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ Client: Docker Engine      │      │ Server Version: 26.x │   │
  │  │  Version:    26.1.0        │      │ Storage Driver:      │   │
  │  │  API version: 1.45         │      │   overlay2           │   │
  │  │  Go version:  go1.21       │      │ Logging Driver:      │   │
  │  │  OS/Arch:     linux/amd64  │      │   json-file          │   │
  │  │                            │      │ Cgroup Driver:       │   │
  │  │ Server: Docker Engine      │      │   systemd            │   │
  │  │  Engine:                   │      │ Cgroup Version: 2    │   │
  │  │   Version:    26.1.0       │      │ Swarm: active        │   │
  │  │   API version: 1.45        │      │  NodeID: abc123      │   │
  │  │   Go version:  go1.21      │      │  Is Manager: true    │   │
  │  │   OS/Arch:     linux/amd64 │      │ Runtimes: runc       │   │
  │  │  containerd:               │      │ Registry:            │   │
  │  │   Version:     1.6.x       │      │  https://index...    │   │
  │  │  runc:                     │      │ Containers: 5        │   │
  │  │   Version:     1.1.x       │      │ Images: 23           │   │
  │  └────────────────────────────┘      └──────────────────────┘   │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: docker version = version numbers only (client + server). docker info = daemon configuration (storage driver, logging driver, Swarm status, registries). If docker version shows client but "Cannot connect to daemon," the daemon isn't running.

Installation Q96 of 200
What is the role of containerd in the Docker architecture?
A) It's the Docker CLI replacement — you type commands directly to containerd
B) It manages the container lifecycle — pulling images, creating/running containers, managing storage and networking — while dockerd delegates to it
C) It's a monitoring daemon that collects container metrics
D) It's a Kubernetes-specific container runtime, not used by Docker
✅ Correct Answer: B

containerd is the industry-standard container runtime (CNCF graduated project). Docker daemon (dockerd) delegates the heavy lifting — image pull/push, container lifecycle (create, start, stop), storage, and networking — to containerd. containerd then uses runc for the actual container execution via OCI runtime spec.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Docker Architecture — dockerd → containerd → runc
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ┌──────────┐     ┌──────────────┐     ┌──────────┐            │
  │  │ DOCKER   │────▶│ dockerd      │────▶│containerd│────▶ runc  │
  │  │ CLI      │ gRPC│ (daemon)     │ gRPC│ (runtime │ fork │(OCI)│
  │  │          │     │              │     │ manager) │      │     │
  │  └──────────┘     └──────────────┘     └──────────┘      └─────│
  │                                                                 │
  │  RESPONSIBILITIES:                                              │
  │  ┌──────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
  │  │ dockerd      │ API, image building, swarm, volumes,     │    │
  │  │              │ networking setup, auth, logging drivers  │    │
  │  ├──────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────┤    │
  │  │ containerd   │ Image push/pull, container lifecycle,    │    │
  │  │              │ snapshot management (storage), task exec │    │
  │  ├──────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────┤    │
  │  │ runc         │ Creates containers from OCI bundles,     │    │
  │  │              │ sets up namespaces + cgroups, executes   │    │
  │  └──────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
  │                                                                 │
  │  KUBERNETES: Also uses containerd (CRI plugin) — same runtime!  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: Docker = dockerd → containerd → runc. containerd is a CNCF graduated project, also used by Kubernetes. Know the chain: CLI talks to dockerd via REST API, dockerd talks to containerd via gRPC, containerd forks runc to create containers.

Installation Q97 of 200
How do you start, stop, and enable Docker to start on boot on a systemd-based Linux?
A) docker start / docker stop / docker enable
B) service docker start / service docker stop / chkconfig docker on
C) sudo systemctl start docker / sudo systemctl stop docker / sudo systemctl enable docker
D) dockerd --start / dockerd --stop / dockerd --enable-boot
✅ Correct Answer: C

Docker runs as a systemd service on Linux. systemctl start docker starts the daemon; systemctl stop docker stops it (terminating all running containers); systemctl enable docker configures it to start automatically on boot.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Docker Service Management — systemctl Commands
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ sudo systemctl start docker     → Start daemon immediately   │
  │  $ sudo systemctl stop docker      → Stop daemon (containers     │
  │                                       keep running if --live-    │
  │                                       restore is set)            │
  │  $ sudo systemctl restart docker   → Stop + start (containers    │
  │                                       restart if restart policy  │
  │                                       is "always" or "unless-    │
  │                                       stopped")                  │
  │  $ sudo systemctl enable docker    → Auto-start on boot          │
  │  $ sudo systemctl disable docker   → No auto-start on boot       │
  │  $ sudo systemctl status docker    → Check if running            │
  │                                                                 │
  │  LIVE RESTORE (keep containers running during daemon restart):  │
  │  /etc/docker/daemon.json:                                       │
  │  { "live-restore": true }                                       │
  │  → docker stop/restart won't kill running containers!           │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: Docker is a systemd service. Know: systemctl start/stop/restart/enable/disable/status docker. Without live-restore:true, stopping dockerd kills ALL containers. With live-restore, containers survive daemon restarts.

Installation Q98 of 200
When upgrading Docker Engine, what happens to existing containers and images?
A) Containers and images are preserved — only the engine binaries are replaced. Stopped containers remain; running containers need restart.
B) All containers and images are deleted — you must recreate everything
C) Images are preserved but all containers are permanently lost
D) Docker automatically migrates containers to the new version without downtime
✅ Correct Answer: A

Upgrading Docker Engine only replaces the binaries (dockerd, containerd, runc). All data in /var/lib/docker/ — images, containers, volumes, networks — is preserved. Stopped containers remain stopped; running containers need to be restarted after the daemon restarts (or use live-restore).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Docker Upgrade — What Survives
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  BEFORE UPGRADE:                   AFTER UPGRADE:                │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────┐     ┌──────────────────────┐     │
  │  │ /var/lib/docker/         │     │ /var/lib/docker/     │     │
  │  │ ├── containers/          │     │ ├── containers/  ✅  │     │
  │  │ ├── image/overlay2/      │     │ ├── image/...    ✅  │     │
  │  │ ├── volumes/             │     │ ├── volumes/     ✅  │     │
  │  │ ├── swarm/               │     │ ├── swarm/       ✅  │     │
  │  │ └── overlay2/ (layers)   │     │ └── overlay2/    ✅  │     │
  │  └──────────────────────────┘     └──────────────────────┘     │
  │                                                                 │
  │  UPGRADE PROCESS (Ubuntu):                                     │
  │  1. $ sudo apt-get update                                      │
  │  2. $ sudo apt-get upgrade docker-ce docker-ce-cli containerd.io│
  │  3. $ sudo systemctl restart docker                            │
  │  4. $ docker ps -a  → All containers still here! ✅            │
  │                                                                 │
  │  ⚠️ Always check release notes for breaking changes!            │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: Upgrading Docker preserves ALL data in /var/lib/docker/. Containers are NOT deleted. Use live-restore: true in daemon.json to keep containers running during daemon restart. Always back up /var/lib/docker before major upgrades.

3.2 Docker Daemon ConfigurationQ99-Q106

Installation Q99 of 200
What is /etc/docker/daemon.json used for?
A) It stores container runtime configuration applied per-container
B) It's the Docker Compose configuration file for the host
C) It's the master configuration file for the Docker daemon — controls storage driver, logging driver, registry mirrors, insecure registries, and more
D) It's a log file that records all daemon events
✅ Correct Answer: C

/etc/docker/daemon.json is the JSON configuration file for the Docker daemon. It configures: storage driver, logging driver + options, registry mirrors, insecure registries, DNS, MTU, live-restore, user namespace remapping, default address pools, and experimental features. Changes require systemctl restart docker.

📝 Detailed Explanation
daemon.json — The Master Configuration File
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  /etc/docker/daemon.json:                                        │
  │  {                                                               │
  │    "storage-driver": "overlay2",                                 │
  │    "log-driver": "json-file",                                    │
  │    "log-opts": {                                                 │
  │      "max-size": "10m",                                          │
  │      "max-file": "3"                                             │
  │    },                                                            │
  │    "registry-mirrors": ["https://mirror.local:5000"],            │
  │    "insecure-registries": ["myreg.local:5000"],                  │
  │    "dns": ["8.8.8.8", "1.1.1.1"],                               │
  │    "live-restore": true,                                         │
  │    "userns-remap": "default",                                    │
  │    "default-address-pools": [                                    │
  │      {"base": "172.17.0.0/16", "size": 24}                      │
  │    ],                                                            │
  │    "experimental": false,                                        │
  │    "metrics-addr": "0.0.0.0:9323"                                │
  │  }                                                               │
  │                                                                 │
  │  ⚠️ dockerd flags override daemon.json.                          │
  │     Changes require: systemctl restart docker                   │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: daemon.json is the primary Docker configuration file. dockerd CLI flags take precedence over daemon.json. Always restart Docker after editing. Key settings: storage-driver, log-driver, registry-mirrors, insecure-registries, live-restore, userns-remap.

Installation Q100 of 200
What is the default and recommended storage driver for Docker on modern Linux?
A) aufs — the original Docker storage driver
B) overlay2 — the modern, performant driver using the Linux kernel's OverlayFS
C) devicemapper — the legacy enterprise driver
D) btrfs — the filesystem-specific driver for Btrfs
✅ Correct Answer: B

overlay2 is the default and recommended storage driver for all modern Linux distributions. It uses the kernel's built-in OverlayFS to create a union filesystem — base image layers are read-only "lower" directories; container writes go to a "upper" directory. It's fast, stable, and included in the mainline kernel since 4.0.

📝 Detailed Explanation
overlay2 — How Container Layers Work
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  CONTAINER FILESYSTEM (overlay2):                                │
  │  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │  UPPER DIR (writable)                                     │ │
  │  │  /var/lib/docker/overlay2/abc.../diff/                    │ │
  │  │  → All container writes go here                           │ │
  │  ├───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤ │
  │  │  MERGED VIEW (what the container sees)                    │ │
  │  │  /var/lib/docker/overlay2/abc.../merged/                  │ │
  │  │  → Union of ALL lower layers + upper                     │ │
  │  ├───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤ │
  │  │  LOWER DIRS (read-only, image layers)                    │ │
  │  │  Layer4: COPY . .              (5MB)                      │ │
  │  │  Layer3: RUN pip install ...   (45MB)                     │ │
  │  │  Layer2: COPY requirements.txt (2MB)                      │ │
  │  │  Layer1: FROM python:3.12-slim (150MB)                    │ │
  │  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │                                                                 │
  │  STORAGE DRIVERS: overlay2 (default), fuse-overlayfs (rootless) │
  │  LEGACY: aufs (deprecated), devicemapper (deprecated)           │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: overlay2 is the default and recommended storage driver. Don't change it unless you have a specific reason. Legacy drivers (aufs, devicemapper) are deprecated. Check current driver: docker info | grep "Storage Driver".

Installation Q101 of 200
What is the default logging driver for Docker and where are logs stored?
A) syslog — logs are sent to /var/log/syslog
B) journald — logs are stored in the systemd journal
C) fluentd — logs are forwarded to a Fluentd aggregator
D) json-file — logs are stored as JSON files at /var/lib/docker/containers/<id>/<id>-json.log
✅ Correct Answer: D

The default json-file driver stores each container's stdout/stderr as JSON-formatted lines in /var/lib/docker/containers/<id>/<id>-json.log. Without log rotation, these files grow indefinitely. Configure rotation in daemon.json with "log-opts": {"max-size": "10m", "max-file": "3"}.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Logging Drivers — json-file vs Alternatives
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  LOGGING DRIVERS:                                                │
  │  ┌──────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ DRIVER       │ USE CASE                                   │   │
  │  ├──────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤   │
  │  │ json-file    │ Default. Local JSON files. Needs rotation. │   │
  │  │ syslog       │ Send to syslog daemon (/var/log/syslog)   │   │
  │  │ journald     │ Send to systemd journal (journalctl)      │   │
  │  │ gelf         │ Graylog Extended Log Format (centralized) │   │
  │  │ fluentd      │ Forward to Fluentd aggregator             │   │
  │  │ awslogs      │ Send directly to CloudWatch Logs          │   │
  │  │ splunk       │ Send to Splunk (HTTP Event Collector)     │   │
  │  │ none         │ Disable logging entirely                  │   │
  │  └──────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                 │
  │  CONFIGURE IN daemon.json (global) OR per-container (--log-driver)│
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: Default logging driver = json-file. Logs stored at /var/lib/docker/containers/<id>/<id>-json.log. Always configure log rotation (max-size, max-file) to prevent disk exhaustion. Logging driver can be set globally (daemon.json) or per-container (--log-driver).

Installation Q102 of 200
How do you configure a registry mirror in Docker?
A) Add "registry-mirrors": ["https://mirror.example.com"] to /etc/docker/daemon.json and restart Docker
B) Set the DOCKER_MIRROR environment variable
C) Run docker mirror add https://mirror.example.com
D) Configure the mirror in /etc/hosts pointing to Docker Hub
✅ Correct Answer: A

Registry mirrors are configured in /etc/docker/daemon.json under the "registry-mirrors" array. Docker tries the mirror first; if the image isn't cached, it fetches from Docker Hub and caches it. Multiple mirrors can be specified — Docker tries them in order.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Registry Mirror Configuration — daemon.json Setup
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  /etc/docker/daemon.json:                                        │
  │  {                                                               │
  │    "registry-mirrors": [                                         │
  │      "https://mirror1.company.local:5000",                       │
  │      "https://mirror2.company.local:5000"                        │
  │    ]                                                             │
  │  }                                                               │
  │                                                                 │
  │  FLOW:                                                           │
  │  $ docker pull python:3.12-slim                                  │
  │  1. Try mirror1.company.local:5000  → cache MISS                │
  │  2. Try mirror2.company.local:5000  → cache MISS                │
  │  3. Pull from docker.io/library/python:3.12-slim                 │
  │  4. Cache in mirror1 for next time                               │
  │                                                                 │
  │  $ docker pull python:3.12-slim  (second time)                   │
  │  1. Try mirror1 → cache HIT → return instantly (@LAN speed)     │
  │                                                                 │
  │  ⚠️ Changes require: sudo systemctl restart docker              │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: Registry mirrors are configured in daemon.json via "registry-mirrors": []. They act as pull-through caches. Multiple mirrors = fallback order. This is critical for CI/CD environments to avoid Docker Hub rate limits (100 pulls/6h unauthenticated).

Installation Q103 of 200
When do you need to configure an insecure registry in Docker?
A) When the registry requires a username and password
B) When the registry is hosted on a different cloud provider
C) When using a private registry with a self-signed certificate or plain HTTP (no TLS) — otherwise Docker rejects the connection
D) When the registry has rate limiting enabled
✅ Correct Answer: C

Docker requires TLS for all registry communication by default. If you're running a local registry with HTTP (no TLS) or self-signed certificates, you must add it to "insecure-registries" in daemon.json. Production registries should ALWAYS use proper TLS certificates — insecure registries are for development only.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Insecure Registries — When and How
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  /etc/docker/daemon.json:                                        │
  │  {                                                               │
  │    "insecure-registries": [                                      │
  │      "myreg.local:5000",                                        │
  │      "192.168.1.100:5000"                                       │
  │    ]                                                             │
  │  }                                                               │
  │                                                                 │
  │  WITHOUT CONFIG:                     WITH CONFIG:                │
  │  $ docker pull myreg.local:5000/img  $ docker pull myreg...     │
  │  Error: http: server gave HTTP       → Pull succeeds            │
  │  response to HTTPS client                                       │
  │                                                                 │
  │  ⚠️ SECURITY: Insecure registries disable TLS verification!     │
  │     NEVER use for registries over the internet.                 │
  │     For self-signed certs, add the CA cert to the trust store   │
  │     instead of using insecure-registries in production.         │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: Docker requires HTTPS/TLS for all registries by default. "insecure-registries" in daemon.json bypasses this — for dev only. For self-signed certs in production, add the CA certificate to the OS trust store instead. Restart Docker after changes.

Installation Q104 of 200
What does the --host flag on dockerd control?
A) It sets the hostname of the container
B) It specifies which sockets/ports the Docker daemon listens on — e.g., unix:///var/run/docker.sock or tcp://0.0.0.0:2375
C) It configures the Docker registry hostname
D) It sets the hostname used in Swarm service discovery
✅ Correct Answer: B

--host (or -H) controls where dockerd listens for API connections. Default: unix:///var/run/docker.sock (local only, secure). Adding tcp://0.0.0.0:2375 exposes the Docker API over the network — which is a MAJOR security risk without TLS (port 2376 with TLS).

📝 Detailed Explanation
dockerd --host — Remote API Access (Use with Caution)
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  LOCAL ONLY (default, secure):                                   │
  │  dockerd -H unix:///var/run/docker.sock                         │
  │  → docker CLI uses this socket automatically                    │
  │                                                                 │
  │  REMOTE ACCESS (⚠️ DANGER without TLS):                          │
  │  dockerd -H tcp://0.0.0.0:2375 -H unix:///var/run/docker.sock  │
  │  → Port 2375: UNENCRYPTED (anyone can control Docker!)          │
  │  → Port 2376: TLS-encrypted (secure remote access)              │
  │                                                                 │
  │  REMOTE CLIENT:                                                  │
  │  $ docker -H tcp://192.168.1.10:2376 ps                         │
  │  OR: docker context create remote --docker host=tcp://...       │
  │                                                                 │
  │  ⚠️ NEVER open port 2375 to the internet!                       │
  │     Equivalent to giving root SSH access.                       │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: dockerd -H controls where the daemon listens. Default: unix:// socket (local, secure). TCP port 2375 = unencrypted (NEVER expose publicly). TCP port 2376 = TLS-encrypted. docker -H <host> connects a remote client. docker context is the modern way to manage remote daemons.

Installation Q105 of 200
What is the purpose of docker context?
A) It sets environment variables for containers at build time
B) It configures the build context directory for docker build
C) It manages Kubernetes contexts for container orchestration
D) It manages connections to multiple Docker daemons — switching between local, remote, and cloud Docker hosts
✅ Correct Answer: D

docker context lets you manage connections to multiple Docker endpoints (daemons). Create a context for each environment (local dev, remote production, cloud Docker host) and switch between them with docker context use. This replaces manually setting DOCKER_HOST environment variables.

📝 Detailed Explanation
docker context — Multi-Daemon Management
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ docker context ls                                            │
  │  NAME          TYPE        DESCRIPTION                          │
  │  default *     moby        Current DOCKER_HOST based config     │
  │  prod-swarm    moby        Production Swarm manager              │
  │  staging       moby        Staging server                        │
  │  desktop-linux moby        Docker Desktop                        │
  │                                                                 │
  │  CREATE A CONTEXT:                                              │
  │  $ docker context create prod \                                 │
  │      --docker "host=tcp://10.0.0.5:2376" \                      │
  │      --description "Production Swarm"                           │
  │                                                                 │
  │  SWITCH CONTEXTS:                                               │
  │  $ docker context use prod        → All docker commands now     │
  │  $ docker ps                      → go to production!          │
  │  $ docker context use default     → Back to local               │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: docker context manages multiple Docker endpoints. Create with docker context create, switch with docker context use, list with docker context ls. This is the modern replacement for setting DOCKER_HOST manually. Know this for the DCA.

Installation Q106 of 200
Why is exposing /var/run/docker.sock to containers dangerous?
A) It slows down container startup because of socket contention
B) Mounting docker.sock into a container gives that container FULL control over the Docker daemon — it can start/stop containers, pull images, and potentially escape to the host
C) docker.sock contains encrypted passwords that can be brute-forced
D) The socket is only dangerous on Windows containers
✅ Correct Answer: B

Mounting /var/run/docker.sock into a container is essentially giving that container root access to the host. The container can run privileged containers, mount host filesystems, escape via --privileged containers, and access all other containers' data. It's one of the most common and dangerous Docker security anti-patterns.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Docker Socket Security — The Danger of Mounting docker.sock
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  DANGEROUS PATTERN (Docker-in-Docker via sock mount):            │
  │  $ docker run -v /var/run/docker.sock:/var/run/docker.sock ...  │
  │                                                                 │
  │  INSIDE THE CONTAINER:                                          │
  │  $ docker run -it --privileged -v /:/host alpine chroot /host  │
  │  → FULL HOST ROOT ACCESS! 😱                                    │
  │                                                                 │
  │  SAFER ALTERNATIVES:                                            │
  │  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ 1. Docker-in-Docker (dind): Run a separate Docker daemon  │ │
  │  │    inside the container (--privileged still needed)        │ │
  │  │                                                           │ │
  │  │ 2. Kaniko, Buildah: Build images WITHOUT Docker daemon    │ │
  │  │    (no socket needed!)                                    │ │
  │  │                                                           │ │
  │  │ 3. Rootless Docker: Even if socket leaked, limited blast  │ │
  │  │    radius (user namespace remapping)                      │ │
  │  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: Mounting docker.sock into containers is a severe security risk — it grants full Docker API access. For CI/CD image building, use Kaniko or Buildah (daemonless) instead. If you must use Docker-in-Docker, use the dind image with proper isolation.

3.3 Logging & MetricsQ107-Q114

Installation Q107 of 200
What does docker logs --tail 50 --follow container1 do?
A) Shows the last 50 lines of the Docker daemon log and exits
B) Shows the last 50 lines of container1's log, then follows (streams) new log output in real-time
C) Sends the last 50 log lines to the container's stdout
D) Truncates the container log file to only keep the last 50 lines
✅ Correct Answer: B

--tail 50 shows only the last 50 lines (useful for large logs). --follow (or -f) keeps the connection open and streams new log entries in real-time — like tail -f. --since shows logs from a specific time; --timestamps prepends timestamps to each line.

📝 Detailed Explanation
docker logs — Key Flags Reference
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ docker logs [OPTIONS] CONTAINER                              │
  │                                                                 │
  │  ┌──────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ FLAG         │ DESCRIPTION                               │   │
  │  ├──────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤   │
  │  │ --tail 100   │ Show last N lines (default: all)          │   │
  │  │ --follow (-f)│ Stream logs in real-time (like tail -f)   │   │
  │  │ --since 1h   │ Show logs newer than relative time        │   │
  │  │ --since      │ Show logs since RFC3339 timestamp         │   │
  │  │   2026-06-14 │                                            │   │
  │  │ --until 30m  │ Show logs older than relative time        │   │
  │  │ --timestamps │ Prepend RFC3339 timestamp to each line    │   │
  │  │ --details    │ Show extra details from log driver         │   │
  │  └──────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                 │
  │  EXAMPLE:                                                       │
  │  $ docker logs --tail 100 -f --since 30m --timestamps anihpj-api│
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: docker logs works with the json-file and journald logging drivers — NOT with syslog, gelf, fluentd, etc (those send logs elsewhere). --tail, --follow, --since, --timestamps are the most tested flags. Know them all.

Installation Q108 of 200
Which logging driver sends container logs to the systemd journal?
A) syslog
B) json-file
C) gelf
D) journald
✅ Correct Answer: D

The journald driver sends logs to the systemd journal. Logs can then be viewed with journalctl CONTAINER_NAME=<name> or journalctl -u docker. This is common on systemd-based Linux distributions and integrates with existing logging infrastructure.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Logging Driver Selection — When to Use Which
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  LOGGING DRIVER SELECTION FLOWCHART:                             │
  │                                                                 │
  │  Need logs accessible via docker logs?                           │
  │  ├── YES → json-file (default) or journald                       │
  │  │         (these support docker logs command)                   │
  │  │                                                               │
  │  └── NO → Need centralized logging?                              │
  │      ├── Splunk → splunk driver                                  │
  │      ├── ELK Stack → gelf driver                                 │
  │      ├── Fluentd → fluentd driver                                │
  │      ├── CloudWatch → awslogs driver                             │
  │      └── Syslog server → syslog driver                           │
  │                                                                 │
  │  ⚠️ ONCE YOU CHANGE FROM json-file, docker logs STOPS WORKING!  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: journald = systemd journal (use journalctl to view). json-file = Docker's default (use docker logs). Once you switch to syslog/gelf/fluentd, docker logs no longer works — query the logging backend directly.

Installation Q109 of 200
How can you send container logs to multiple destinations simultaneously?
A) Configure multiple --log-driver flags on docker run
B) Docker natively supports dual logging in daemon.json
C) Use a log router container (like Fluentd or Logspout) that captures all container logs and forwards to multiple backends
D) Run two containers from the same image with different logging drivers
✅ Correct Answer: C

Docker only supports ONE logging driver per container. To send logs to multiple destinations (e.g., Splunk + CloudWatch), use a log router like Fluentd, Logspout, or Vector as a sidecar or system container that collects all container logs and forwards to multiple backends.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Dual/Multi Logging — Log Router Pattern
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  WITHOUT LOG ROUTER:                 WITH LOG ROUTER:            │
  │  ┌────────────────────┐             ┌────────────────────┐     │
  │  │ Container → Splunk │             │ Container           │     │
  │  └────────────────────┘             │ (json-file driver)  │     │
  │  (ONLY one destination)             │       │              │     │
  │                                      │       ▼              │     │
  │                                      │  ┌──────────────┐   │     │
  │                                      │  │ Fluentd/     │   │     │
  │                                      │  │ Logspout     │──▶│     │
  │                                      │  │ (log router) │   │     │
  │                                      │  └──────┬───────┘   │     │
  │                                      │      ┌───┴───┐       │     │
  │                                      │      ▼       ▼       │     │
  │                                      │  Splunk  CloudWatch  │     │
  │                                      │  (MULTIPLE backends) │     │
  │                                      └────────────────────┘     │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: Docker has no built-in dual logging. One container = one logging driver. Use a log router (Fluentd, Logspout) for multi-destination logging. docker logs only works with json-file and journald drivers.

Installation Q110 of 200
What does docker stats display?
A) Real-time streaming of CPU %, memory usage, network I/O, disk I/O, and PIDs for all running containers
B) Historical resource usage aggregated over the last 24 hours
C) Storage driver performance metrics for the Docker daemon
D) Image download/upload statistics from registries
✅ Correct Answer: A

docker stats provides a live, streaming view of container resource usage: CPU percentage, memory usage/limit, network I/O (TX/RX), disk I/O, and PID count. It reads from cgroups (the same metrics the kernel enforces). Use --no-stream for a one-shot snapshot.

📝 Detailed Explanation
docker stats — Real-Time Resource Monitoring
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ docker stats --no-stream                                      │
  │  CONTAINER   CPU %   MEM USAGE   MEM %   NET I/O       BLOCK I/O│
  │  anihpj-api  2.5%    128MiB      12.8%   1.2MB/890kB   45MB/2MB │
  │  anihpj-db   0.8%    256MiB      25.6%   890kB/1.2MB   120MB/8MB│
  │  anihpj-web  0.1%    15MiB       1.5%    5.6MB/12MB    1MB/0B   │
  │                                                                 │
  │  $ docker stats --format "{{.Name}}: {{.CPUPerc}}"              │
  │  anihpj-api: 2.50%                                              │
  │                                                                 │
  │  METRICS SOURCE: cgroups (same data as /sys/fs/cgroup/)         │
  │  NOT PERSISTENT: Only shows current values, no history          │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: docker stats = real-time cgroup metrics. docker stats --no-stream = one-shot. For persistent metrics + history, use Prometheus (dockerd --metrics-addr) or cAdvisor. docker stats shows LIVE data only — no historical data.

Installation Q111 of 200
What does docker system df show?
A) The available disk space on the host filesystem
B) Docker Compose file validation results
C) Disk usage breakdown by Docker objects: images, containers, local volumes, and build cache — with reclaimable space
D) Docker daemon log file sizes
✅ Correct Answer: C

docker system df shows how much disk space Docker objects consume: images (and dangling images), containers, local volumes, and build cache. It also shows how much is reclaimable (can be freed with prune commands). Use -v for detailed per-image/per-container breakdowns.

📝 Detailed Explanation
docker system df — Disk Usage Audit
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ docker system df                                             │
  │  TYPE          TOTAL       ACTIVE      SIZE      RECLAIMABLE   │
  │  Images        12          5           2.5GB     1.2GB (48%)   │
  │  Containers    8           3           150MB     50MB (33%)    │
  │  Local Volumes 4           2           500MB     200MB (40%)   │
  │  Build Cache   25          0           800MB     800MB (100%)  │
  │                                                                 │
  │  $ docker system df -v  → Detailed breakdown per image/container│
  │                                                                 │
  │  CLEANUP COMMANDS:                                              │
  │  $ docker system prune      → Remove unused data                │
  │  $ docker system prune -a   → Remove ALL unused (images too!)   │
  │  $ docker system prune --volumes → Include volumes              │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: docker system df = docker's disk usage report. Shows: Images, Containers, Volumes, Build Cache. RECLAIMABLE = can be freed. docker system prune cleans up. This is critical for preventing disk exhaustion on build agents.

Installation Q112 of 200
What does docker events show?
A) Historical logs of all docker commands run by users
B) A real-time stream of Docker daemon events — container create/start/stop/die, image pull/push/tag, volume/network create/destroy
C) Upcoming scheduled Docker events like image expiration
D) Crash reports from failed containers
✅ Correct Answer: B

docker events streams all daemon events in real-time: container lifecycle (create, start, stop, kill, die, destroy), image operations (pull, push, tag, delete), volume/network mutations, and daemon events. Filter with --filter to watch specific event types or containers.

📝 Detailed Explanation
docker events — Real-Time Daemon Event Stream
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ docker events --since 1h --filter "event=die"                │
  │  2026-06-14T10:30:00 container die anihpj-api (exit=137, ...)  │
  │  2026-06-14T10:30:02 container start anihpj-api                 │
  │                                                                 │
  │  EVENT TYPES:                                                   │
  │  ┌──────────────┬────────────────────────────────────────┐     │
  │  │ container    │ attach, commit, copy, create, destroy,  │     │
  │  │              │ die, exec_create, kill, oom, pause,     │     │
  │  │              │ restart, start, stop, unpause, update   │     │
  │  ├──────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤     │
  │  │ image        │ delete, import, load, pull, push, save, │     │
  │  │              │ tag, untag                              │     │
  │  ├──────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤     │
  │  │ volume       │ create, destroy, mount, unmount         │     │
  │  ├──────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────┤     │
  │  │ network      │ connect, create, destroy, disconnect    │     │
  │  └──────────────┴────────────────────────────────────────┘     │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: docker events streams daemon events in real-time. Use --filter to narrow results, --since/--until for time ranges, --format for Go template output. Useful for debugging: watch for "die" events to catch crashing containers.

Installation Q113 of 200
How do you expose Docker daemon metrics for Prometheus scraping?
A) Install the Prometheus Docker plugin from Docker Hub
B) docker metrics export --prometheus
C) Metrics are automatically exposed on port 9090
D) Configure "metrics-addr": "0.0.0.0:9323" in daemon.json (or --metrics-addr flag), then scrape /metrics endpoint
✅ Correct Answer: D

Docker has a built-in Prometheus metrics endpoint. Enable it by setting "metrics-addr": "0.0.0.0:9323" in daemon.json or using the --metrics-addr 0.0.0.0:9323 flag on dockerd. Then configure Prometheus to scrape <host>:9323/metrics for daemon metrics.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Prometheus Metrics — Docker Daemon Monitoring
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  daemon.json:                                                    │
  │  {                                                               │
  │    "metrics-addr": "0.0.0.0:9323",                              │
  │    "experimental": true                                          │
  │  }                                                               │
  │                                                                 │
  │  $ curl http://localhost:9323/metrics                            │
  │  # HELP engine_daemon_container_actions_seconds                 │
  │  engine_daemon_container_actions_seconds_count{action="start"} 42│
  │  engine_daemon_events_subscribers_total 5                        │
  │  builder_builds_triggered_total 128                              │
  │  swarm_manager_nodes_total 3                                     │
  │  ...                                                             │
  │                                                                 │
  │  PROMETHEUS CONFIG (prometheus.yml):                             │
  │  scrape_configs:                                                │
  │    - job_name: 'docker'                                         │
  │      static_configs:                                            │
  │        - targets: ['docker-host:9323']                          │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: Docker exposes Prometheus metrics via --metrics-addr or "metrics-addr" in daemon.json. Default port: 9323. Endpoint: /metrics. This provides daemon-level metrics (builds, container actions, Swarm events) — for per-container metrics, use cAdvisor.

Installation Q114 of 200
How do you configure log rotation for the json-file logging driver?
A) In daemon.json: "log-opts": {"max-size": "10m", "max-file": "3"} — limits each log file to 10MB and keeps max 3 files
B) Use logrotate on the host to rotate /var/lib/docker files
C) docker logs rotate --max-size 10m container
D) Log rotation is automatic; no configuration needed
✅ Correct Answer: A

JSON file logs grow indefinitely without rotation. Configure "log-opts" in daemon.json (global) or per-container with --log-opt max-size=10m --log-opt max-file=3. max-size triggers rotation when the file reaches that size; max-file limits the number of rotated files kept.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Log Rotation — Preventing Disk Exhaustion
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  GLOBAL (daemon.json):              PER-CONTAINER:               │
  │  {                                  $ docker run \               │
  │    "log-driver": "json-file",         --log-driver json-file \   │
  │    "log-opts": {                      --log-opt max-size=10m \   │
  │      "max-size": "10m",               --log-opt max-file=3 \     │
  │      "max-file": "3"                  nginx                       │
  │    }                                                            │
  │  }                                                              │
  │                                                                 │
  │  ROTATION BEHAVIOR (max-size=10m, max-file=3):                 │
  │  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ container-id-json.log      (current, writing)              │ │
  │  │ container-id-json.log.1    (rotated, 10MB full)           │ │
  │  │ container-id-json.log.2    (rotated, 10MB full)           │ │
  │  │ container-id-json.log.3    → DELETED (max-file=3)         │ │
  │  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │                                                                 │
  │  ⚠️ WITHOUT ROTATION: A busy container can fill the disk!       │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: Without log rotation, json-file logs grow forever → disk exhaustion. Configure max-size + max-file in daemon.json (global) or per-container (--log-opt). This is a critical production configuration. The DCA tests this frequently.

3.4 Enterprise ToolingQ115-Q120

Installation Q115 of 200
What is Mirantis Kubernetes Engine (MKE) in the context of Docker Enterprise?
A) A free, open-source alternative to Docker Swarm
B) A desktop application for managing Docker on Windows

✅ Correct Answer: C

MKE (Mirantis Kubernetes Engine, formerly Docker UCP) is an enterprise platform that provides a web UI, centralized authentication (LDAP/AD/SAML), RBAC, and manages both Swarm and Kubernetes clusters from one control plane. It's the enterprise version of Docker's orchestration capability.

MKE — Enterprise Orchestration Platform

  │  │ • LDAP/AD/SAML: Enterprise identity integration            │ │
  │  │ • Dual Orchestration: Swarm + Kubernetes, same cluster    │ │
  │  │ • Centralized Logging: All container logs in one place    │ │
  │  │ • Security Scanning: Integrated vulnerability scanning    │ │
  │  │ • Secrets Management: Encrypted at rest + in transit       │ │
  │  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │                                                                 │
  │  MKE vs COMMUNITY DOCKER:                                       │
  │  Community: docker swarm init (CLI only, basic auth)            │
  │  MKE: Web UI + RBAC + LDAP + K8s + scanning (enterprise)       │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: MKE (Mirantis Kubernetes Engine) = enterprise Docker orchestration. Formerly Docker UCP. Provides: web UI, RBAC, LDAP/AD, dual Swarm+K8s orchestration. The DCA exam covers Mirantis enterprise offerings.

Installation Q116 of 200
What changed with Docker's licensing in 2021?
A) Docker became completely free for all users
B) Docker Desktop required a paid subscription (Docker Business/Pro) for professional use in companies with 250+ employees or $10M+ revenue
C) Docker Engine became proprietary and closed-source
D) Docker Hub removed all free image hosting
✅ Correct Answer: B

In August 2021, Docker introduced a subscription model for Docker Desktop. Companies with 250+ employees OR $10M+ annual revenue must pay for Docker Business/Pro. Docker Engine (dockerd) remains free and open-source under Apache 2.0. Personal use, small businesses, education, and open-source remain free.

  │  └──────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                 │
  │  ALTERNATIVES (free): Podman Desktop, Rancher Desktop, OrbStack │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: Docker Desktop requires a paid subscription for large enterprises (250+ employees or $10M+ revenue). Docker Engine (dockerd) is ALWAYS free and open-source. Know the distinction: Desktop ≠ Engine. The DCA covers this licensing change.

Installation Q117 of 200
What does Docker Scout provide beyond basic vulnerability scanning?
A) Real-time container intrusion detection
B) Automatic image optimization and compression
C) Container orchestration and scheduling
D) Policy evaluation, fix recommendations, SBOM generation, supply chain attestations, and continuous vulnerability monitoring
✅ Correct Answer: D

Docker Scout goes beyond basic CVE scanning. It provides: policy evaluation (define rules like "no critical CVEs"), fix recommendations (suggests base image updates), SBOM generation (Software Bill of Materials), supply chain attestations (provenance), and continuous monitoring (re-scans images when new CVEs are published).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Docker Scout — Full Supply Chain Security Suite
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  DOCKER SCOUT CAPABILITIES:                                     │
  │  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ 1. CVE Scanning: Identifies known vulnerabilities          │ │
  │  │ 2. Policy Evaluation: Define organizational policies       │ │
  │  │    e.g., "block images with CRITICAL CVEs"                │ │
  │  │ 3. Fix Recommendations: Suggests base image updates        │ │
  │  │ 4. SBOM Generation: Software Bill of Materials             │ │
  │  │ 5. Provenance Attestations: Verify image origin            │ │
  │  │ 6. Continuous Monitoring: Re-scans on new CVE disclosures  │ │
  │  │ 7. Repository watcher: Auto-scan pushed images             │ │
  │  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │                                                                 │
  │  COMPARISON:                                                    │
  │  Basic scan (Trivy):       "Image has 5 CVEs"                   │
  │  Docker Scout:              "5 CVEs (2 fixable by updating base│
  │                              image). Policy violation: critical │
  │                              CVEs not allowed. Recommended fix: │
  │                              python:3.12.4-slim"                │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: Docker Scout = Docker's full supply chain security tool (not just scanning). Key features: policies, fix recommendations, SBOM, provenance, continuous monitoring. docker scout quickview, docker scout cves, docker scout policy are the key commands.

Installation Q118 of 200
What is Docker Build Cloud?
A) A cloud-based build service that offloads docker build to remote infrastructure — faster builds, shared cache, no local resource usage
B) A Docker image hosting service similar to Docker Hub
C) A weather service for predicting Docker Hub downtime
D) A Kubernetes cluster for running Docker containers
✅ Correct Answer: A

Docker Build Cloud offloads image builds to Docker's cloud infrastructure. Benefits: much faster builds (cloud CPUs often faster than local), shared team cache (one person builds, everyone benefits), no local resource consumption (your laptop stays responsive), and native multi-architecture builds.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Docker Build Cloud — Remote Build Infrastructure
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  LOCAL BUILD:                        CLOUD BUILD:                │
  │  ┌────────────────────┐             ┌────────────────────┐     │
  │  │ Developer laptop   │             │ Developer laptop   │     │
  │  │ ┌────────────────┐ │             │ docker build \     │     │
  │  │ │ docker build   │ │             │   --builder cloud- │     │
  │  │ │ (CPU at 100%)  │ │             │   anihpj-builder . │     │
  │  │ │ 3 min build    │ │             │       │             │     │
  │  │ │ Laptop HOT! 🔥 │ │             │       ▼             │     │
  │  │ └────────────────┘ │             │ ┌────────────────┐  │     │
  │  └────────────────────┘             │ │ DOCKER BUILD   │  │     │
  │                                      │ │ CLOUD          │  │     │
  │  BENEFITS OF CLOUD:                 │ │ 30 sec build   │  │     │
  │  • Faster builds (dedicated CPUs)   │ │ Laptop COOL 😎 │  │     │
  │  • Shared team cache                 │ └────────────────┘  │     │
  │  • Multi-arch builds (amd64+arm64)  └────────────────────┘     │
  │  • No local resource drain                                     │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: Docker Build Cloud = remote build service. Offloads docker build to the cloud. Benefits: faster builds, shared cache, multi-arch support. Use --builder cloud-<name> to switch. Part of Docker's enterprise/Pro features.

Installation Q119 of 200
When should you use Docker Compose vs Docker Swarm vs Mirantis Kubernetes Engine (MKE)?
A) Compose for production, Swarm for development, MKE for testing
B) All three are different names for the same thing
C) Compose: local dev (single host); Swarm: production orchestration (multi-host, built-in); MKE: enterprise production (RBAC, LDAP, dual Swarm+K8s, web UI)
D) Swarm for Windows, Compose for Linux, MKE for macOS
✅ Correct Answer: C

Docker Compose is for local development on a single host. Docker Swarm (built into the engine) is for production multi-host orchestration with services, secrets, and rolling updates. MKE adds enterprise features: RBAC, LDAP/AD authentication, web UI, dual Swarm+ Kubernetes orchestration, and centralized management.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Compose vs Swarm vs MKE — Decision Matrix
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ┌──────────────┬──────────────┬──────────────┬──────────────┐ │
  │  │ FEATURE      │ COMPOSE      │ SWARM        │ MKE          │ │
  │  ├──────────────┼──────────────┼──────────────┼──────────────┤ │
  │  │ Scope        │ Single host  │ Multi-host   │ Enterprise   │ │
  │  │ Setup        │ docker compose│ docker swarm │ Commercial   │ │
  │  │              │   up         │   init       │   license    │ │
  │  │ Scaling      │ Manual (up)  │ docker service│ Built-in     │ │
  │  │              │              │   scale      │   autoscaling│ │
  │  │ Secrets      │ Not encrypted│ Encrypted    │ Encrypted +  │ │
  │  │              │ (file mount) │   (Raft log) │   RBAC       │ │
  │  │ Load Balance │ Manual (haproxy)│Routing mesh│ Built-in LB │ │
  │  │ Web UI       │ None         │ None (CLI)   │ Full web UI  │ │
  │  │ Kubernetes   │ No           │ No           │ Yes (dual)   │ │
  │  │ Use Case     │ Dev, testing │ Prod, SMB    │ Enterprise   │ │
  │  └──────────────┴──────────────┴──────────────┴──────────────┘ │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: Compose = dev (single host). Swarm = prod (multi-host, built into engine). MKE = enterprise (RBAC, LDAP, web UI, dual Swarm+K8s). Know when to use each. The progression: compose up (dev) → stack deploy (Swarm prod) → MKE (enterprise).

Installation Q120 of 200
How do you deploy Docker on AWS EC2 with ECR integration?
A) Use the AWS-managed Docker service — no installation needed
B) Launch EC2 (Amazon Linux 2/Ubuntu), install Docker Engine, configure ECR auth (aws ecr get-login-password), and use IAM roles for secure registry access
C) Docker is pre-installed on all EC2 instances by default
D) Use AWS Lambda with Docker support instead of EC2
✅ Correct Answer: B

To deploy Docker on AWS EC2: launch an EC2 instance, install Docker Engine (same as any Linux), attach an IAM role with ECR permissions, and use aws ecr get-login-password | docker login --username AWS --password-stdin <account>.dkr.ecr.<region>.amazonaws.com. The IAM role eliminates the need to store AWS credentials on the instance.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Docker on AWS EC2 — Architecture with ECR
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  AWS CLOUD                                                       │
  │  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │                     EC2 INSTANCE                          │ │
  │  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │ │
  │  │  │ IAM Role: ECR-ReadOnly                              │ │ │
  │  │  │                                                     │ │ │
  │  │  │ $ docker pull 123.dkr.ecr.us-east-1.amazonaws.com/  │ │ │
  │  │  │   anihpj-api:v2                                     │ │ │
  │  │  │ → Authenticated via IAM role (no creds on disk!)    │ │ │
  │  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │ │
  │  │                              │                            │ │
  │  │                              ▼                            │ │
  │  │                         ECR (private)                     │ │
  │  │                         anihpj-api:v2                     │ │
  │  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │                                                                 │
  │  ALTERNATIVE: AWS ECS Fargate (no Docker install needed!)       │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: Docker on EC2 = normal Docker install + IAM role for ECR. Use aws ecr get-login-password for auth. Alternatively, use ECS (managed orchestration) or EKS (managed K8s) to avoid managing Docker Engine yourself. IAM roles are preferred over access keys for security.

🌐 Category 4: Networking15% — Q121-Q150

🔗 Category 4: Networking — 30 Questions

This category is about how containers talk to each other and the outside world. Think of it like the plumbing and electrical wiring of a building — invisible when working, catastrophic when broken. Master Docker network drivers, port publishing, DNS resolution, and advanced networking features. 15% of the DCA exam.

4.1 Docker Network DriversQ121-Q130

Networking Q121 of 200
What is the default Docker network driver and what is its scope?
A) overlay — multi-host networking across all Swarm nodes
B) bridge — isolated network on a single Docker host with NAT to the host's network
C) host — containers share the host's network stack directly
D) macvlan — containers get their own MAC addresses on the physical network
✅ Correct Answer: B

The bridge driver is Docker's default. It creates an isolated network on a single host using a software bridge (docker0). Containers get private IPs (172.17.0.0/16 by default) and access the outside world via NAT. Containers on the default bridge can only communicate by IP; user-defined bridge networks also provide DNS resolution.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Bridge Network — Docker's Default Single-Host Network
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  HOST (192.168.1.10)                                            │
  │  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │                    docker0 bridge (172.17.0.1)             │ │
  │  │         ┌─────────────────┼─────────────────┐              │ │
  │  │         ▼                 ▼                  ▼              │ │
  │  │  ┌──────────────┐ ┌──────────────┐ ┌──────────────┐       │ │
  │  │  │ Container A  │ │ Container B  │ │ Container C  │       │ │
  │  │  │ 172.17.0.2   │ │ 172.17.0.3   │ │ 172.17.0.4   │       │ │
  │  │  │ nginx:80     │ │ anihpj-api   │ │ postgres:5432│       │ │
  │  │  └──────┬───────┘ └──────┬───────┘ └──────────────┘       │ │
  │  │         └────────────────┘ (can communicate via IP)        │ │
  │  └───────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────┘ │
  │                              │ NAT (iptables)                   │
  │                              ▼                                  │
  │                    eth0 (192.168.1.10) → Internet               │
  │                                                                 │
  │  DEFAULT BRIDGE: no DNS (IP only). USER BRIDGE: DNS enabled.   │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: bridge = default driver, single-host only, NAT to host network. Default bridge (docker0) has NO DNS — containers communicate by IP only. User-created bridge networks (docker network create) HAVE DNS. Always create custom bridges for container apps.

Networking Q122 of 200
What network driver must be used for Swarm services that span multiple nodes?
A) bridge — it can be extended across nodes with manual configuration
B) host — Swarm nodes automatically share network stacks
C) overlay — it creates a VXLAN-based distributed network spanning all Swarm nodes
D) macvlan — services get MAC addresses on the physical network
✅ Correct Answer: C

The overlay driver creates a distributed network using VXLAN tunnels spanning all Swarm nodes. Containers on different hosts can communicate as if on the same subnet. docker stack deploy automatically creates an overlay network for each stack.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Overlay Network — Multi-Host Swarm Networking
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  NODE 1 (manager)                    NODE 2 (worker)             │
  │  ┌────────────────────────┐         ┌────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ anihpj-api (10.0.0.2)  │         │ anihpj-api (10.0.0.4)  │ │
  │  │ anihpj-web (10.0.0.3)  │         │ anihpj-db  (10.0.0.5)  │ │
  │  └───────────┬────────────┘         └───────────┬────────────┘ │
  │              │                                   │              │
  │              │     VXLAN Tunnel (encrypted)      │              │
  │              └───────────────────────────────────┘              │
  │                                                                 │
  │  All containers on 10.0.0.0/24 — communicate transparently     │
  │  docker network create -d overlay --attachable my-overlay       │
  │  docker stack deploy auto-creates overlay: <stack>_default     │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: overlay = REQUIRED for multi-host Swarm services. Uses VXLAN tunnels (UDP port 4789). docker stack deploy auto-creates overlay networks. Can encrypt overlay traffic with --opt encrypted=true. The routing mesh uses overlay+ingress.

Networking Q123 of 200
What does the host network driver do?
A) Creates a virtual network with its own subnet and DHCP
B) Connects containers to all available host networks simultaneously
C) Isolates containers completely — no network access
D) Removes network isolation — the container shares the host's network stack directly (same IP, same ports)
✅ Correct Answer: D

The host driver removes network namespace isolation — the container uses the host's network stack directly. Container ports are the host's ports (no -p needed). It's faster (no NAT overhead) but less secure (no isolation) and can only bind ports once per host. Useful for high-performance networking.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Host Network — Direct Host Stack Access
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  BRIDGE (default):                   HOST (direct):              │
  │  ┌────────────────────────┐         ┌──────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ Container (172.17.0.2) │         │ Container (same IP!) │   │
  │  │ ↓ docker0 bridge        │         │                      │   │
  │  │ ↓ NAT (iptables)        │         │ $ docker run --network│   │
  │  │ ↓ eth0 (192.168.1.10)   │         │   host nginx         │   │
  │  │                          │         │ → nginx binds :80    │   │
  │  │ -p 8080:80 needed       │         │ → host's port 80!    │   │
  │  └────────────────────────┘         │ → No -p needed       │   │
  │                                      │ → No NAT overhead    │   │
  │  USE CASES:                          └──────────────────────┘   │
  │  • High-performance apps (no NAT overhead)                      │
  │  • Monitoring agents that need host network access              │
  │  ⚠️ No isolation, port conflicts possible                      │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: host = container shares host's network stack. No -p needed (ports ARE host ports). Faster (no NAT) but less secure. Only one container can bind a given port. Use for performance-critical or monitoring workloads. NOT for Swarm services.

Networking Q124 of 200
What does the macvlan network driver do?
A) Assigns each container a unique MAC address and IP on the physical network — containers appear as separate physical devices
B) Creates a virtual VLAN for container isolation
C) Bridges the container network with the host's WiFi adapter
D) Encrypts all container network traffic with MAC-level encryption
✅ Correct Answer: A

macvlan assigns each container its own MAC address and IP on the physical network — they appear as separate physical devices to the network. This is ideal for legacy applications that expect to be on the physical network or need direct L2 access. However, most cloud providers don't support promiscuous mode required by macvlan.

📝 Detailed Explanation
macvlan — Containers as Physical Network Devices
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  PHYSICAL NETWORK (192.168.1.0/24)                               │
  │  ┌──────────────┬──────────────┬──────────────┬──────────────┐ │
  │  │ Host         │ Container A  │ Container B  │ Container C  │ │
  │  │ .10          │ .50 (MAC aa) │ .51 (MAC bb) │ .52 (MAC cc) │ │
  │  │ Real NIC     │ Virtual MAC  │ Virtual MAC  │ Virtual MAC  │ │
  │  └──────────────┴──────────────┴──────────────┴──────────────┘ │
  │                                                                 │
  │  $ docker network create -d macvlan \                           │
  │    --subnet=192.168.1.0/24 \                                    │
  │    --gateway=192.168.1.1 \                                      │
  │    -o parent=eth0 my-macvlan                                    │
  │                                                                 │
  │  USE CASES: Legacy apps needing physical network, DHCP integration│
  │  ⚠️ Cloud: AWS/GCP/Azure usually DON'T support macvlan          │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: macvlan = containers get their own MAC/IP on the physical network. Requires promiscuous mode on the host NIC. NOT supported by most cloud providers. Use for legacy app migration or when containers must appear as physical devices.

Networking Q125 of 200
What does the none network driver do?
A) It connects the container to all available networks
B) It gives the container a network stack with NO network interfaces except loopback — complete network isolation
C) It disables the Docker daemon's networking entirely
D) It uses the default bridge network with no DNS resolution
✅ Correct Answer: B

The none driver creates a container with only the loopback interface (lo) — no external network access whatsoever. The container has its own network namespace but zero connectivity beyond localhost. Use for security-sensitive batch jobs or containers that should never communicate with anything.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Network Drivers — Complete Comparison
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  DRIVER   │ SCOPE      │ ISOLATION │ DNS  │ USE CASE            │
  │  ─────────┼────────────┼───────────┼──────┼───────────────────── │
  │  bridge   │ Single host│ Partial   │ Yes* │ Default, dev, single │
  │  overlay  │ Multi-host │ Partial   │ Yes  │ Swarm services       │
  │  host     │ Host stack │ NONE      │ N/A  │ Performance critical │
  │  macvlan  │ Physical   │ Partial   │ Yes  │ Legacy apps, own IP  │
  │  none     │ Loopback   │ COMPLETE  │ No   │ Security, batch jobs │
  │  ─────────┴────────────┴───────────┴──────┴───────────────────── │
  │  * DNS only on user-defined bridge networks, not default docker0 │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: none = complete network isolation (loopback only). Know all 5 drivers (bridge, overlay, host, macvlan, none) and their use cases. The DCA exam frequently asks "which driver for scenario X?" — this comparison is key.

Networking Q126 of 200
How do you create a custom bridge network with a specific subnet?
A) Edit /etc/docker/daemon.json and add "bridge-subnet": "10.0.0.0/24"
B) docker bridge create --subnet 10.0.0.0/24 mynet
C) docker network create --driver bridge --subnet 10.0.0.0/24 --gateway 10.0.0.1 mynet
D) Custom subnets are not supported on bridge networks — only on overlay
✅ Correct Answer: C

docker network create creates user-defined networks. The --subnet flag specifies the CIDR subnet; --gateway sets the gateway IP (default: first usable address). Custom bridges enable DNS-based service discovery, unlike the default docker0 bridge.

📝 Detailed Explanation
docker network create — Custom Bridge Network
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ docker network create \                                      │
  │      --driver bridge \                                          │
  │      --subnet 10.100.0.0/24 \                                   │
  │      --gateway 10.100.0.1 \                                     │
  │      --ip-range 10.100.0.128/25 \                               │
  │      --label env=production \                                   │
  │      anihpj-net                                                 │
  │                                                                 │
  │  BENEFITS OVER DEFAULT docker0:                                 │
  │  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ ✅ Automatic DNS resolution (container name → IP)          │ │
  │  │ ✅ Better isolation (containers only see their network)   │ │
  │  │ ✅ Custom subnet/IPAM control                              │ │
  │  │ ✅ Connect/disconnect containers at runtime                │ │
  │  │ ✅ dns, dns-search, and dns-opt configuration             │ │
  │  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For the anihpj/jobpost stack, we create anihpj-net with a custom subnet to ensure the API, database, and Redis all communicate via DNS names (anihpj-db, anihpj-redis) instead of hardcoded IPs.

💡 DCA Exam Tip: docker network create makes USER-DEFINED networks with DNS. Default docker0 bridge has NO DNS. Always create custom networks for multi-container apps. Key flags: --subnet, --gateway, --ip-range, --driver.

Networking Q127 of 200
What does docker network inspect show?
A) Only the network driver and subnet
B) Real-time network traffic statistics
C) DNS query logs for the network
D) Full JSON: driver, subnet, gateway, connected containers (with IPs and MACs), labels, options, and IPAM config
✅ Correct Answer: D

docker network inspect <name> returns comprehensive JSON: network driver, IPAM configuration (subnet, gateway), ALL connected containers with their IP addresses and MACs, labels, options, scope (local/swarm), and internal flag. This is the go-to command for network troubleshooting.

📝 Detailed Explanation
docker network inspect — Network Diagnostics
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ docker network inspect anihpj-net                             │
  │  [                                                               │
  │    {                                                             │
  │      "Name": "anihpj-net",                                       │
  │      "Driver": "bridge",                                         │
  │      "Scope": "local",                                           │
  │      "IPAM": {                                                   │
  │        "Config": [{"Subnet": "10.100.0.0/24",                   │
  │                     "Gateway": "10.100.0.1"}]                    │
  │      },                                                          │
  │      "Containers": {                                             │
  │        "abc123...": {                                            │
  │          "Name": "anihpj-api",                                   │
  │          "IPv4Address": "10.100.0.2/24",                         │
  │          "MacAddress": "02:42:0a:64:00:02"                       │
  │        },                                                        │
  │        "def456...": {                                            │
  │          "Name": "anihpj-db",                                    │
  │          "IPv4Address": "10.100.0.3/24"                          │
  │        }                                                         │
  │      }                                                           │
  │    }                                                             │
  │  ]                                                               │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
