  │  SWARM (production, ENCRYPTED):                                 │
  │  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ $ docker secret create db-pass ./db-password.txt           │ │
  │  │ # stack.yml                                               │ │
  │  │ secrets:                                                  │ │
  │  │   db-pass:                                                │ │
  │  │     external: true  ← must already exist in Swarm         │ │
  │  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: Compose secrets = file-based, NOT encrypted (dev only). Swarm secrets = Raft-encrypted (production). For stack deploy, secrets must be pre-created with docker secret create and referenced as external: true.

Security Q163 of 200
Why should secrets NEVER be placed in .env files committed to version control?
A) .env files committed to Git are visible to anyone with repository access — and they persist in Git history forever, even after deletion
B) Docker doesn't support .env files — they're ignored at runtime
C) .env files are automatically encrypted by Docker
D) .env files slow down container startup
✅ Correct Answer: A

Environment files committed to Git are a major security leak vector. Even if you delete the file later, it remains in Git history (git log -p). Use .gitignore to exclude .env, store secrets in Swarm secrets or a vault (HashiCorp Vault, AWS Secrets Manager), and provide a .env.example template with placeholder values.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Secret Management — The Right Way
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ❌ WRONG (secrets in Git):           ✅ RIGHT (secrets external):│
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────┐    ┌──────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ # .env (COMMITTED! 😱)      │    │ # .gitignore          │  │
  │  │ DB_PASSWORD=supersecret     │    │ .env  ← excluded!     │  │
  │  │ API_KEY=sk-abc123           │    │                       │  │
  │  │                              │    │ # .env.example        │  │
  │  │ $ git log -p → SECRETS!     │    │ DB_PASSWORD=changeme  │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────────┘    │ (template, safe)      │  │
  │                                       └──────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                 │
  │  PRODUCTION: Swarm secrets, HashiCorp Vault, AWS Secrets Manager│
  │  CI/CD: GitHub Secrets, GitLab CI Variables, Jenkins Credentials│
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: NEVER commit .env files to Git — they persist in history forever. Add .env to .gitignore. Provide .env.example as a template. Use Swarm secrets for production. Use CI/CD secret management for pipelines.

Security Q164 of 200
What is the correct way to provide the anihpj database password to the Postgres container in production?
A) Set POSTGRES_PASSWORD=mysecret in the Dockerfile ENV instruction
B) Pass -e POSTGRES_PASSWORD=mysecret on docker run
C) Create a Swarm secret (docker secret create db-pass), mount it in the service, and use POSTGRES_PASSWORD_FILE=/run/secrets/db-pass
D) Store the password in docker-compose.yml under environment:
✅ Correct Answer: C

In production, the database password should be a Swarm secret. The official Postgres image supports POSTGRES_PASSWORD_FILE — it reads the password from a file instead of an environment variable. This prevents the password from appearing in docker inspect, process lists, or image layers.

📝 Detailed Explanation
anihpj DB Password — Production Secret Pattern
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  # 1. Create the secret                                         │
  │  $ echo "my-production-pw-2026" | docker secret create db-pass -│
  │                                                                 │
  │  # 2. Create the service (or in stack.yml)                      │
  │  $ docker service create \                                      │
  │      --name anihpj-db \                                         │
  │      --secret db-pass \                                         │
  │      -e POSTGRES_PASSWORD_FILE=/run/secrets/db-pass \           │
  │      -e POSTGRES_USER=anihpj \                                  │
  │      -e POSTGRES_DB=jobpost \                                   │
  │      postgres:15                                                │
  │                                                                 │
  │  # 3. Postgres reads password from FILE, not env var            │
  │  → Password never exposed in docker inspect or process list    │
  │                                                                 │
  │  ❌ DOCKERFILE: ENV POSTGRES_PASSWORD=... ← visible in history │
  │  ❌ docker run -e POSTGRES_PASSWORD=... ← visible in inspect  │
  │  ✅ Swarm secret + _FILE variant ← production-ready            │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: Use _FILE variants of environment variables (POSTGRES_PASSWORD_FILE, MYSQL_PASSWORD_FILE) with Swarm secrets. Never put passwords in Dockerfiles, docker run -e, or docker-compose environment:. Swarm secrets + _FILE = production pattern.

5.3 Container Isolation & Linux SecurityQ165-Q172

Security Q165 of 200
What Linux kernel feature provides the foundation of container isolation?
A) hypervisor — each container gets its own kernel
B) namespaces — wrap global system resources so each container sees its own isolated instance (PID, NET, MNT, UTS, IPC, USER, cgroup)
C) chroot — changes the root directory only
D) SELinux — mandatory access control labels on files
✅ Correct Answer: B

Linux namespaces are the core isolation primitive. Each container gets its own: PID namespace (own process tree), NET namespace (own network stack), MNT namespace (own filesystem mounts), UTS namespace (own hostname), IPC namespace (own inter-process communication), USER namespace (own UID/GID mapping). Containers share the host kernel — unlike VMs.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Linux Namespaces — Container Isolation Primitives
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  HOST KERNEL (shared)                                            │
  │  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │  CONTAINER A              CONTAINER B                     │ │
  │  │  ┌──────────────────┐    ┌──────────────────┐            │ │
  │  │  │ PID NS: 1,2,3... │    │ PID NS: 1,2,3... │  (isolated)│ │
  │  │  │ NET NS: eth0     │    │ NET NS: eth0     │  (isolated)│ │
  │  │  │ MNT NS: /app     │    │ MNT NS: /app     │  (isolated)│ │
  │  │  │ UTS NS: web-01   │    │ UTS NS: web-02   │  (isolated)│ │
  │  │  │ IPC NS: msg queue │    │ IPC NS: msg queue│  (isolated)│ │
  │  │  │ USER NS: 0→1000  │    │ USER NS: 0→1001  │  (isolated)│ │
  │  │  └──────────────────┘    └──────────────────┘            │ │
  │  │       SHARE KERNEL (no hypervisor, no guest OS!)         │ │
  │  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: Containers = process isolation via namespaces, NOT virtualization. Know the 7 namespace types: PID, NET, MNT, UTS, IPC, USER, cgroup. Containers share the host kernel — this is why Linux containers must match the host kernel architecture.

Security Q166 of 200
What do cgroups (control groups) do for containers?
A) They provide filesystem isolation between containers
B) They encrypt inter-container network traffic
C) They authenticate users accessing the Docker daemon
D) They limit, account for, and isolate resource usage (CPU, memory, disk I/O, network) per container — preventing noisy neighbors
✅ Correct Answer: D

cgroups (control groups) are the Linux kernel feature that limits and monitors resource usage per process group. Docker uses cgroups to enforce --memory, --cpus, --blkio-weight limits. Without cgroups, one container could consume all host resources (noisy neighbor problem). cgroups v2 is the current standard.

📝 Detailed Explanation
cgroups — Resource Limiting and Accounting
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  WITHOUT CGROUPS:                    WITH CGROUPS:               │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────┐       ┌──────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ Container A: CPU hog     │       │ Container A: max 1 CPU│   │
  │  │ → Uses 100% CPU          │       │ Container B: max 0.5 │   │
  │  │ Container B: starved 😱  │       │ Container C: max 512MB│   │
  │  └──────────────────────────┘       │ → FAIR! No starvation │   │
  │                                      └──────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                 │
  │  CGROUP SUBSYSTEMS (v2):                                        │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ cpu     → CPU usage limits (--cpus, --cpu-shares)        │  │
  │  │ memory  → Memory limits (--memory, --memory-swap)        │  │
  │  │ io      → Disk I/O limits (--blkio-weight, --device-*)   │  │
  │  │ pids    → Max processes per container (--pids-limit)     │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: cgroups = resource limiting (CPU, memory, I/O). namespaces = isolation (what you can see). Together they make containers secure. cgroups v2 is default on modern systems. docker stats reads from cgroups. Know the difference between namespaces and cgroups.

Security Q167 of 200
What is the best practice for Linux capabilities in containers?
A) Drop ALL capabilities (--cap-drop=ALL), then add back only what's needed (--cap-add=NET_BIND_SERVICE for binding to ports < 1024)
B) Use the default capabilities — Docker already limits them sufficiently
C) Add --cap-add=ALL for maximum compatibility
D) Capabilities are a Kubernetes feature, not available in Docker
✅ Correct Answer: A

Docker starts containers with a default set of capabilities (around 14). Best practice: drop ALL capabilities with --cap-drop=ALL, then add back only what your application truly needs. For example, nginx needs --cap-add=NET_BIND_SERVICE to bind to port 80. This follows the principle of least privilege.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Linux Capabilities — Least Privilege Approach
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  DOCKER DEFAULT (~14 caps):          LEAST PRIVILEGE (safe):    │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────┐       ┌──────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ CHOWN, DAC_OVERRIDE,     │       │ --cap-drop=ALL \     │   │
  │  │ FOWNER, FSETID, KILL,    │       │ --cap-add=NET_BIND_  │   │
  │  │ SETGID, SETUID, NET_BIND │       │   SERVICE \          │   │
  │  │ _SERVICE, NET_RAW,       │       │ --cap-add=CHOWN      │   │
  │  │ SYS_CHROOT, MKNOD,       │       │ → Only 2 caps!       │   │
  │  │ AUDIT_WRITE, SETFCAP     │       └──────────────────────┘   │
  │  └──────────────────────────┘                                   │
  │                                                                 │
  │  KEY CAPABILITIES TO KNOW:                                      │
  │  NET_BIND_SERVICE → Bind to ports < 1024 (nginx, web servers)  │
  │  SYS_TIME         → Set system time (NTP containers)           │
  │  SYS_ADMIN        → Almost root — AVOID unless essential       │
  │  NET_ADMIN        → Network config (iptables, routes)          │
  │  SYS_PTRACE       → Debug processes (strace, gdb)             │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: --cap-drop=ALL + --cap-add=<needed> = least privilege. Docker runs with ~14 default caps — drop what you don't need. NET_BIND_SERVICE is commonly needed for web servers. SYS_ADMIN is almost equivalent to root — avoid it.

Security Q168 of 200
Why should you NEVER use --privileged in production?
A) It makes the container start slower
B) It's deprecated and no longer supported
C) It disables ALL security features — gives the container ALL capabilities, access to ALL host devices, and disables seccomp/AppArmor — essentially granting root on the HOST
D) It prevents the container from accessing the network
✅ Correct Answer: C

--privileged is a nuclear option — it gives the container ALL Linux capabilities, access to ALL host devices, and disables seccomp, AppArmor, and other security mechanisms. A compromised privileged container means a compromised HOST. There is almost always a more granular alternative.

📝 Detailed Explanation
--privileged — The Nuclear Option (AVOID!)
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  NORMAL CONTAINER:                   --privileged CONTAINER:     │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────┐       ┌──────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ • Limited capabilities   │       │ • ALL capabilities    │   │
  │  │ • Seccomp enabled        │       │ • NO seccomp          │   │
  │  │ • AppArmor enabled       │       │ • NO AppArmor         │   │
  │  │ • No host devices        │       │ • ALL host devices    │   │
  │  │ • Isolated namespace     │       │ • Weak isolation      │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────┘       └──────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                 │
  │  INSTEAD OF --privileged, USE:                                  │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ Need devices?   → --device /dev/fuse                      │  │
  │  │ Need caps?      → --cap-add=SYS_ADMIN (specific cap)     │  │
  │  │ Need seccomp?   → --security-opt seccomp=profile.json    │  │
  │  │ Need AppArmor?  → --security-opt apparmor=profile        │  │
  │  │ Docker-in-Docker?→ Use dind image, not --privileged      │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: --privileged = ALL caps + ALL devices + NO seccomp + NO AppArmor ≈ HOST ROOT. NEVER in production. Use granular alternatives: --device, --cap-add, --security-opt. The DCA exam frequently tests this security anti-pattern.

Security Q169 of 200
What is a seccomp profile in Docker?
A) A network firewall configuration for containers
B) A user authentication profile for Docker Hub
C) A storage compression algorithm
D) A whitelist/blacklist of Linux system calls — Docker's default profile blocks ~44 dangerous syscalls out of ~300+ available
✅ Correct Answer: D

seccomp (secure computing mode) filters which system calls a container can make. Docker's default seccomp profile blocks ~44 dangerous syscalls (like mount, reboot, kexec_load) while allowing ~300+ safe ones. Custom profiles can be even more restrictive: --security-opt seccomp=/path/to/profile.json.

📝 Detailed Explanation
seccomp — System Call Filtering
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  WITHOUT SECCOMP:                     WITH SECCOMP:              │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────┐      ┌──────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ Container can call ANY     │      │ Container can call    │  │
  │  │ ~300+ syscalls             │      │ ~260 safe syscalls   │  │
  │  │                            │      │ BLOCKED (~44):        │  │
  │  │ mount() → mount filesystem │      │ mount()    ❌ BLOCKED │  │
  │  │ reboot() → reboot host 😱  │      │ reboot()   ❌ BLOCKED │  │
  │  │ kexec() → load new kernel  │      │ kexec()    ❌ BLOCKED │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────┘      │                     │  │
  │                                       │ write(), read() ✅ OK │  │
  │  CUSTOM PROFILE:                     └──────────────────────┘  │
  │  $ docker run --security-opt \                                  │
  │      seccomp=/path/to/custom.json \                             │
  │      myapp                                                      │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: seccomp = syscall filtering. Docker defaults block ~44 dangerous syscalls. Custom profiles for stricter control. --security-opt seccomp=unconfined disables seccomp entirely (equivalent to --privileged for syscalls). Know that seccomp reduces kernel attack surface.

Security Q170 of 200
What are AppArmor and SELinux in the context of Docker security?
A) Network encryption protocols for container communication
B) Mandatory Access Control (MAC) systems — they enforce per-container profiles that restrict file, network, and capability access beyond Linux DAC
C) Container orchestration alternatives to Docker Swarm
D) Image vulnerability scanning tools
✅ Correct Answer: B

AppArmor (Ubuntu/Debian) and SELinux (RHEL/CentOS) are Mandatory Access Control systems that add an extra layer of security on top of standard Linux Discretionary Access Control (DAC). Docker generates default profiles, but custom profiles can lock down containers further — restricting which files they can read, which capabilities they can use, and which network operations they can perform.

📝 Detailed Explanation
AppArmor vs SELinux — MAC for Containers
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  LAYER 1: DAC (Discretionary Access Control)                    │
  │  → File permissions (rwx), user/group ownership                 │
  │                                                                 │
  │  LAYER 2: MAC (Mandatory Access Control) — Docker integration   │
  │  ┌──────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ AppArmor (Ubuntu)│ SELinux (RHEL/CentOS)                │   │
  │  ├──────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┤   │
  │  │ Profile per app  │ Type enforcement per container       │   │
  │  │ Path-based rules │ Label-based rules                    │   │
  │  │ $ docker run \   │ $ docker run \                       │   │
  │  │   --security-opt \│   --security-opt \                   │   │
  │  │   apparmor=custom│   label=type:container_t             │   │
  │  └──────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                 │
  │  BOTH: Restrict file access, network, capabilities, mounts     │
  │        Beyond what namespaces + cgroups provide alone           │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: AppArmor (Ubuntu) and SELinux (RHEL) = MAC systems for containers. Docker auto-generates default profiles. Custom profiles via --security-opt apparmor=PROFILE or --security-opt label=type:TYPE. They add defense-in-depth beyond namespaces and capabilities.

Security Q171 of 200
What does user namespace remapping (userns-remap) do?
A) Maps the container's root user (UID 0) to a high-numbered, unprivileged UID on the host — so even "root" in the container is nobody on the host
B) Syncs container user accounts with the host's /etc/passwd
C) Allows containers to create new users at runtime
D) Migrates users between Docker and Kubernetes
✅ Correct Answer: A

User namespace remapping maps container UIDs to different host UIDs. For example, container root (UID 0) maps to host UID 100000 — a completely unprivileged user. Even if an attacker escapes the container as "root," they're actually a nobody user on the host with zero privileges. Configured via "userns-remap": "default" in daemon.json.

📝 Detailed Explanation
User Namespace Remapping — Root is NOT Root
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  WITHOUT USENS-REMAP:                 WITH USENS-REMAP:          │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────┐      ┌──────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ Container: UID 0 (root)    │      │ Container: UID 0     │  │
  │  │            │               │      │        │              │  │
  │  │            ▼               │      │        ▼              │  │
  │  │ Host:      UID 0 (ROOT!)😱 │      │ Host: UID 100000     │  │
  │  │                            │      │ (nobody, no perms!)  │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────┘      └──────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                 │
  │  CONFIGURATION (daemon.json):                                   │
  │  {                                                               │
  │    "userns-remap": "default"                                     │
  │  }                                                               │
  │  → Creates dockremap user, maps container 0:65536 → host high   │
  │                                                                 │
  │  ⚠️ RESTRICTIONS: No --privileged, host network, or PID=host   │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: userns-remap = container root ≠ host root. Maps container UID 0 → high host UID (e.g., 100000). Configured in daemon.json. Limitations: incompatible with --privileged, host networking, and some volume mounts. A powerful defense-in-depth measure.

Security Q172 of 200
What does --read-only do when running a container?
A) It prevents the container from reading any files
B) It makes the container image read-only on the registry
C) It mounts the container's root filesystem as read-only — the container cannot modify any files unless a writable volume or tmpfs is explicitly mounted
D) It disables the container's stdout/stderr output
✅ Correct Answer: C

--read-only makes the container's root filesystem immutable — no writes allowed. Applications that need to write (logs, temp files, caches) must have writable volumes or tmpfs mounts explicitly specified. This is a powerful security measure: even if compromised, the attacker can't modify binaries or install backdoors.

📝 Detailed Explanation
--read-only — Immutable Container Filesystem
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  NORMAL CONTAINER:                    --read-only CONTAINER:     │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────┐    ┌──────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ / (R/W)                      │    │ / (READ-ONLY)        │  │
  │  │ ├── /app (R/W)               │    │ ├── /app (locked)    │  │
  │  │ ├── /etc (R/W)               │    │ ├── /etc (locked)    │  │
  │  │ ├── /tmp (R/W)               │    │ ├── /tmp → tmpfs ✅  │  │
  │  │ └── /var/log (R/W)           │    │ └── /var/log → vol ✅ │  │
  │  │                              │    │                       │  │
  │  │ Attacker: modify /bin/ls!😱  │    │ Attacker: can't write│  │
  │  └──────────────────────────────┘    │ to /bin! 🛡️           │  │
  │                                       └──────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                 │
  │  $ docker run --read-only \                                     │
  │      --tmpfs /tmp \                                             │
  │      --tmpfs /run \                                             │
  │      -v app-logs:/var/log \                                     │
  │      myapp                                                      │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: --read-only = immutable root filesystem. Use with --tmpfs for /tmp and /run, and volumes for writable data. Prevents attackers from modifying binaries or installing backdoors. Great for stateless web apps — combine with non-root USER for defense-in-depth.

5.4 Security Best PracticesQ173-Q180

Security Q173 of 200
Why should production Docker images always use a non-root USER?
A) Non-root users have better performance due to kernel optimizations
B) If the container is compromised, the attacker is limited to that user's permissions — not root. This is defense-in-depth: namespaces may fail, but file permissions still apply
C) Docker Hub requires non-root users for all official images
D) Root users cannot access mounted volumes
✅ Correct Answer: B

Running as non-root is defense-in-depth. Even if namespace isolation fails (kernel exploit, misconfiguration), the attacker is still limited by standard Linux file permissions. Combine with USER 1000:1000 in Dockerfile, --read-only filesystem, and dropped capabilities for maximum protection.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Non-Root USER — Defense-in-Depth
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ROOT CONTAINER (bad):                NON-ROOT CONTAINER (good): │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────┐    ┌──────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ $ docker run --user root ... │    │ USER 1000:1000       │  │
  │  │                              │    │                       │  │
  │  │ If namespace escape:         │    │ If namespace escape: │  │
  │  │ → Attacker is ROOT on host  │    │ → Attacker is UID    │  │
  │  │ → Can read /etc/shadow      │    │   1000 (no perms!)   │  │
  │  │ → Can install kernel module │    │ → Can't read shadow  │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────────┘    └──────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                 │
  │  DOCKERFILE:                                                    │
  │  RUN addgroup -S appgroup && adduser -S appuser -G appgroup     │
  │  USER appuser                                                   │
  │  → All subsequent RUN, CMD, ENTRYPOINT run as appuser           │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: USER <uid> in Dockerfile = non-root. Defense-in-depth: even if containment fails, file permissions limit damage. Distroless images default to non-root. Combine with --read-only, dropped caps, and seccomp for maximum security.

Security Q174 of 200
How do minimal base images (alpine, distroless, scratch) improve security?
A) They include built-in antivirus scanning
B) They encrypt all data stored in the image
C) They automatically update themselves when vulnerabilities are found
D) They contain fewer packages → fewer potential CVEs → smaller attack surface. Distroless also removes the shell, eliminating a common attack vector
✅ Correct Answer: D

Smaller images = fewer packages = fewer CVEs. Alpine has ~5 packages; Debian full has ~200+. Distroless goes further: no shell, no package manager, no utilities — just the runtime. If an attacker can't spawn a shell, they can't explore the filesystem, install tools, or pivot to other systems.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Image Size vs Security — The Correlation
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  IMAGE        │ SIZE   │ PACKAGES │ CVEs  │ SHELL  │ ATTACK SURF│
  │  ─────────────┼────────┼──────────┼───────┼────────┼───────────│
  │  Debian full  │ 1GB    │ 200+     │ 100+  │ bash   │ HUGE      │
  │  Slim         │ 150MB  │ 50+      │ 30-50 │ bash   │ MEDIUM    │
  │  Alpine       │ 50MB   │ 5-10     │ 0-5   │ ash    │ SMALL     │
  │  Distroless   │ 50MB   │ 3-5      │ 0-2   │ NONE!  │ MINIMAL   │
  │  Scratch      │ ~0MB   │ 1 (app)  │ 0     │ NONE!  │ TINY      │
  │  ─────────────┴────────┴──────────┴───────┴────────┴───────────│
  │                                                                 │
  │  RULE OF THUMB: If you don't need it, don't include it.         │
  │  Every package = potential CVE. Every tool = attacker's toolkit.│
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: Smaller images = smaller attack surface. Alpine/distorless/scratch dramatically reduce CVEs. Distroless has NO shell — attackers can't explore. The DCA expects you to know the security tradeoffs of different base image types.

Security Q175 of 200
Where should image vulnerability scanning occur in the CI/CD pipeline?
A) BEFORE pushing to registry — scan the built image, fail the pipeline if CRITICAL/HIGH CVEs are found (shift-left security)
B) After deploying to production — scan running containers
C) Only when a security incident is reported
D) Scanning is automatic — no pipeline integration needed
✅ Correct Answer: A

Scanning should be integrated into CI/CD (shift-left): after docker build, immediately run docker scout cves or trivy image. If CRITICAL or HIGH CVEs are found, FAIL the pipeline — the image never reaches the registry, let alone production. This catches vulnerabilities minutes after they're introduced, not days later.

📝 Detailed Explanation
CI/CD Security Scanning — Shift-Left Pipeline
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  CI/CD PIPELINE (anihpj/jobpost):                                │
  │                                                                 │
  │  1. git push → trigger pipeline                                 │
  │  2. docker build -t anihpj-api:$SHA .                           │
  │  3. docker scout cves anihpj-api:$SHA  ← SCAN (shift-left)     │
  │     ├── CRITICAL CVEs → 🛑 FAIL PIPELINE                       │
  │     ├── HIGH CVEs     → ⚠️  WARN + optional block             │
  │     └── Clean         → ✅ continue                             │
  │  4. docker push anihpj-api:$SHA                                 │
  │  5. docker stack deploy -c stack.yml anihpj                     │
  │                                                                 │
  │  TOOLS: Trivy, Snyk, Docker Scout, Clair, Grype                │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: Scan BEFORE push = shift-left security. Fail pipeline on CRITICAL/HIGH CVEs. Tools: Docker Scout, Trivy (open-source), Snyk. The DCA expects you to know that scanning happens in CI/CD, not just in production monitoring.

Security Q176 of 200
What is Docker Bench Security?
A) A performance benchmark for comparing container runtimes
B) A Docker Hub service that rates image security on a scale of 1-100
C) An open-source script that checks Docker hosts against the CIS Docker Benchmark — ~100+ security configuration checks
D) A competition for finding Docker vulnerabilities
✅ Correct Answer: C

Docker Bench Security is a script that audits your Docker host against the CIS (Center for Internet Security) Docker Benchmark. It checks: host configuration, daemon configuration, daemon configuration files, container images and build files, container runtime, and Docker Swarm configuration. Run it regularly on production hosts.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Docker Bench Security — CIS Benchmark Auditing
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ docker run --rm --net host --pid host \                      │
  │      -v /var/run/docker.sock:/var/run/docker.sock \             │
  │      -v /etc:/etc \                                             │
  │      docker/docker-bench-security                               │
  │                                                                 │
  │  CHECKS (~100+):                                                │
  │  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ 1. Host Configuration (10 checks)                         │ │
  │  │ 2. Docker Daemon Configuration (15 checks)                │ │
  │  │ 3. Docker Daemon Config Files (5 checks)                  │ │
  │  │ 4. Container Images & Build (10 checks)                    │ │
  │  │ 5. Container Runtime (25 checks)                          │ │
  │  │ 6. Docker Security Operations (10 checks)                 │ │
  │  │ 7. Docker Swarm Configuration (10 checks)                 │ │
  │  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │                                                                 │
  │  OUTPUT: [PASS] / [WARN] / [INFO] for each check               │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: Docker Bench Security = CIS benchmark auditing script. Run regularly on production hosts. Checks host config, daemon, containers, Swarm. Outputs PASS/WARN/INFO. It's a container that audits the host — needs host-level access (--pid host, --net host, docker.sock).

Security Q177 of 200
Why is exposing /var/run/docker.sock a critical security risk?
A) It contains encrypted passwords that can be brute-forced
B) It slows down container startup due to socket contention
C) It exposes the Docker daemon logs to unauthorized readers
D) Mounting docker.sock into a container grants FULL Docker API access — containers can start privileged containers, mount host filesystems, and escape to the host
✅ Correct Answer: D

/var/run/docker.sock is the Docker daemon's Unix socket — owning it means owning Docker. A container with access to the socket can: run privileged containers (--privileged), mount the host root filesystem, and escape to the host. This is one of the most dangerous anti-patterns and a frequent DCA exam topic.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Docker Socket Exposure — Attack Chain
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  DANGEROUS: docker run -v /var/run/docker.sock:/var/run/docker.│
  │  sock ...                                                       │
  │                                                                 │
  │  ATTACK CHAIN (inside the compromised container):               │
  │  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ $ docker run -it --privileged \                           │ │
  │  │     -v /:/host \                                          │ │
  │  │     alpine chroot /host                                   │ │
  │  │ → FULL HOST ROOT ACCESS! 😱                                │ │
  │  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │                                                                 │
  │  SAFER ALTERNATIVES:                                            │
  │  • Kaniko: Build images without Docker daemon                  │
  │  • Buildah: Rootless image building                            │
  │  • docker:dind: Separate Docker daemon (still needs privileges)│
  │  • CI/CD: Use platform-native builders (GitHub Actions, etc.)  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: docker.sock = root on host. NEVER mount it into untrusted containers. Use Kaniko or Buildah for daemonless image building. The DCA exam tests this frequently — know why it's dangerous and what alternatives exist.

Security Q178 of 200
How do secrets end up in Docker image layers, and how do you prevent it?
A) Docker automatically strips secrets from image layers
B) Secrets leak via COPY/ADD of .env files, RUN commands that use credentials, or environment variables baked with ENV. Prevent with .dockerignore, multi-stage builds, BuildKit --mount=type=secret, and Swarm secrets at runtime
C) Secrets only leak if you use docker push without --encrypt flag
D) Secrets cannot leak — Docker encrypts all image layers
✅ Correct Answer: B

Secrets leak into image layers through: (1) COPY/ADD of .env files, (2) RUN commands with credentials (RUN curl -u admin:pass http://...), (3) ENV instructions with secrets. Once in a layer, they persist FOREVER — even if deleted in a later layer (the old layer still contains them). Prevention: .dockerignore, BuildKit secrets, runtime Swarm secrets.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Secret Leakage — How It Happens and Prevention
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ❌ LEAK PATTERNS (NEVER DO THIS):                               │
  │  COPY .env .                             ← .env in image!      │
  │  RUN curl -u admin:MyPassword http://...  ← in layer history!   │
  │  ENV DATABASE_PASSWORD=secret            ← in image inspect!   │
  │                                                                 │
  │  ✅ PREVENTION:                                                  │
  │  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ .dockerignore:                                            │ │
  │  │   .env                                                     │ │
  │  │   *.pem                                                    │ │
  │  │                                                           │ │
  │  │ BuildKit secrets (build-time, NOT in layer):              │ │
  │  │ RUN --mount=type=secret,id=aws_creds \                    │ │
  │  │     aws s3 cp s3://bucket/file /app/                      │ │
  │  │                                                           │ │
  │  │ Swarm secrets (runtime):                                  │ │
  │  │ docker secret create db-pass ./pw.txt                     │ │
  │  │ docker service create --secret db-pass ...                │ │
  │  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: Secrets in layers are FOREVER — deleting in a later layer doesn't remove from earlier layers. Prevention: .dockerignore, BuildKit --mount=type=secret, Swarm secrets at runtime. Use docker image history to audit for leaked secrets.

Security Q179 of 200
What is the relationship between docker scan and Snyk?
A) docker scan was powered by Snyk — it provided vulnerability scanning for local images. It has been superseded by docker scout
B) docker scan and Snyk are competitors with no relationship
C) docker scan uses Snyk to encrypt container images
D) docker scan is a Snyk alternative built from scratch by Docker
✅ Correct Answer: A

The original docker scan command (Docker 19.03+) was powered by Snyk's vulnerability database. In 2023+, Docker introduced docker scout as its own built-in supply chain security tool, replacing docker scan. While docker scan may still work on older versions, docker scout is the current recommended tool for the DCA exam.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Docker Security Scanning — Evolution
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  THEN (~2019-2023):                    NOW (2023+):              │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────┐    ┌──────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ $ docker scan nginx:latest   │    │ $ docker scout \     │  │
  │  │ → Powered by Snyk            │    │   quickview nginx    │  │
  │  │ → Showed CVEs                │    │ → Docker's own engine │  │
  │  │ → Limited to Docker Hub      │    │ → CVEs + SBOM +      │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────────┘    │   policies + fixes   │  │
  │                                       └──────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                 │
  │  CURRENT TOOLS: docker scout (Docker), trivy (Aqua), snyk (Snyk)│
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: docker scan (legacy) = Snyk-powered. docker scout (current) = Docker's own tool. Know both for the exam — docker scout is the modern recommendation with SBOM, policies, and fix recommendations. trivy is the popular open-source alternative.

Security Q180 of 200
What is the best practice for AWS security when running Docker on ECS?
A) Use the root AWS account credentials for ECS task execution
B) Store AWS credentials in the Docker image as environment variables
C) Use IAM Roles for Tasks — each ECS task gets its own IAM role with least-privilege permissions (e.g., ECR read-only for pulls, S3 access for data)
D) Disable all AWS security features — Docker handles security internally
✅ Correct Answer: C

ECS task IAM roles provide temporary, automatically-rotated credentials to each task — no access keys on disk. Each task gets exactly the permissions it needs (least privilege): ECR read-only for pulling images, S3 access for data, Secrets Manager for secrets. This is far more secure than storing static credentials in images or environment variables.

📝 Detailed Explanation
AWS ECS Security — IAM Roles for Tasks
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  AWS CLOUD                                                        │
  │  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │  ECS TASK: anihpj-api                                      │  │
  │  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │  │
  │  │  │ IAM Role: ecsTaskRole-anihpj-api                     │  │  │
  │  │  │                                                      │  │  │
  │  │  │ Permissions (least privilege):                       │  │  │
  │  │  │ • ecr:GetAuthorizationToken  (pull images)           │  │  │
  │  │  │ • ecr:BatchGetImage          (pull images)           │  │  │
  │  │  │ • secretsmanager:GetSecretValue (DB password)        │  │  │
  │  │  │ • s3:GetObject               (static assets)         │  │  │
  │  │  │                                                      │  │  │
  │  │  │ ✅ NO static credentials in image or env vars!       │  │  │
  │  │  │ ✅ Temporary creds auto-rotated by AWS               │  │  │
  │  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │  │
  │  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: AWS ECS = IAM Roles for Tasks (least privilege). NEVER store AWS credentials in Docker images. Use ECR for image storage (IAM auth), Secrets Manager for secrets. ECS task roles provide automatic, temporary credential rotation.

💾 Category 6: Storage & Volumes10% — Q181-Q200

🗄️ Category 6: Storage & Volumes — 20 Questions

This category is about persisting data beyond the container lifecycle. Think of it like deciding where to store important documents — a temporary sticky note (tmpfs), your desk drawer (volume), or a filing cabinet (bind mount). Master volume types, volume management, and storage drivers. 10% of the DCA exam.

6.1 Volume TypesQ181-Q186

Storage Q181 of 200
What is an anonymous volume in Docker?
A) A volume that can be accessed by any container on the host
B) A volume created with docker volume create --anonymous
C) A volume created automatically when you use -v /container/path (no host path) or VOLUME in Dockerfile — gets a random hash name, hard to track
D) A volume shared anonymously between Swarm nodes
✅ Correct Answer: C

Anonymous volumes are created when you use -v /container/path (no host path, no volume name) or the VOLUME instruction in a Dockerfile. Docker assigns a random hash as the name — they're hard to identify and manage. Prefer named volumes (-v myvolume:/path) for clarity.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Anonymous vs Named Volumes
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ANONYMOUS (auto-created, messy):      NAMED (explicit, clean):  │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────┐     ┌──────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ $ docker run -v /data nginx  │     │ $ docker volume \    │  │
  │  │ → Creates volume:            │     │   create pgdata      │  │
  │  │   a1b2c3d4... (random hash!)│     │ $ docker run \       │  │
  │  │                              │     │   -v pgdata:/data \  │  │
  │  │ $ docker volume ls           │     │   postgres           │  │
  │  │ DRIVER  VOLUME NAME          │     │                      │  │
  │  │ local   a1b2c3d4e5f6...     │     │ DRIVER  VOLUME NAME  │  │
  │  │ (Which container uses this?!)│     │ local   pgdata       │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────────┘     │ (Clear name!)        │  │
  │                                        └──────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                 │
  │  ⚠️ Anonymous volumes are NOT removed with docker rm.          │
  │     Use docker rm -v to remove them with the container.        │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: Anonymous volumes = random hash names, hard to track. Created by -v /path (no name) or VOLUME in Dockerfile. Named volumes = explicit names, easy to manage. Always prefer named volumes. docker rm -v removes anonymous volumes with the container.

Storage Q182 of 200
How do you create and use a named volume?
A) docker volume create myvol → docker run -v myvol:/app/data myimage — the volume persists even after the container is removed
B) docker run --create-volume myvol:/app/data myimage
C) Add VOLUME myvol to the Dockerfile
D) Named volumes are created automatically — no explicit creation needed
✅ Correct Answer: A

Named volumes are the recommended way to persist data. Create with docker volume create, then mount with -v <name>:<path>. The volume survives container removal — data persists until you explicitly delete the volume with docker volume rm. Named volumes are managed by Docker and stored in /var/lib/docker/volumes/.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Named Volume Lifecycle
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  CREATE:                     USE:                               │
  │  $ docker volume create \    $ docker run -d \                  │
  │      pgdata                    -v pgdata:/var/lib/postgresql \  │
  │                                --name db postgres:15            │
  │  INSPECT:                    REMOVE:                            │
  │  $ docker volume inspect \   $ docker volume rm pgdata          │
  │      pgdata                  → Fails if any container uses it! │
  │  [{"Mountpoint":             → Use -f to force remove           │
  │    "/var/lib/docker/                                           │
  │     volumes/pgdata/_data"}]                                    │
  │                                                                 │
  │  ✅ Volume SURVIVES: docker rm db (container gone, data safe)  │
  │  ❌ Volume DELETED:  docker volume rm pgdata (explicit action) │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For the anihpj/jobpost Postgres database, we use a named volume pgdata — the database files survive container restarts, upgrades, and even accidental removal of the Postgres container.

💡 DCA Exam Tip: Named volumes = the gold standard for persistent data. docker volume create → -v name:path. Volumes survive container removal. Stored in /var/lib/docker/volumes/. Use docker volume ls/inspect/rm to manage. Always name your volumes.

Storage Q183 of 200
What is a bind mount and when should you use it?
A) A network-mounted filesystem that binds containers to remote storage
B) A Docker-managed volume that binds to the container's lifecycle
C) A temporary in-memory mount that disappears when the container stops
D) Mounting a specific host directory or file into a container — use for development (live code reload) or when you need specific host paths
✅ Correct Answer: D

Bind mounts map a specific host path to a container path: -v /host/path:/container/path. Unlike volumes (Docker-managed), bind mounts rely on the host's filesystem structure. Ideal for development (mount source code for live reload) or sharing host config files (e.g., /etc/nginx/nginx.conf).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Bind Mounts vs Volumes — Host Path vs Docker-Managed
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  BIND MOUNT (host path):              VOLUME (Docker-managed):   │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────┐    ┌──────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ $ docker run -v \            │    │ $ docker run -v \    │  │
  │  │   /home/dev/app:/app \       │    │   pgdata:/data \     │  │
  │  │   myapp                      │    │   postgres           │  │
  │  │                              │    │                      │  │
  │  │ Host: /home/dev/app          │    │ Docker manages path: │  │
  │  │   ↕ direct mapping           │    │ /var/lib/docker/     │  │
  │  │ Container: /app              │    │   volumes/pgdata/... │  │
  │  │                              │    │                      │  │
  │  │ ✅ Dev: live code reload     │    │ ✅ Prod: persistent  │  │
  │  │ ✅ Config: share host files  │    │    data, backups     │  │
  │  │ ❌ Prod: host dependency     │    │ ❌ Dev: not live sync│  │
  │  └──────────────────────────────┘    └──────────────────────┘  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: Bind mount = host path → container (you control the path). Volume = Docker-managed path (Docker controls the path). Use bind mounts for dev (live code); use volumes for production data persistence. --mount syntax is preferred over -v for clarity.

Storage Q184 of 200
What is a tmpfs mount and when is it useful?
A) A permanent filesystem stored on the host's SSD for fast access
B) A temporary in-memory filesystem — data is stored in RAM, never touches disk, and is lost when the container stops. Ideal for secrets, caches, and temp files
C) A network filesystem that caches data on local tmp storage
D) A Docker volume that uses the host's /tmp directory
✅ Correct Answer: B

tmpfs mounts store data in RAM — never written to disk. This is ideal for: secrets (Swarm secrets use tmpfs), temporary caches, and any data that should never persist. Created with --tmpfs /path or --mount type=tmpfs,destination=/path. Data disappears when the container stops.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Storage Types — Volume vs Bind Mount vs tmpfs
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  TYPE        │ LOCATION          │ PERSISTS? │ MANAGED BY       │
  │  ────────────┼───────────────────┼───────────┼───────────────── │
  │  Named Volume│ /var/lib/docker/  │ YES       │ Docker           │
  │             │   volumes/         │           │                  │
  │  Bind Mount  │ Any host path     │ YES       │ User (host FS)   │
  │  tmpfs       │ RAM (memory)      │ NO        │ Kernel           │
  │  ────────────┴───────────────────┴───────────┴───────────────── │
  │                                                                 │
  │  tmpfs USE CASES:                                               │
  │  • Swarm secrets (/run/secrets/ is tmpfs)                       │
  │  • Temporary caches (no disk I/O, faster)                       │
  │  • Sensitive data that should NEVER hit disk                    │
  │                                                                 │
  │  $ docker run --tmpfs /tmp:rw,noexec,nosuid,size=64m nginx     │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: tmpfs = RAM-based storage, never on disk. Lost on container stop. Used for secrets (Swarm), caches, temp files. --tmpfs /path or --mount type=tmpfs. Options: size, mode, noexec, nosuid. Faster than disk but consumes RAM.

Storage Q185 of 200
What is the difference between -v and --mount for mounting volumes?
A) -v is for volumes; --mount is for bind mounts only
B) There is no difference; they are exact aliases
C) -v is the older, simpler syntax (one field); --mount is the newer, more explicit syntax (key=value pairs) — Docker recommends --mount for clarity
D) -v uses the local driver; --mount always uses NFS
✅ Correct Answer: C

-v combines three colon-separated fields (name:path:options). --mount uses explicit key=value pairs (type=volume,src=myvol,dst=/app,readonly). Docker recommends --mount because it's self-documenting, less error-prone, and supports all options clearly. Both achieve the same result.

📝 Detailed Explanation
-v vs --mount — Old vs New Syntax
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  -v (old, colon-separated):          --mount (new, key=value):   │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────┐   ┌──────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ $ docker run -v \            │   │ $ docker run --mount │   │
  │  │   pgdata:/data:ro \          │   │   type=volume,\      │   │
  │  │   nginx                      │   │   src=pgdata,\       │   │
  │  │                              │   │   dst=/data,\        │   │
  │  │ Ambiguous: Is "pgdata" a     │   │   readonly \         │   │
  │  │ volume name or host path?    │   │   nginx              │   │
  │  │ Docker tries to guess!       │   │                      │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────┘   │ ✅ Explicit: type=   │   │
  │                                      │   volume tells Docker│   │
  │  BIND MOUNT with -v:                │   it's a named volume│   │
  │  $ docker run -v \                  └──────────────────────┘   │
  │    /host/path:/app nginx                                        │
  │                                                                 │
  │  BIND MOUNT with --mount:                                       │
  │  $ docker run --mount \                                         │
  │    type=bind,src=/host/path,dst=/app nginx                      │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: -v = old syntax (colon-separated). --mount = new syntax (key=value, more explicit). Docker recommends --mount for new deployments. --mount supports ALL options (bind propagation, volume options) that -v doesn't. Know both for the exam.

Storage Q186 of 200
Which storage type should you use for a production PostgreSQL database?
A) tmpfs — databases need maximum speed and RAM is fastest
B) Bind mount — gives you direct access to the database files on the host
C) Anonymous volume — Docker will manage it automatically
D) Named volume — persistent, Docker-managed, survives container removal, easy to backup and migrate
✅ Correct Answer: D

Named volumes are the production standard for databases: Docker manages the storage location, the volume survives container removal, it can be backed up easily (docker run --rm -v pgdata:/data alpine tar czf /backup/pgdata.tar.gz /data), and it's portable across hosts with volume drivers. tmpfs would lose data on stop; bind mounts create host dependency; anonymous volumes are hard to track.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Storage Choice Decision — Production PostgreSQL
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  CHOOSING STORAGE FOR anihpj-db (PostgreSQL):                    │
  │                                                                 │
  │  ❌ tmpfs:     Data lost on stop. Not for databases!            │
  │  ❌ Anonymous: Random name, hard to find later. No.             │
  │  ❌ Bind mount: Host-dependent. Breaks if container moves.      │
  │  ✅ Named vol: Persistent, portable, backup-friendly. YES!      │
  │                                                                 │
  │  $ docker volume create pgdata                                  │
  │  $ docker run -d \                                              │
  │      --name anihpj-db \                                         │
  │      -v pgdata:/var/lib/postgresql/data \                       │
  │      -e POSTGRES_PASSWORD_FILE=/run/secrets/db-pass \           │
  │      postgres:15                                                │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: Production databases = named volumes. tmpfs = RAM (lost on stop). Bind mounts = host-dependent. Anonymous = untracked. Named volumes are the gold standard for stateful production workloads. Back up volumes regularly.

6.2 Volume ManagementQ187-Q192

Storage Q187 of 200
What does docker volume inspect show?
A) The contents of all files inside the volume
B) JSON metadata: mountpoint on the host, driver, labels, scope, and options — but NOT the files inside
C) A list of containers currently using the volume
D) The volume's creation date and last modified timestamp only
✅ Correct Answer: B

docker volume inspect shows the volume's metadata: mountpoint (where data lives on the host), driver (local, nfs, etc.), labels, scope (local/global), and options. It does NOT show the files inside — for that, you'd mount the volume to a container and explore.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Volume Management Commands
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ docker volume ls                 → List all volumes           │
  │  $ docker volume create myvol       → Create volume             │
  │  $ docker volume inspect myvol      → Metadata (mountpoint, etc)│
  │  $ docker volume rm myvol           → Delete volume             │
  │  $ docker volume prune              → Remove all unused volumes │
  │                                                                 │
  │  $ docker volume inspect pgdata                                  │
  │  [                                                               │
  │    {                                                             │
  │      "Name": "pgdata",                                           │
  │      "Driver": "local",                                          │
  │      "Mountpoint": "/var/lib/docker/volumes/pgdata/_data",       │
  │      "Scope": "local"                                            │
  │    }                                                             │
  │  ]                                                               │
  │                                                                 │
  │  To see files INSIDE: mount it to a container!                  │
  │  $ docker run --rm -v pgdata:/data alpine ls -la /data          │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: docker volume inspect = metadata only (mountpoint, driver). To see files, mount the volume to a container. docker volume prune removes ALL unused volumes — use with caution. Volumes are NOT removed with docker rm unless -v flag is used.

Storage Q188 of 200
What does docker volume prune do?
A) Compresses all volumes to save disk space
B) Removes only volumes with specific labels
C) Backs up all volumes before removing them
D) Removes ALL unused local volumes — volumes not referenced by any container. Use -f to skip confirmation
✅ Correct Answer: D

docker volume prune removes all volumes not currently used by at least one container. This is useful for cleaning up after development or removing orphaned volumes from removed containers. WARNING: This permanently deletes data — ensure you have backups before running in production.

📝 Detailed Explanation
docker volume prune — Safe Cleanup
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ docker volume ls                                             │
  │  DRIVER    VOLUME NAME                                          │
  │  local     pgdata         (IN USE by anihpj-db)                 │
  │  local     old-data       (no containers using)                 │
  │  local     test-vol       (no containers using)                 │
  │                                                                 │
  │  $ docker volume prune                                          │
  │  WARNING! This will remove all local volumes not used by...     │
  │  Are you sure? [y/N] y                                          │
  │  Deleted: old-data                                              │
  │  Deleted: test-vol                                              │
  │  → pgdata PRESERVED (still in use)                              │
  │                                                                 │
  │  ⚠️ SYSTEM-WIDE: docker system prune --volumes                  │
  │     Removes unused volumes + everything else                    │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: docker volume prune = remove ALL unused volumes. Always check docker volume ls first. Does NOT remove volumes currently in use. Use --filter to target specific volumes. Back up before pruning in production!

Storage Q189 of 200
What are Docker volume drivers?
A) Plugins that allow volumes to be stored on external storage systems — NFS, AWS EBS, Azure Disk, Ceph, etc. — instead of the local Docker host
B) Kernel modules that optimize local disk I/O for volumes
C) Docker Hub plugins for downloading pre-built volumes
D) Drivers for compressing volume data to save space
✅ Correct Answer: A

Volume drivers are plugins that extend Docker's volume system beyond the local filesystem. Examples: local (default, host filesystem), nfs (network file system), rexray (AWS EBS, Azure Disk), ceph (distributed storage). Use with docker volume create -d <driver> --opt ....

📝 Detailed Explanation
Volume Drivers — Beyond Local Storage
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  DRIVER      │ STORAGE BACKEND         │ USE CASE               │
  │  ────────────┼─────────────────────────┼─────────────────────── │
  │  local       │ Host filesystem          │ Default, single host   │
  │  nfs         │ NFS server               │ Shared across hosts    │
  │  rexray/ebs  │ AWS EBS volumes          │ Persistent cloud block │
  │  rexray/efs  │ AWS EFS                  │ Shared cloud NFS       │
  │  azurefile   │ Azure Files              │ Cloud file shares      │
  │  ceph        │ Ceph cluster             │ Distributed storage    │
  │  ────────────┴─────────────────────────┴─────────────────────── │
  │                                                                 │
  │  $ docker volume create -d rexray/ebs \                         │
  │      --opt size=100 \                                           │
  │      --opt volumetype=gp3 \                                     │
  │      pgdata-aws                                                 │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: Volume drivers = pluggable storage backends. Default: local (host filesystem). Cloud: rexray for AWS EBS/EFS, Azure Disk, GCE Persistent Disk. docker volume create -d <driver>. Drivers enable multi-host persistent storage in Swarm.

Storage Q190 of 200
What does --volumes-from do?
A) Creates a copy of another container's volumes
B) Migrates volumes from one Docker host to another
C) Mounts ALL volumes from another container into the new container — useful for sharing data, backup containers, or sidecars
D) Deletes volumes from a specified container
✅ Correct Answer: C

--volumes-from <container> mounts all volumes from an existing container into the new one — same mount points. This is useful for: backup containers (mount the DB container's volumes into a backup container), log shippers (access log volumes), and data migration between containers.

📝 Detailed Explanation
--volumes-from — Sharing Volumes Between Containers
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  DATA CONTAINER:                    BACKUP CONTAINER:            │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────┐   ┌──────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ $ docker run -d \            │   │ $ docker run --rm \   │   │
  │  │   --name anihpj-db \        │   │   --volumes-from \    │   │
  │  │   -v pgdata:/var/lib/postgre│   │   anihpj-db \         │   │
  │  │     sql/data \               │   │   -v /backup:/backup \│   │
  │  │   postgres:15                │   │   alpine \            │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────┘   │   tar czf \           │   │
  │                                      │   /backup/pgdata.tar \│   │
  │  Both containers see same           │   /var/lib/postgresql │   │
  │  pgdata at same mount point!        │   /data               │   │
  │                                      └──────────────────────┘   │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: --volumes-from = mount all volumes from another container. Primary use case: backup containers. Also useful for sidecars (logging, monitoring) that need access to the same data. Data container pattern is legacy — use named volumes instead.

Storage Q191 of 200
How do you back up a Docker volume?
A) docker volume backup myvol --output backup.tar
B) Run a temporary container that mounts the volume and a backup directory, then creates an archive: docker run --rm -v myvol:/data -v /backup:/backup alpine tar czf /backup/myvol.tar.gz /data
C) Copy /var/lib/docker/volumes/myvol/ directly — Docker handles consistency
D) Docker volumes are automatically backed up to Docker Hub
✅ Correct Answer: B

There's no built-in docker volume backup command. The standard pattern: run a temporary container that mounts both the source volume and a backup destination, then creates an archive. For databases, stop writes first or use database-native backup tools (pg_dump) for consistency.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Volume Backup and Restore Pattern
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  BACKUP:                                                        │
  │  $ docker run --rm \                                            │
  │      -v pgdata:/data \               ← source volume            │
  │      -v /backup:/backup \            ← host backup dir          │
  │      alpine \                                                  │
  │      tar czf /backup/pgdata-$(date +%Y%m%d).tar.gz /data       │
  │                                                                 │
  │  RESTORE:                                                       │
  │  $ docker run --rm \                                            │
  │      -v pgdata-restore:/data \        ← target volume           │
  │      -v /backup:/backup \            ← host backup dir          │
  │      alpine \                                                  │
  │      tar xzf /backup/pgdata-20260614.tar.gz -C /               │
  │                                                                 │
  │  FOR DATABASES: Stop writes first or use pg_dump for consistency│
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: No built-in volume backup command. Pattern: temp container + tar. For databases: pause/stop before backup for consistency, or use native tools (pg_dump, mysqldump). The DCA tests this backup pattern — know the exact docker run command.

Storage Q192 of 200
What is the correct volume configuration for anihpj-db (PostgreSQL) in a Swarm stack?
A) No volume needed — PostgreSQL stores data inside the container
B) Use a bind mount to /mnt/data on each Swarm node
C) Use an anonymous volume — Swarm will replicate data across nodes
D) Use a named volume with placement constraints to ensure the DB task always runs on the same node (where the volume exists)
✅ Correct Answer: D

In Swarm, the default local volume driver stores data on a single node. To ensure the database task always runs on that node, use placement constraints: constraints: [node.hostname == db-node]. For multi-node storage, use a volume driver like NFS or cloud block storage (rexray/ebs).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Swarm Database — Volume + Placement Constraint
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  docker-stack.yml:                                              │
  │  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ services:                                                 │ │
  │  │   db:                                                     │ │
  │  │     image: postgres:15                                    │ │
  │  │     volumes:                                              │ │
  │  │       - pgdata:/var/lib/postgresql/data                   │ │
  │  │     deploy:                                               │ │
  │  │       placement:                                          │ │
  │  │         constraints:                                      │ │
  │  │           - node.hostname == db-node  ← pin to one host  │ │
  │  │                                                           │ │
  │  │ volumes:                                                  │ │
  │  │   pgdata:                    ← local driver (single node)│ │
  │  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │                                                                 │
  │  ⚠️ local volume = tied to one node. Use placement constraint! │
  │  ☁️ For multi-node: rexray/ebs, nfs, or cloud-managed DB (RDS) │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: Swarm DB volumes need placement constraints (node.hostname == X) because local volumes are tied to a single node. For multi-node storage, use NFS or cloud volume drivers. Better yet: use managed cloud databases (RDS) for production.

6.3 Storage Drivers & CleanupQ193-Q200

Storage Q193 of 200
What does the overlay2 storage driver do?
A) It creates overlay networks for Swarm container communication
B) It implements a union filesystem — stacking read-only image layers with a writable container layer using the Linux kernel's OverlayFS
C) It compresses image layers to reduce disk usage
D) It encrypts image layers at rest on disk
✅ Correct Answer: B

overlay2 is the default Docker storage driver. It uses the Linux kernel's OverlayFS to create a union filesystem: multiple read-only "lower" directories (image layers) stacked with a writable "upper" directory (container layer) and a "merged" view that the container sees. This enables layer sharing and Copy-on-Write efficiency.

📝 Detailed Explanation
overlay2 — Union Filesystem Architecture
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  /var/lib/docker/overlay2/<container-id>/                       │
  │                                                                 │
  │  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │  MERGED (what container sees):                             │ │
  │  │  /var/lib/docker/overlay2/abc.../merged/                   │ │
  │  │  → Union of ALL lower layers + upper                      │ │
  │  ├───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤ │
  │  │  UPPERDIR (writable, container changes):                  │ │
  │  │  /var/lib/docker/overlay2/abc.../diff/                    │ │
  │  ├───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤ │
  │  │  LOWERDIR (read-only, image layers):                      │ │
  │  │  l1: FROM python:3.12-slim      (150MB, shared!)          │ │
  │  │  l2: COPY requirements.txt       (2MB, shared!)            │ │
  │  │  l3: RUN pip install ...         (45MB, shared!)           │ │
  │  │  l4: COPY . .                    (5MB, shared!)            │ │
  │  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │                                                                 │
  │  Layers are SHARED between containers — only the UPPER is unique│
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: overlay2 = default storage driver (since Docker 18.09). Uses Linux OverlayFS. Lower dirs = read-only image layers (shared). Upper dir = writable container layer (unique). Merged = what the container sees. Don't confuse with overlay network driver!

Storage Q194 of 200
How does the union filesystem enable efficient image storage?
A) Image layers are read-only and SHARED between containers — 10 containers from the same image share the same base layers, only the writable container layer is unique
B) It compresses all image layers into a single archive file
C) It stores each container as a complete copy of the entire image
D) It deduplicates files within a single image layer
✅ Correct Answer: A

The union filesystem is the key to Docker's storage efficiency. Image layers are read-only and shared across all containers using that image. If you run 10 nginx containers, the nginx image layers (~25MB) are stored ONCE, and each container only has its own small writable layer. Without this, 10 containers would consume 250MB just for the base image.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Layer Sharing — Space Efficiency
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  10 nginx CONTAINERS — Storage Usage:                            │
  │                                                                 │
  │  WITHOUT LAYER SHARING (hypothetical):                          │
  │  10 × 25MB (full image copy) = 250MB                            │
  │                                                                 │
  │  WITH overlay2 (actual):                                        │
  │  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │  SHARED (once on disk):                                   │ │
  │  │  Layer1: FROM alpine:3.19     (7MB)  ← shared by all 10  │ │
  │  │  Layer2: RUN apk add nginx   (15MB)  ← shared by all 10  │ │
  │  │  Layer3: COPY nginx.conf      (3MB)  ← shared by all 10  │ │
  │  │                                                           │ │
  │  │  UNIQUE (per container):                                  │ │
  │  │  Container1: upper/ (1MB)     Container6: upper/ (2MB)   │ │
  │  │  Container2: upper/ (1MB)     Container7: upper/ (1MB)   │ │
  │  │  ...                                                      │ │
  │  │  TOTAL: 25MB (shared) + ~15MB (10 uppers) = ~40MB!      │ │
  │  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │  → 250MB → 40MB (6x savings!)                                   │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: Union filesystem = layers shared across containers. Image layers are read-only and shared; only the container's writable layer is unique. This is why running 10 containers from the same image doesn't use 10× the disk space. overlay2 implements this.

Storage Q195 of 200
What is Copy-on-Write (CoW) in Docker storage?
A) A feature that creates backup copies of all files before modification
B) A compression algorithm for Docker images
C) When a container modifies a file from a read-only image layer, the file is COPIED to the writable layer FIRST, then modified — the original in the image layer stays untouched
D) A method for copying images between registries
✅ Correct Answer: C

Copy-on-Write is the mechanism that makes union filesystems efficient. When a container reads a file, it reads from the image layer (shared). When it writes, the file is first COPIED up to the writable container layer, then modified there. The original in the image layer remains unchanged and still shared with other containers.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Copy-on-Write — Efficiency in Action
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  CONTAINER READS /etc/nginx/nginx.conf:                         │
  │  ┌──────────────────────┐                                       │
  │  │ UPPER (container RW) │  → File NOT here                     │
  │  │ LOWER (image R/O)    │  → File FOUND → return to container │
  │  └──────────────────────┘  (no copy needed, direct read!)      │
  │                                                                 │
  │  CONTAINER WRITES to /etc/nginx/nginx.conf:                     │
  │  ┌──────────────────────┐                                       │
  │  │ UPPER (container RW) │  ← 1. COPY file from lower to upper  │
  │  │                      │  ← 2. MODIFY in upper                │
  │  │ LOWER (image R/O)    │  ← 3. Original UNCHANGED (still      │
  │  │                      │       shared with other containers)  │
  │  └──────────────────────┘                                       │
  │                                                                 │
  │  ✅ Only modified files consume writable layer space            │
  │  ✅ Unmodified files are read directly from shared layers       │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: CoW = files copied to writable layer only when modified. Reads from shared layer (fast, space-efficient). Writes trigger copy to upper layer first. This is why containers start fast — they don't copy the entire image, just reference it.

Storage Q196 of 200
What does docker system df -v show?
A) The host's total disk usage broken down by filesystem
B) A summary of Docker Compose file validation results
C) Docker Daemon log file sizes with verbose output
D) Detailed per-image, per-container, per-volume disk usage — including shared size, unique size, and which containers use which images
✅ Correct Answer: D

docker system df -v provides a verbose breakdown: each image with shared/unique size, each container with size and which image it uses, each volume with usage data, and build cache details. This is the go-to command for understanding where Docker disk space is going.

📝 Detailed Explanation
docker system df -v — Detailed Disk Audit
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ docker system df -v                                          │
  │                                                                 │
  │  Images space usage:                                            │
  │  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ REPOSITORY   TAG     IMAGE ID    SHARED   UNIQUE   SIZE   │ │
  │  │ anihpj-api   v2      abc123...   150MB    15MB     165MB  │ │
  │  │ python       3.12-sl def456...   150MB    0B       150MB  │ │
  │  │ (Shared = layers shared with other images)                 │ │
  │  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │                                                                 │
  │  Containers space usage:                                        │
  │  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ CONTAINER ID  NAME         IMAGE         SIZE (R/W)       │ │
  │  │ xyz789...     anihpj-api   anihpj-api:v2 2.5MB            │ │
  │  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: docker system df = summary. docker system df -v = verbose (per-image, per-container, per-volume). Shows SHARED vs UNIQUE size — critical for understanding why "total" may look bigger than actual disk usage (shared layers counted once).

Storage Q197 of 200
What does docker system prune -a remove?
A) ALL unused containers, networks, images (both dangling AND unreferenced), and build cache — effectively a full Docker cleanup
B) Only dangling images — leaves all tagged images intact
C) Only stopped containers — preserves images and volumes
D) Everything including running containers and in-use volumes
✅ Correct Answer: A

docker system prune -a is the comprehensive cleanup: stopped containers, unused networks, dangling images, AND all images not referenced by any container. Add --volumes to also remove unused volumes. This is safe to run — it won't touch running containers or in-use resources.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Docker Cleanup Commands — What Gets Removed
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  COMMAND                          │ WHAT IT REMOVES              │
  │  ─────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────── │
  │  docker system prune              │ Stopped containers, unused   │
  │                                   │ networks, dangling images,   │
  │                                   │ build cache                  │
  │  docker system prune -a           │ ALL above + ALL unused images│
  │  docker system prune --volumes    │ ALL above + unused volumes   │
  │  docker system prune -a --volumes │ EVERYTHING unused            │
  │  ─────────────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────── │
  │                                                                 │
  │  ⚠️ -a removes ALL images not used by any container!            │
  │  ⚠️ --volumes removes ALL unused volumes — DATA LOSS possible!  │
  │  ✅ Always run docker system df FIRST to see what will be removed│
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

💡 DCA Exam Tip: docker system prune = basic cleanup. prune -a = + all unused images. prune --volumes = + unused volumes. prune -a --volumes = full nuclear cleanup. Always check df first. Safe — running containers and in-use resources are never removed.

Storage Q198 of 200
Which storage driver should you use on a modern Linux system?
A) aufs — the original Docker storage driver, still the fastest
B) devicemapper — the enterprise-grade storage driver
C) overlay2 — the default and recommended driver for all modern Linux distributions (kernel 4.0+)
